The non-resonant bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator
Cristina Benea, Frédéric Bernicot, Victor Lie, Marco Vitturi

Abstract
. In this paper we introduce the class of bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operators { B ​ C a } a > 0 \{BC^{a}\}_{a>0} defined by B ​ C a ​ ( f , g ) ​ ( x ) := sup λ ∈ ℝ | ∫ f ⁡ ( x − t ) ​ g ​ ( x + t ) ​ e i ​ λ ​ t a ​ d ​ t t | BC^{a}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int f(x-t)\,g(x+t)\,e^{i\lambda t^{a}}\,\frac{dt}{t}\Big| and show that in the non-resonant case a ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) ∖ { 1 , 2 } a\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1,2\} the operator B ​ C a BC^{a} extends continuously from L p ​ ( ℝ ) × L q ​ ( ℝ ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{q}(\mathbb{R}) into L r ​ ( ℝ ) L^{r}(\mathbb{R}) whenever 1 p + 1 q = 1 r \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r} with 1 < p , q ≤ ∞ 1<p,\,q\leq\infty and 2 3 < r < ∞ \frac{2}{3}<r<\infty . A key novel feature of these operators is that – in the non-resonant case – B ​ C a BC^{a} has a hybrid nature enjoying both (I) zero curvature features inherited from the modulation invariance property of the classical bilinear Hilbert transform (BHT), and (II) non-zero curvature features arising from the Carleson-type operator with nonlinear phase λ ​ t a \lambda t^{a} . In order to simultaneously control these two competing facets of our operator we develop a two-resolution approach : • A low resolution, multi-scale analysis addressing (I) and relying on the time-frequency discretization of B ​ C a BC^{a} into suitable versions of “dilated” phase-space BHT-like portraits. The resulting decomposition will produce rank-one families of tri-tiles { ℙ m } m \{\mathbb{P}_{m}\}_{m} such that the components of any such tri-tile will no longer have area one Heisenberg localization. The control over these families will be obtained via a refinement of the time-frequency methods introduced in [ 63 ] and [ 64 ] . • A high resolution, single scale analysis addressing (II) and relying on a further discretization of each of the tri-tiles P ∈ ℙ m P\in\mathbb{P}_{m} into a four-parameter family of tri-tiles 𝕊 ⁡ ( P ) \mathbb{S}(P) with each of the resulting tri-tiles s ∈ 𝕊 ⁡ ( P ) s\in\mathbb{S}(P) now obeying the area one Heisenberg localization. The design of these latter families as well as the extraction of the cancellation encoded in the non-zero curvature of the multiplier’s phase within each given P P relies on the LGC-methodology introduced in [ 71 ] . A further interesting aspect of our work is that the high resolution analysis itself involves two types of decompositions capturing the local (single scale) behavior of our operator: • A continuous phase-linearized spatial model that serves as the vehicle for extracting the cancellation from the multiplier’s phase. The latter is achieved via T ​ T ∗ TT^{*} arguments, number-theoretic tools (Weyl sums) and phase level set analysis exploiting time-frequency correlations. • A discrete phase-linearized wave-packet model that takes the just-captured phase cancellation and feeds it into the low resolution analysis in order to achieve the global control over B ​ C a BC^{a} . As a consequence of the above, our proof offers a unifying perspective on the distinct methods for treating the zero/non-zero curvature paradigms.



1.
Introduction

The present problem belongs to an extensive research program
1
1

1

For an overview of some of the directions that are part of this extensive program please see the historical presentation in Section
1.3
.
that aims to advance our understanding of the subtle interplay between modulation invariance and curvature in harmonic analysis.

Concretely, in this paper we study the boundedness behavior of a (sub)bilinear operator
T:S(\mathbb{R})\times S(\mathbb{R})\to S^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
that has the following key properties
2
2

2

In what follows, we let
\tau_{b}f(x):=f(x-b)
the translation symmetry,
D_{\alpha}f(x):=f(\alpha x)
the dilation symmetry, and
M_{c}f(x):=e^{icx}f(x)
the modulation symmetry where
x,\alpha,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
.
:

(P1)

T
commutes with translations and dilations,
i.e.

T(\tau_{b}f,\tau_{b}g)=\tau_{b}T(f,g)\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>T(D_{\alpha}f,D_{\alpha}g)=D_{\alpha}T(f,g);

(P2)

T
is invariant under the action of modulation,
i.e.

|T(M_{c}f,M_{c}g)|=|T(f,g)|;

(P3)

T
is a maximal singular integral operator whose kernel representation involves a maximal (generalized) modulation in the presence of curvature.
3
3

3

The concept of curvature is of course multifaceted and covers many aspects within and beyond the area of harmonic analysis. For this reason, and also to allow for some flexibility in covering a diverse array of problems that will be discussed in the Introduction of our paper, we will not attempt to provide a precise definition of it. We will thus only observe that the study of an operator in a non-zero curvature regime will canonically rely on an application of the principle of (non)stationary phase, usually targeting the multiplier side and having as an effect the presence of a decaying-type factor quantifying the non-zero curvature. This latter property strongly correlates with the fact that the operator under discussion lacks a modulation invariance structure.

The above features, expectedly, will play a determinative rôle in our analysis and will be responsible for the methodology used in approaching our problem and its novelties.

Before entering into a more detailed discussion of the above considerations, we present the main result of our paper.

1.1.
Main result

Let
a>0
be a fixed parameter and
f,g\in S(\mathbb{R})
two smooth functions. Define the
bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator
with monomial phase of exponent
a
as
4
4

4

Throughout this paper, for notational simplicity we will drop the principal value symbol from singular integral formulas. Also, by convention, when
a>0
is not an integer, the expression
t^{a}
stands here for either
|t|^{a}
or for
\textrm{sgn}{t}|t|^{a}
. Finally, for simplicity we will appeal to a notational abuse and write
e^{iA}
as a shortcut for the expression
e^{2\pi iA}
, where here
A
is always assumed to be real.

(1)

BC^{a}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda t^{a}}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|.

Observation 1.1
.

When
a\notin\{1,2\}
,
BC^{a}
has no modulation symmetries beyond the expected ones in
(P2)
inherited from the bilinear Hilbert transform. In such a situation we say that
BC^{a}
belongs to the family of
non-resonant
bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operators. For more on this subject one is invited to consult Sections
1.2.1
and
1.4
.

With the above notations, we have

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
5
5

5

For a discussion on the necessity of imposing the restriction
a\notin\{1,2\}
please see the items
1)
and
2)
in the open question Section
1.4
below.

a\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1,2\}
and assume
p,q,r
are Hölder indices, i.e.
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}
, with
1<p,q\leq\infty
and
\frac{2}{3}<r<\infty
. Then the non-resonant bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator extends continuously from
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{q}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{r}(\mathbb{R})
with

(2)

\|BC^{a}(f,g)\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim_{a,p,q}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}.

Remark 1.3
.

The above theorem extends to more general types of phases that obey suitable conditions meant to preserve the hybrid character of the newly defined operator. A natural such extension that involves extra-technicalities but requires no fundamental modification relative to the proof strategy for Theorem
1.2
is the following
6
6

6

Due to space limitation concerns we make no attempt to provide a proof of this result in our present paper.
:

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
d\geq 3
and
P\in\mathbb{R}[t]
be a real polynomial of degree
d
with
P^{\prime}(0)=P^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
. Then the operator

(3)

BC^{P}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Big|\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda P(t)}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|

satisfies the bound

(4)

\|BC^{P}(f,g)\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim_{d,p,q}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}

for the same range of
p,q
and
r
as that for
BC^{a}
above.

Observation 1.4
.

As the name suggests,
BC^{a}
is a “concatenation” of two classical objects in harmonic analysis:

•

the bilinear Hilbert transform defined by

(5)

B(f,g)(x):=\int f(x-t)g(x+t)\frac{dt}{t};

•

the Carleson-type
7

7

Such operators are explicitly defined in
[

]
under the name
\gamma

with the intention of creating a continuum family of operators that covers both the classical Carleson operator and the purely curved (no linear term) polynomial Carleson operator considered by Stein
[

and Stein and Wainger
[

. In this more general setting
\gamma
stands for a suitably parametrized planar curve.
operator given by

(6)

C^{a}(f)(x):=\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int f(x-t)e^{i\lambda t^{a}}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|.

As a consequence of the above and in direct correspondence with the properties
(P1)
-

For an overview of the key ideas and novelties in our approach the interested reader is invited to consult Section

. In the reminder of this introductory section we will focus on the motivation/relevance for considering the class of bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operators
\{BC^{a}\}_{a>0}
as well as on a discussion of the historical background that reviews several related topics.

1.2.
Motivation

Since in this paper we introduce a new class of operators,
i.e.

\{BC^{a}\}_{a}
, it seems natural to dedicate an entire section to thoroughly presenting the multiple reasons for our interest in its study. Indeed, the introduction of the class of bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operators has a multifaceted motivation:

•

The first and most relevant for us is the novel complexity hierarchy that unfolds within the zero/non-zero curvature paradigm.

•

A second motivation is provided by the general, unifying character of this class of operators, which brings under the same umbrella (and extends) a few classical objects of interest in harmonic analysis.

•

Thirdly, our class naturally relates to some interesting current directions of research.

In what follows we discuss in more detail each of the above items.

1.2.1.
A novel manifestation within the zero/non-zero curvature paradigm

As mentioned at the beginning of our introduction, the formulation of our present problem was mainly motivated by the desire to shed more light into the ambitious program of developing a unified theory that simultaneously treats zero and non-zero curvature versions of several important themes within harmonic analysis.

Heuristically speaking, it is well known that the non-zero curvature problems are expected to have a lesser degree of complexity than their zero curvature counterparts, partly because of the (much) richer classes of symmetries obeyed by operators in the latter category. Thus, in the quest to obtain an integrated theory for treating these two regimes, it becomes natural to focus on the nature of changes in one’s approach as one transitions from non-zero to zero curvature problems. In particular, it is of high interest to design relevant and historically motivated hybrid models that contain both ends of the spectrum. In order to provide better context to our discussion, we continue with the following philosophical discussion:

Generic proof strategy outline for approaching the zero/non-zero curvature paradigm.

In the context of the literature to date – see also the situations discussed in Section
1.3
below – one deals with an operator, referred to generically as
T
, that lands in precisely one of the following two scenarios:

(I)

the
zero curvature
case
: This is the situation in which
T
has a (generalized) modulation invariance symmetry, thus requiring an approach that puts equal weight on all frequency locations (
i.e.
, the structure of the operator is invariant under frequency translations). This type of problem requires a time-frequency localization of the input functions on which
T
acts, thus naturally bringing into the picture wave-packet analysis and, in particular, a time-frequency tile discretization of the phase-space portrait of
T
. As expected, the fundamental structures in this context are represented by geometric configurations of tiles that share the same time-frequency location, called trees. The crux of the proof in these problems is to properly quantify 1) the almost-orthogonality relations among various trees, and 2) the “amount of information” relative to the input functions that each of these trees carries. The former aspect is achieved via a careful combinatorial argument, while the latter relies on a greedy algorithm involving concepts such as
mass
, in the spirit of Fefferman’s proof of the
L^{2}
-boundedness of the Carleson operator, and/or
size
, reminiscent of the Lacey-Thiele approach to the boundedness of the bilinear Hilbert transform.

(II)

the
non-zero curvature
case
: in this situation
T
has no modulation symmetry, and thus the zero frequency is expected to play a favoured rôle in its analysis. As a consequence, one applies the decomposition

(7)

T=:T_{0}+T_{\text{osc}},

such that

•

T_{0}
is a (maximal truncated) singular operator whose Fourier multiplier has essentially no phase oscillation and whose behavior is well understood.

•

T_{\text{osc}}
is a (maximal) oscillatory integral operator whose Fourier multiplier has a highly oscillatory phase. In this situation one performs a further discretization

(8)

T_{\text{osc}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}T_{m},

with each
T_{m}
representing the piece of
T
whose multiplier phase has the height
\approx 2^{\mu m}
for some properly chosen
\mu>0
depending on the specific nature of
T
. Via (non)stationary phase analysis,
TT^{*}
-method, and interpolation techniques, the goal is to show that

(9)

\|T_{m}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}\lesssim_{p}2^{-\delta(\mu,p)m}

for some
\delta(\mu,p)>0
and thus conclude that the
L^{p}
norm of
T_{\text{osc}}
is under control via a telescoping sum argument.

It is in this realm that the thought of developing a unified theory that covers the entire non-zero/zero curvature spectrum naturally comes to life. However, with the notable exception of the polynomial Carleson theme discussed below in Section
1.3.1
, there is to date no other fundamental class of operators for which such an integrated theory exists. Within this context, our paper is intended as a step forward in providing a better understanding of the subtle interplay between non-zero and zero curvature. Indeed, the present paper provides a first instance in which the analysis of the operator
T

simultaneously

•

has both zero and non-zero curvature features,

•

incorporates both approaches
(I)
and
(II)
above, and

•

requires modulation invariance techniques for both components in decomposition (
7
).

These features reflect the
hybrid
nature of our operator
T:=BC^{a}
which is designed as a mixture between the bilinear Hilbert transform and a Carleson-type operator:

(i)

regarded from the input side via
(P2)
,
BC^{a}
has zero-curvature features;

(ii)

regarded from the complex exponential kernel side, in the case
a\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1\}
, our operator
BC^{a}
has non-zero curvature features.

Observation 1.5
.

In this newly created hybrid category, it is important to say that any discussion of the complexity hierarchy centered around the concepts of zero/non-zero curvature must take into account the
joint
behavior of the symmetries determined by the interaction between the structure of the input function(s)
and
that of the operator’s kernel. Indeed, taking as an example the operator
BC^{a}
defined in (
1
), it is this interaction effect that truly determines the difficulty of the problem, since this joint interference may produce “
resonances
” not captured by either of the single items (i) and (ii) above if treated independently – see the discussion for the
a=2
case in the third item of Section
1.4
with special emphasis on (
60
). For another illuminating example one is invited to read the “open problems” part of Section
1.3.4
that discusses the behavior of suitable maximally modulated singular Radon transforms.

1.2.2.
Unifying and extending some classical themes

As noted above, our (non-resonant) bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator
BC^{a}

combines
and – as we will see immediately below –
controls
the behavior of the bilinear Hilbert transform
B
and that of the Carleson-type operator
C^{a}
. Indeed, assume in what follows that the conclusion of our Theorem
1.2
holds. Then we deduce the following:

•

Implications about the behavior of the bilinear maximal/maximally truncated/bilinear Hilbert transform
:
We first notice that after linearizing the supremum in (
1
), if we take
\lambda(x)=0
then the resulting object is precisely the classical bilinear Hilbert transform (BHT), and hence any bounds for
BC^{a}
trivially imply the same bounds for the BHT. Applying now the strategy described in (
7
)-(
9
) for
T=BC^{a}
and following the proof
8
8

8

Note that the proof of Theorem
3.2
involves only BHT-type behavior and does not rely on any
a priori
understanding of the bilinear maximal Hilbert transform; accordingly, deducing the local-
L^{2}
boundedness of the maximally truncated bilinear Hilbert transform in this way does not involve circular reasonings.
of our key underlying result stated as Theorem
3.2
we deduce that the highly oscillatory component
T_{\text{osc}}
is bounded within the local-
L^{2}
Hölder range. Once at this point, one observes that the low oscillatory component
T_{0}=BC^{a}-T_{\text{osc}}
is equivalent to a maximally truncated bilinear Hilbert transform (up to well behaved error terms) and thus the boundedness of the latter operator – at least in the local-
L^{2}
Hölder range – is implied by the corresponding bounds on
BC^{a}
.

•

Implications about the behavior of the Carleson-type operator
C^{a}
: For this theme one can simply take in (
2
) the endpoint bound
1<p=r<\infty
and
q=\infty
and set
g\equiv 1
to deduce immediately the corresponding
L^{p}
bounds for the Carleson-type operator
C^{a}
.
9
9

9

There is an alternate, indirect approach for showing that
L^{p}
bounds on
BC^{a}
imply corresponding
L^{p}
bounds for
C^{a}
for
1<p\leq 2
without relying on the endpoint bounds
(p,\infty,p)
in our Theorem
1.2
. Indeed, by first restricting the support of
f
to
[-1,1]
and then choosing
g=\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}
one can deduce from the open boundedness range for
BC^{a}
that
C^{a}_{\mathbb{T}}
– the restriction of
C^{a}
to the torus – maps
L^{q}(\mathbb{T})
into
L^{r}(\mathbb{T})
continuously for any
1<r<q<\infty
. Using Stein’s maximal principle
[
94
]
and real interpolation one concludes that
C^{a}_{\mathbb{T}}
is of strong type
(p,p)
for any
1<p<2
and of weak type
(2,2)
. The corresponding conclusions on the real line,
i.e.
the
L^{p}\to L^{p}
boundedness of
C^{a}
for
1<p<2
, can now be obtained via a transference principle.

1.2.3.
Connections with other research directions

I. Power decay property for multilinear oscillatory integrals and polynomially modulated bilinear Hilbert transforms.

We start our discussion here with the following:

Problem A.
[
21
]

Fix
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 2
,
\lambda
a real parameter and let
P:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
be a suitably smooth function. Define the multilinear functional

(10)

\Lambda_{\lambda}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}e^{i\lambda P(x)}\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(\pi_{j}(x))\eta(x)dx,

where here
\eta\in C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
\pi_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to V_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
are orthogonal projections with
V_{j}
linear subspaces of dimension
s<m
, and
f_{j}:V_{j}\to\mathbb{C}
are locally integrable functions (relative to the induced Lebesgue measure on
V_{j}
).

Characterize those data
(P,\{V_{j}\}_{j})
for which the following power decay property holds: there exist
\epsilon>0
and
C=C(\|\eta\|_{C^{1}})>0
such that

(11)

|\Lambda_{\lambda}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})|\leq C(1+|\lambda|)^{-\epsilon}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|f_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(V_{j})},

for all
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(V_{j})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

This theme of research may be seen as a natural development of some old and fundamental questions investigating the asymptotic behavior of expressions involving highly oscillatory phases. A prime and basic example of the latter is offered by the classical Van der Corput lemma, which states that

(12)

\int_{a}^{b}e^{i\lambda\varphi(t)}dt=O((1+|\lambda|)^{-\epsilon}),

where here
\lambda>0
,
a<b
,
\varphi
is a real smooth function with some derivative
\varphi^{(k)}
bounded away from zero
10
10

10

If
k=1
one also assumes
\varphi^{\prime}
monotone.
and
\epsilon=\epsilon(k)>0
. Another representative example,
11
11

11

This may serve as a linear prototype for the formulation of Problem A above.
proved in
[
87
]
, is the relation

(13)

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{i\lambda P(x,y)}f(x)g(y)\eta(x,y)dxdy\Big|\lesssim(1+|\lambda|)^{-\epsilon}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}},

whenever
P
is a real-valued polynomial that obeys suitable nondegeneracy conditions.
12
12

12

The presence of the
L^{2}
norm in the right-hand side of (
13
) as opposed to the
L^{\infty}
norm appearing in (
11
) is not of key relevance since, via standard interpolation techniques, one can always establish a correspondence between these two situations.

Returning to the initial Problem A, with the exception of the cases
n=2
or
s=1
and
m=n
which were extensively studied and are thus better understood, few results have been established for more general situations, with these almost exclusively limited to the case of
P
real analytic or merely polynomial – for more on this entire subject please see
[
21
]
and more recently
[
19
]
and the bibliography therein.

Focusing now on the case
P
polynomial – in analogy with the restrictions imposed for validating (
13
) -- it turns out that some suitable nondegeneracy condition is in fact necessary: Indeed, one is naturally
13
13

13

It is immediate that if
P
is degenerate in the sense of (
14
), then the terms in this decomposition can be distributed to the input functions, thus removing any possibility of decay in (
11
).
led to the requirement that
P
does not admit a decomposition of the form

(14)

P=\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\circ\pi_{j}\qquad\textrm{with}\qquad p_{j}:V_{j}\to{\mathbb{R}}\qquad\textrm{polynomial}.

Thus, a natural next step is to consider

Problem B.

Is the power decay property in (
11
) equivalent to nondegeneracy,
i.e.
, nonexistence of a decomposition of the form (
14
)?

Now as revealed in
[
21
]
, Problem B (and A in this generality) was motivated and inspired by the desire to answer the following:

Problem C. (Bilinear Hilbert transform with polynomial phase)

Let
P=P(x,t)
be a real polynomial of degree at most
d\in\mathbb{N}
and, for
f,g\in C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, define

(15)

B^{P}(f,g)(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{iP(x,t)}f(x-t)g(x+t)\frac{dt}{t}.

Then, show that for any
p,q,r
Hölder indices, i.e.
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}
, with
1<p,q\leq\infty
and
\frac{2}{3}<r<\infty
, there exists
C=C(d)>0
such that

(16)

\|B^{P}(f,g)\|_{L^{r}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}},

uniformly for all real valued polynomials of degree at most
d
.

A simple inspection of (
15
) shows that if
d\leq 2
then (
16
) can be reduced – via some simple generalized modulation transformations – to the classical known bounds for the bilinear Hilbert transform (
[
63
,
64
]
). Thus the only interesting case is
d\geq 3
, for which the proof of (
16
) can be reduced to the following key statement – which indeed clarifies the connections between Problems A, B and C: Let
P
be a real nondegenerate polynomial of degree
d
and
\eta\in C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
supported away from zero. Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for any
u\in\mathbb{N}

(17)

\Big\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{iP(2^{u}x,2^{u}t)}f(x-t)g(x+t)\eta(t)dt\Big\|_{L^{r}_{x}}\leq C2^{-\epsilon u}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}.

Now Problem C together with (
17
) and also some particular instances of Problem B were addressed in
[
21
]
using an approach relying on the concept of
\sigma-
uniformity introduced by Gowers in
[
41
]
.

In this context, the connection between the research theme of our present paper and Problem C together with its “relatives” Problems A and B above becomes transparent:

•

The bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator
BC^{a}
– together with its polynomial bilinear Hilbert–Carleson generalization

BC_{d}
in (
63
) – extends the behavior of
B^{P}
in (
16
) to the much wilder classes of phases
P(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}(x)t^{j}
with
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{a_{j}\}_{j}
arbitrary real measurable functions.

•

In the non-resonant case, Theorem
1.2
together with Remark
1.3
immediately imply the bounds in (
16
) for operators
B^{P}
in (
15
) having phase functions of the form
P(x,t):=Q(x)R(t)
with
R
any real polynomial obeying
R^{\prime}(0)=R^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
and
Q
any real
measurable
function.

•

Moreover, a fundamental component of the proof of Theorem
1.2
is the so-called
single-scale estimate
– stated as Proposition
5.1
– whose proof provides a novel (and seemingly first such) treatment of a particular instance of Problem A (and thus (
17
)) in the setting
n=3
,
m=2
and
s=1
without assuming any
x
-smoothness for the phase
P
(specifically, we treat phases
P
obeying the same properties as in the previous item). We note further that the proof of our Proposition
5.1
relies on the methodology developed in
[
71
]
paired with a number-theoretic approach and thus circumvents the
\sigma-
uniformity technique employed in
[
21
]
to approach Problems A, B and C.
14
14

14

It is worth noticing here that – under the assumption that
P
is non-degenerate (recall (
14
)) and that is properly normalized – the proof of Problem C reduces to a single scale estimate (in the sense described by Proposition
5.1
) and does not require any multi-scale/time-frequency analysis.

II. Convergence of (bilinear) Fourier Series

II.1. Historical context: The linear, one dimensional case.
As is well known, the celebrated problem of the pointwise convergence of Fourier Series is the central pillar of the structure on which the edifice of harmonic analysis area was built.
15
15

15

For more on this one is invited to consult Section
1.3.1
and also the significantly more detailed historical perspective offered in the Introduction of
[
77
]
.
Indeed, emerging from the foundational study of Fourier on heat propagation,
[
38
]
, the fundamental question was to convey a proper meaning to the equality sign in

(18)

f(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{f}(n)e^{2\pi inx},

where here
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
is a suitable
1
-periodic function and
\hat{f}(n)
is the Fourier coefficient of order
n
associated to
f
.

The initial inquiry into the subtle pointwise convergence properties of (
18
) motivated throughout the
19\textsuperscript{th}
century the rigorous reshaping of mathematical analysis through the contributions of personalities such as Dirichlet, Cauchy, Weierstrass, Riemann and many others. Along the way, the pointwise equality in (
18
) was positively settled for differentiable functions and refuted for the class of continuous functions. This latter surprising finding – manifested in the last decades of the
19\textsuperscript{th}
century – shifted the interest towards understanding the “pathologies” around the failure of the pointwise convergence behavior in (
18
). This was the context that generated two of deeply consequential mathematical developments: the set theory of Cantor and the modern theory of integration due to Lebesgue. With these, the theoretical framework around (
18
) was finally properly established.

Having reached at this point, we enumerate – moving fast-forward in time – the most relevant contributions to the topic discussed here:

•

The resolution of (
18
) in the
L^{p}
sense,
1<p<\infty
, was achieved via complex analysis by Riesz,
[
89
]
, thus establishing a deep connection with the theory of harmonic functions and their conjugates and consequently with what was soon to become the theory of Hardy spaces. Moreover, Riesz’s result, which can be stated equivalently as the
L^{p}
boundedness of the Hilbert transform, served as a main prototype for the Calderón-Zygmund theory of singular integral operators developed few decades later in
[
13
]
,
[
14
]
.

•

In the realm of (almost everywhere) pointwise convergence, the first major contribution belongs to Kolmogorov,
[
55
,
54
]
, who proved the almost-everywhere divergence of the Fourier Series within the class of
L^{1}
functions.

•

In the positive direction, Carleson,
[
17
]
, settled affirmatively Lusin’s conjecture on the almost everywhere convergence of Fourier series for square integrable functions. This was later extended to the
L^{p}
case,
1<p<\infty
, by Hunt,
[
52
]
. The proof of both results relied on providing
L^{p}
-weak bounds for the associated maximal operator

(19)

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|\sum_{|n|\leq N}\hat{f}(n)e^{2\pi inx}\Big|,

which is equivalent with the so-called (classical) Carleson operator, which in the real line framework may be expressed as

(20)

Cf(x):=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int_{\xi<N}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{ix\xi}d\xi\Big|.

We add that the approach developed by Carleson in
[
17
]
together with the different perspective offered by Fefferman in
[
37
]
constitutes the keystone of today’s time-frequency analysis.

We end the brief historical overview on (
18
) by mentioning another fascinating aspect, namely the question regarding the optimal spaces for the almost-everywhere convergence of the Fourier series near
L^{1}
. Far from being just an unanswered curiosity, this question is deeply connected with special Cantor-set-type pathologies and additive-combinatoric time-frequency structures – for more on these one is invited to consult
[
74
]
and
[
76
]
, respectively.

II.2. Historical context: The linear, two dimensional case.
Once the question of norm and pointwise convergence for (
18
) was settled in the
L^{p}
,
1<p<\infty
, context, the next natural step was the investigation of its higher dimensional analogue. More precisely, one would like to understand whether or not one can associate a meaning to

(21)

f(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\hat{f}(n)e^{2\pi i\langle n,x\rangle}=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{f}(n_{1},n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x_{1}+n_{2}x_{2})},

where here
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
is a suitable
1-
periodic function in both coordinates
x=(x_{1},x_{2})
,
\hat{f}(n)
is the Fourier coefficient of order
n
associated to
f
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
stands for the standard inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

As in the case of its one dimensional analogue, this question is deeply interconnected with several fundamental mathematical phenomena among which a central role is played by the increased complexity of the geometric structure of higher dimensional sets. This brings to light new “pathological” structures – specific to the higher dimensional setting – called Besicovitch sets that turn out to have a profound impact in the study of (
21
).
16
16

16

The concept of Besicovitch set plays a key role in harmonic analysis: a number of fundamental open questions are intimately related to it, among which we mention the Zygmund conjecture on differentiation along vector fields (see Section
1.3.3
), the optimal behavior of Bochner-Riesz means with the related topic of summability of the Fourier Series in two dimensions, and of course the well-known Kakeya and Restriction conjectures.
To better understand this, we discuss antithetically both the discrete setting of (
21
) and
its continuous Fourier integral analogue:

In the continuous case, we have:

•

Consider first the two-dimensional version of (
20
), which on the physical-space side corresponds to

(22)

Sf(x):=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(t)K(x-t)e^{i\langle N,t\rangle}dt\Big|,

where here
K
stands for a standard Calderón-Zygmund kernel. Then, as in the one-dimensional case,
S
is
L^{p}
-bounded for
1<p<\infty
– see
[
93
]
.

•

Next, consider the rough convolution operator defined on the Fourier side by

(23)

T_{B}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\hat{f}(\xi)\mathbf{1}_{B}(\xi)e^{i\langle x,\xi\rangle}d\xi,

where here
\mathbf{1}_{B}
stands for the characteristic function of the unit disc (ball) in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then the celebrated result of
Fefferman
[
34
]
states that

(24)

T_{B}:L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\>\>\textrm{boundedly}\>\>\Longleftrightarrow\>\>p=2.

The proof is based on constructing a certain counterexample based precisely on the existence of Besicovitch sets in any
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
. It follows that the maximal operator
Tf:=\sup_{n>0}|T_{nB}f|
is unbounded on any
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
except, possibly, for
p=2
.

In the discrete case,
17
17

17

In this context
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
is a
1
-periodic function in both variables.
we have the following correspondences:

•

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an open polygonal region in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing the origin. Then the relation

(25)

f(x_{1},x_{2})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in N\mathcal{P}}\hat{f}(n)e^{2\pi i\langle n,x\rangle}

holds both almost everywhere and in norm for any
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
1<p<\infty
. This result was proved in
[
35
]
and is based on the observation that (
25
) can be reduced to the one-dimensional Carleson-Hunt result.

•

In the opposite direction, if one considers the problem of summability along rectangles of arbitrary eccentricity, then there are continuous functions
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for which the relation

(26)

f(x_{1},x_{2})=\lim_{N_{1},N_{2}\to\infty}\sum_{|n_{1}|\leq N_{1},|n_{2}|\leq N_{2}}\hat{f}(n_{1},n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x_{1}+n_{2}x_{2})},

holds nowhere. The proof of this result is provided in
[
36
]
and is based on constructing the function
f
as a suitable superposition of smoothened variants of imaginary polynomial exponentials of the form
f_{\lambda}(x_{1},x_{2})=e^{i\lambda x_{1}x_{2}}
.

•

Finally, if one considers the problem of summability along discs, that is

(27)

f(x_{1},x_{2})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}\leq N^{2}}\hat{f}(n_{1},n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x_{1}+n_{2}x_{2})},

then due to (
24
), we know that (
27
) is ill posed both in norm and pointwise for
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and
p\not=2
, while the pointwise behavior for the
p=2
case is a well known open problem in harmonic analysis.

II.3. The bilinear, one dimensional case.
With the above historical context settled, and following the natural
path of relating linear multidimensional behavior to multilinear one-dimensional behavior,
18
18

18

For another instance of such a connection one is invited to read the saga of the Hilbert transform along curves (Section
1.3.3
) and that of the bilinear Hilbert transform along curves (Section
1.3.2
).
one is invited to consider the bilinear one dimensional analogue of (
21
), that is, the question of what meaning one can assign to the equality

(28)

f(x)g(x)=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{in_{1}x}e^{in_{2}x},

where now
f,g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
are both suitable
1
-periodic functions with
(f,g)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{q}(\mathbb{T})
for some
1<p,q\leq\infty
.

With the perspective gained from II.2, we now expect that the answer to (
28
) will fundamentally depend on the geometry of the sets along which we perform the summation. However, in contrast with II.2, the level of difficulty assigned to summability along the earlier considered shapes – polygons, rectangles and circles – is partly reversed. Indeed, in order to maintain a clear parallelism with II.2, we now discuss the analogues of (
25
)-(
27
) in a reversed order:

•

The problem of almost-everywhere pointwise summability along discs

(29)

f(x)g(x)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}\leq N^{2}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x+n_{2}x)}

seems to be open, and we are not aware of any nontrivial
19
19

19

Several cases are treated in
[
27
]
but they are more or less straightforward adaptations of the ideas in
[
34
]
to the bilinear setting.
positive or negative results regardless of the value of
p
. We leave this as an enticing open problem and mention that convergence in the
L^{r}
-norm sense for
f\in L^{p}
and
g\in L^{q}
has been proved in
[
42
,
43
]
for the local-
L^{2}
case (
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}
with
2\leq p,q,r^{\prime}<\infty
).

•

Moving to the problem of summability along rectangles of arbitrary eccentricity, one notices that

(30)

f(x)g(x)=\lim_{N_{1},N_{2}\to\infty}\sum_{|n_{1}|\leq N_{1},|n_{2}|\leq N_{2}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x+n_{2}x)}

holds both pointwise and in norm. Indeed, these statements are immediate consequences of the classical Carleson-Hunt result discussed at II.1.

•

Finally, we are turning our attention towards summability along polygonal regions
\mathcal{P}
, by studying the behavior of the expression
20
20

20

For the clarity of our exposition below – favoring the simplicity of the geometric structure of
\mathcal{P}
– we drop the specific value of the limit in (
31
). It is worth mentioning, though, that this value is precisely the expected pointwise product
f(x)g(x)
when we require that the origin belongs to the interior of
\mathcal{P}
. However, if the latter does not hold, then the value of the limit might be different.

(31)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in N\mathcal{P}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x+n_{2}x)}.

This is the setting that realizes the natural connection with the main topic of our present study; in our discussion we will consider two fundamental cases:

(1,1)
(0,1)
(0,0)
(1,0)
(0,-1)
\mathcal{P}_{2}
\mathcal{P}_{1}

Figure 1.
Frequency representation of triangles
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Summation over dilates of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
are known to correspond to bounded operators, but nothing is known about summation over dilates of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

(a)

The triangle
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\big\{(\xi,\eta)\,:\,0\leq\xi\leq\eta\leq 1\big\}.

We start by noticing that at the formal level

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in N\mathcal{P}_{1}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x+n_{2}x)}=\sum_{0\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x+n_{2}x)}.

Now, as in the previous cases II.1 and II.2, one is invited to study the associated (discrete) maximal operator, which, thanks to the transference principle in
[
42
]
, may be reduced to the study of its continuous version defined by

(32)

P_{1}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{N>0}\Big|\iint_{0<\xi<\eta<N}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|.

As it turns out, the behavior of
P_{1}
is equivalent with that of the Bi-Carleson operator

(33)

Bi\text{-}C(f,g)(x):=\sup_{N}\Big|\iint_{\xi<\eta<N}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|.

Indeed, this follows from the simple observation that

\displaystyle P_{1}(f,g)

\displaystyle=Bi\text{-}C(\pi_{+}f,\pi_{+}g),

where here
\pi_{+}
stands for the projection on the positive frequencies.

We thus conclude that the boundedness of
P_{1}
(and hence the almost everywhere convergence of the expression in (
31
)) follows from the work in
[
83
]
asserting that
Bi\text{-}C:L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{q}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{r}(\mathbb{R})
continuously, whenever
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}<\frac{3}{2}
and
p,q>1
.

(b)

The triangle
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{2}:=\big\{(\xi,\eta)\,:\,0\leq\xi\leq\eta+1\leq 1\big\}.

Following similar steps to those in (a), we have that the almost everywhere convergence in (
31
) for
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
may be formally phrased as

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{n\in N\mathcal{P}_{2}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x+n_{2}x)}=\sum_{n_{2}\leq 0\leq n_{1}}\hat{f}(n_{1})\hat{g}(n_{2})e^{2\pi i(n_{1}x+n_{2}x)}

and hence we are naturally led to the study of the boundedness properties of

P_{2}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{N>0}\Big|\iint_{N\mathcal{P}_{2}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|=\sup_{N>0}\Big|\iint_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\leq 0\leq\xi\\
\xi-\eta\leq N\end{subarray}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|.

Observe from the above that
21
21

21

As expected, here
\pi_{-}:=Id-\pi_{+}
.

P_{2}(f,g)(x)=\widetilde{BC}^{1}(\pi_{+}f,\pi_{-}g)(x),

where here

(34)

\widetilde{BC}^{1}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{N>0}\Big|\iint_{\xi-\eta\leq N}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|.

It remains now to notice that
\widetilde{BC}^{1}(f,g)
is equivalent
22
22

22

Indeed,
\widetilde{BC}^{1}(f,g)
may be expressed as a linear combination between
BC^{1}(f,g)
and the pointwise product
f\cdot g
.
– up to smooth error terms – to
BC^{1}
.

We thus deduce that the problem of the almost everywhere convergence in (
31
) for
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
is essentially equivalent with the study of the boundedness properties for the resonant bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator
BC^{1}
defined in (
1
).

Moreover, contrasting the cases
(a)
and
(b)
discussed above, it is worth noticing how a simple translation from
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
to
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
makes for a startling difference in the nature and complexity of the problem on the almost everywhere convergence of (
31
). Indeed, given that the symmetry relations satisfied by
BC^{1}
are closer in spirit to those satisfied by the trilinear Hilbert transform – see (
61
) and (
62
) in Section
1.4
below, we expect the final resolution of
BC^{1}
to be significantly more difficult than that already provided for
Bi\text{-}C
(or for other currently understood time-frequency operators).

As a consequence of this last remark, except for a very few and specific situations
23
23

23

As in the case of
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
that can be dealt with Bi-Carleson and linear Carleson type operators.
the study of (
31
) for generic polygons
\mathcal{P}
requires at the very minimum the understanding of
BC^{1}
-type operators. We conclude, thus, that the generic formulation of the almost everywhere convergence of bilinear Fourier series presents itself as a deep, intriguing open problem that further motivates our introduction of the class of bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operators
\{BC^{a}\}_{a>0}
.

II.4. Maximally modulated operators via symbol translation.
As is transparent from the preceding discussion, see
e.g.
(
20
), (
33
) and (
34
), Carleson-type behavior may be rephrased on the Fourier side in terms of maximally shifted multipliers. In view of this, one can naturally consider – in the bilinear setting – the generic problem of understanding the boundedness properties of the operator

(35)

\mathcal{C}_{m}(f,g)(x)=\sup_{N=(N_{1},N_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}m(\xi-N_{1},\eta-N_{2})d\xi d\eta\Big|,

where here
m
is some suitable singular symbol.

Notice now that indeed, as mentioned in the introduction, taking the supremum over all translated copies of the symbol
m
by vectors
N\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
makes the operator
\mathcal{C}_{m}
invariant under the action of any modulation of the input functions, that is

(36)

\mathcal{C}_{m}(M_{b}f,M_{c}g)(x)=\mathcal{C}_{m}(f,g)(x),\qquad\text{for any }b,c\in\mathbb{R}.

In the case where
m(\xi,\eta)
is a Mikhlin multiplier, and thus in particular smooth away from the origin, the corresponding
\mathcal{C}_{m}
operator was studied in
[
68
]
. Due to (
36
), there is no favored frequency location for the usual Whitney-type analysis, and thus a significant effort in
[
68
]
is put into producing a “translation-invariant” Littlewood-Paley decomposition of the symbol
m
.

Another example that fits the general description (
35
) is provided by the Bi-Carleson operator
Bi\text{-}C
defined earlier in (
33
) and studied in
[
83
]
. Notice that in this situation the original BHT-type symbol
\displaystyle m(\xi,\eta)=\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta<\xi<0\}}
is translated along the line
\{\xi=\eta\}
. Thus, the resulting object may be thought of as a composite operator that integrates the behaviors of both the BHT and the Carleson operator. After a careful analysis of the symbol of
Bi\text{-}C
that relies on a special case of the result in
[
68
]
(that of Mikhlin multipliers translated along the line
\{\xi=\eta\}
), the maximal modulation is seen to act only on the
\eta
variable side – and hence involving only the function
g
– thus allowing a decoupling of the two behaviors corresponding to BHT and the classical Carleson operator.

Coming now to the main topic of our present paper, we note that the operator
BC^{1}
in (
1
) (or equivalently (
34
)), which represents the “purely zero-curvature” form of our bilinear Hilbert–Carleson class
\{BC^{a}\}_{a>0}
, also fits into the description provided by (
35
). Indeed,
BC^{1}
can be realized either by translating the two-variable symbol
\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta<\xi\}}
by any vector
(N_{1},N_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, or by translating the one-variable (here
\xi-\eta
) symbol
\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta-\xi<0\}}
by any real number
N\in\mathbb{R}
.

Finally, at the heuristic level, we remark that
BC^{1}
may be seen – loosely speaking – as the “orthogonal frequency shift counterpart” to
Bi\text{-}C
, since the symbol of the former is invariant under shifts in the direction orthogonal to the line
\{\xi=\eta\}
while the symbol of the latter is invariant under shifts along the line
\{\xi=\eta\}
. However, as simple as this geometric parallel heuristic is, these two operators turn out to belong to different classes of difficulty.

III. Variational estimates and the bilinear Rubio de Francia operator

We start this section by quickly revisiting the main theme of Section II.3 centered around the pointwise convergence of (multiple) Fourier Series. We have learned by now that the standard classical approach to this theme proceeds by providing bounds for suitable associated maximal operators.

Indeed, as an example for our discussion, one can see that the one dimensional case exposed in (
18
) was decided via the boundedness properties of the Carleson operator as revealed by (
19
) and (
20
).

However, there exists another approach, pioneered by Bourgain in the context of ergodic averages
[
10
]
, which relies on the study of the properties of so-called variational means. Although this route presents more technical challenges – since such an
s
-variational operator controls the behavior of the corresponding maximal operator -- it has the advantage that it does not appeal to density results
24
24

24

This is especially useful in the ergodic theory framework where in most of the interesting situations one does not have available a natural dense subclass of functions within which almost everywhere convergence results can be directly established.
and also that it supplies information about the rate of convergence of the quantities under study. Thus, in relation to (
20
), one can define for some
s>2
the so-called variational Carleson operator
\mathcal{C}^{s-var}
given by

(37)

\mathcal{C}^{s-var}f(x)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}N\geq 1\\
a_{1}<\ldots<a_{N}\end{subarray}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{ix\xi}\mathbf{1}_{\{a_{k}<\xi<a_{k+1}\}}d\xi\Big|^{s}\Big)^{\frac{1}{s}}.

In
[
86
]
it was shown that
\mathcal{C}^{s-var}
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
for any
s^{\prime}<p<\infty
, thus, in particular, providing an alternative means of establishing the pointwise convergence of (one-dimensional) Fourier Series for functions in
L^{p}
.

Moving now towards the pointwise convergence of the bilinear Fourier series in (
28
), we focus our attention on the two central cases of convergence along polygonal regions
\mathcal{P}
discussed in II.3, that is the convergence along the polygonal regions
\mathcal{P}
. Maintaining the parallelism with that earlier discussion, we have the following:

•

If
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
, then (
31
) may be approached by studying the variational analogue of (
33
) defined for
s>1
by

(38)

T_{s}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}N\geq 1\\
a_{1}<\cdots<a_{N}\end{subarray}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\Big|\iint_{a_{k}<\xi<\eta<a_{k+1}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|^{s}\Big)^{1/s}.

This operator was studied in
[
29
]
, although the optimality of the range of
L^{p}
spaces for which convergence holds remains open. In a related context, we also mention
[
28
]
, which studied variational estimates for paraproducts.

•

If
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
, then (
31
) may be approached by studying the variational analogue of (
34
), which for
s>1
is defined by

(39)

\displaystyle BC^{1,s-var}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}N\geq 1\\
a_{1}<\cdots<a_{N}\end{subarray}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\Big|\iint_{a_{k}<\xi-\eta<a_{k+1}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|^{s}\Big)^{1/s}.

Of course the problem of providing bounds for (
39
), which is more difficult than the corresponding problem for
BC^{1}
, is wide open.

At this point, it is worth noticing that if we drop the supremum over the parameters
N,a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}
in (
39
) above and we consider the sequence
\{a_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq N}
to be fixed, we recover a bilinear version of the Rubio de Francia operator associated to arbitrary strips:

(40)

BRF^{s}(f,g)(x)=\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\Big|\iint_{a_{k}<\xi-\eta<a_{k+1}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}d\xi d\eta\Big|^{s}\Big)^{1/s}.

The boundedness of
BRF^{s}
remains a largely open problem, except for the situation of equidistant points
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}
. This latter situation was covered in
[
6
]
in the local-
L^{2}
range for
s=2
.
25
25

25

Hence the boundedness in the local-
L^{2}
range remains valid for any
s\geq 2
.
The proof of
[
6
]
relies heavily on the regular distribution of the strips and combines the BHT time-frequency analysis with concepts such as vectorial trees, vectorial sizes, and energies. In hindsight, the result of
[
6
]
can also be obtained using more recent vector-valued extensions for BHT
[
3
]
; namely,
B:L^{p}(\ell^{2}(\ell^{\infty}))\times L^{q}(\ell^{\infty}(\ell^{2}))\to L^{r}(\ell^{2}(\ell^{2}))
estimates are combined with the classical result of Rubio de Francia
[
91
]
to obtain boundedness in the local-
L^{2}
range. The case of equidistant points
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}
makes the
\ell^{\infty}
spaces redundant, and also indicates the limitation of the method in treating the general case.

1.3.
Historical context

In what follows, we will approach the discussion on the historical background from the perspective provided by the zero/non-zero curvature paradigm, which represents the keystone for our entire analysis in this paper.

Indeed, we will review the history of the mathematical literature most closely related to our paper through the lenses offered by the generic strategy discussed at the beginning of Section
1.2.1
. Our story will focus, in chronological order, on the two special classes of operators that contribute to the design of our operator, namely the (polynomial) Carleson operator and the bilinear Hilbert transform. Aside from these two, we will briefly discuss a few other related themes.

1.3.1.
The Carleson operator

In this setting of maximally modulated singular integral operators, the generic formulation of the main question may be phrased as follows:

The Carleson operator along variable curves.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an (infinite) family of plane curves
\Gamma:=(t,-\gamma(t))
with
\gamma(t)
a suitable real piecewise smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
. Study the boundedness properties of the
\mathcal{F}
-Carleson operator, defined by

(41)

C_{\mathcal{F}}(f)(x):=\sup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}}\left|C_{\Gamma}f(x)\right|:=\sup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t)e^{i\gamma(t)}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|.

The (purely) zero-curvature case
:
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{1}[1]:=\{(t,at)|a\in\mathbb{R}\}
.

This subject is a central part of the two-century-old saga – mentioned in Section
1.2.3
, II.1 – investigating the behavior of Fourier Series and originating in the work of Fourier on heat propagation (
[
38
]
). In 1910’s, Luzin (
[
78
]
) conjectured that the Fourier series of a square integrable function converges almost everywhere. Kolmogorov (
[
55
]
,
[
54
]
) showed that the above fails in the
L^{1}
setting. After decades of misapprehension following Kolmogorov’s result, Carleson (
[
17
]
) delivered the positive answer to Luzin’s conjecture by providing
L^{2}
-weak bounds for the so-called (classical) Carleson operator
C:=C_{\mathcal{F}}
with
\mathcal{F}:=\{(t,at)|a\in\mathbb{R}\}
in (
41
).
26
26

26

Notice that the operator
C
is the same as
C^{a}
,
a=1
, defined earlier in (
6
).

The key underlying difficulty – the first of its kind in the literature – resides in the special modulation symmetry relation

(42)

CM_{a}f=Cf\quad\quad\forall\>a\in\mathbb{R}\>,

which was overcome through a wave-packet discretization of the operator. In this way the boundedness of
C
was reduced to understanding simultaneously the space and frequency interactions between the (properly grouped) wave-packets.

Thus Carleson’s approach, together with Fefferman’s alternative proof in
[
37
]
, set the foundation for what today we call time-frequency analysis. A third proof combining elements of the first two approaches was provided by Lacey and Thiele in
[
65
]
.

We end this brief description by mentioning two of the earlier extensions of Carleson’s theorem: the
L^{p}
,
1<p<\infty
, analogue of Hunt
[
52
]
, and the higher dimensional version proved by Sjölin in
[
93
]
.

The non-zero-curvature case
:
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]:=\{(t,\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}t^{\alpha_{j}})|\{a_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}\}
where here
\vec{\alpha}:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in\prod_{1}^{d}((0,\infty)\setminus\{1\})
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
fixed.

This subject originated from two distinct directions: 1) the study of the Hilbert transform along curves – see a brief description of this in Section
1.3.3
below, and 2) the study of singular integrals on the Heisenberg group in connection with the behavior of subelliptic partial differential operators.

As a point of convergence for these two directions of inquiry, Stein introduced and initiated the study of the so-called polynomial Carleson operator, defined via (
41
) by the relation

(43)

C_{d}:=C_{\mathcal{F}_{d}},

where here, for fixed
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{F}_{d}:=\{(t,\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}t^{j})|\{a_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}\}
.

The first step toward a better understanding of
C_{d}
was made, naturally, by analyzing its curved analogue
C^{curv}_{d}:=C_{\mathcal{F}_{d-1}[(2,\ldots,d)]}
where here
d\geq 2
. As we have already learned by now, a key simplification afforded by considering
C^{curv}_{d}
is the non-zero curvature of the phase, which manifests in the fact that, unlike
C_{d}
, the curved model
C^{curv}_{d}
has no (generalized) modulation invariance symmetry.

The
L^{p}
-boundedness of
C^{curv}_{d}
,
1<p<\infty
, was first shown for
d=2
by Stein in
[
96
]
and then for general
d\geq 2
(and also extended to arbitrary dimension
27
27

27

In arbitrary dimensions the (polynomial) Carleson operator is the analogue of (
41
) obtained by replacing the kernel
\frac{1}{t}
with a suitable Calderón-Zygmund kernel and
\gamma(t)
by a suitable multi-variable real polynomial with the obvious modification for the class analogous to
\mathcal{F}_{d}
.
) by Stein and Wainger in
[
99
]
. Both approaches follow the philosophy exposed at
(II)
above and rely on Van der Corput estimates and the
TT^{*}
-method.

In
[
71
]
, the third author extended the one-dimensional
28
28

28

Though involving non-trivial technicalities, it seems likely that the methods developed in
[
71
]
can be adapted to the higher dimensional case.
version of
[
99
]
by providing
L^{p}
bounds,
1<p<\infty
, for a large class of curved
\mathcal{F}
-Carleson operators that in particular includes
C_{\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]}
for any
\vec{\alpha}:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in((0,\infty)\setminus\{1\})^{d}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. The methodology developed in
[
71
]
provides a new approach to Stein-Wainger’s polynomial Carleson operator
C^{curv}_{d}
and has the advantage – confirmed also via the present paper – of being compatible with time-frequency analysis techniques.

Unifying the zero/non-zero-curvature cases
:
\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]
with
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in\prod_{1}^{d}((0,\infty))
,
1\in\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
d\geq 2
fixed.

Returning to the original inquiry of Stein regarding the boundedness of the polynomial Carleson operator defined in (
43
), the first result unifying the zero-curvature case of the classical Carleson operator
C
with the non-zero curvature case of
C^{curv}_{2}
treated in
[
96
]
by Stein was proved in
[
72
]
, which established the
L^{2}
-weak boundedness of
C_{2}
. The full one-dimensional version of the conjecture of Stein asserting the boundedness of the polynomial Carleson operator for any
L^{p}
,
1<p<\infty
, was proved affirmatively in
[
77
]
, thus unifying the results in

[
17
]
and the one-dimensional version of
[
99
]
. In analogy with the purely zero-curvature case discussed earlier, the main difficulty is represented by the higher-order
29
29

29

Here
M_{j,a}f(x):=e^{iax^{j}}f(x)
represents a generalized modulation of order
j\in\mathbb{N}
and parameter
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
modulation invariance relation

(44)

C_{d}M_{j,a}f=C_{d}f\quad\quad\forall\>a\in\mathbb{R}\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\forall\>1\leq j\leq d.

Accordingly, the proof of the
L^{p}
-boundedness of
C_{d}
is based on some key new ideas: 1) developing a higher-order wave-packet analysis consistent with (
44
) that is compatible with the time-frequency approach to the classical Carleson operator
C
, and 2) introducing a local analysis adapted to the concepts of mass and counting function.
30
30

30

This latter aspect provides a novel tile discretization of the time-frequency plane with the consequence of eliminating certain exceptional sets from the analysis of
C_{d}
. (These exceptional sets were present in all of the previously known approaches to the boundedness of the classical Carleson operator
C=C_{1}
– see
[
17
]
,
[
37
]
and
[
65
]
). As a consequence, the approach in
[
77
]
provides the full
L^{p}
boundedness range,
1<p<\infty
, with no appeal to interpolation techniques.

The
n
-dimensional version of Stein’s conjecture on the polynomial Carleson operator was proved more recently – see
[
100
]
and
[
70
]
– by essentially combining the methodology in
[
77
]
with the
n
-dimensional Van der Corput estimates proved in
[
99
]
.

Observation 1.6
.

For future reference, it might be worth noticing that the preceding unified problem can be equivalently rephrased – via a standard linearization procedure – in the following terms:

Study the boundedness of the
\gamma
-Carleson operator defined by

(45)

C_{\gamma}(f)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t)e^{i\gamma(x,t)}\frac{dt}{t},

where here
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is (fiberwise) measurable and
\gamma(x,\cdot)
is a piecewise smooth function a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

1.3.2.
The bilinear Hilbert transform

The generic formulation
31
31

31

Our choice for this formulation (as well as for that in the previous section) is tailored to make more transparent the zero/non-zero curvature paradigm that is at the heart of both the introduction and the treatment of the family of bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operators. Of course, the zero and non-zero curvature sides of the problem can and have been treated independently, with the former being the catalyst for the modern approach to time-frequency analysis.
of the main question within this direction can be stated as follows:

The bilinear Hilbert transform along curves.

Given
\Gamma:=(t,-\gamma(t))
a plane curve with
\gamma
a suitable piecewise smooth real function, study the boundedness properties of the bilinear Hilbert transform along the curve
\Gamma
, defined as

(46)

B_{\Gamma}(f,g)(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t)g(x+\gamma(t))\frac{dt}{t}.

The zero-curvature case
:
\gamma(t)=at
with
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{-1,0\}
.

While also presenting connections with ergodic theory via the study of the
L^{p}
-norm convergence of non-conventional bilinear averages, this theme arose in the realm of harmonic analysis through Calderón’s program,
[
12
]
, dedicated to the study of the Cauchy transform along Lipschitz curves (see also
[
23
]
). His initial plan was to reduce this study – via a Taylor series argument – to the problem of providing uniform (in
a
)
L^{p}
-bounds for the bilinear Hilbert transform
B_{a}\equiv B_{\Gamma_{a}}
with
\gamma(t)=at
and
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{-1,0\}
. While first Calderón,
[
12
]
, (for small Lipschitz constants) and later Coifman, McIntosh and Meyer,
[
23
]
, were eventually successful – via a different route – in providing the
L^{2}
bounds for the Cauchy transform along Lipschitz curves, the original problem about the behavior of the bilinear Hilbert transform established itself as a deep conjecture within classical harmonic analysis.

Two decades later, in
[
59
]
, a key perspective was added based on the observation that, beyond the standard dilation and translation symmetries, the bilinear Hilbert transform
B_{a}
also obeys a key modulation symmetry given by

(47)

B_{a}(M_{a}f,Mg)=M_{1+a}B_{a}(f,g).

This suggested that in order to approach this problem one must appeal to a wave-packet analysis in the spirit of that developed by Carleson
[
17
]
and later Fefferman
[
37
]
for proving the almost everywhere convergence of Fourier Series for square-integrable functions. Indeed, a few years later, Lacey and Thiele
[
63
,
64
]
made the main breakthrough in the affirmative resolution of Calderón’s conjecture by proving that
B_{a}
maps
L^{p}\times L^{q}
into
L^{r}
continuously for
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}
,
1<p,q\leq\infty
and
\frac{2}{3}<r<\infty
.
32
32

32

There is still an open problem regarding the maximal range for
p,q,r
– specifically the interval
\frac{1}{2}<r\leq\frac{2}{3}
– that guarantees the boundedness of
B_{a}
.
The Hardy-Littlewood maximal analogue of (
46
) in the same zero-curvature setting was proved by Lacey in
[
60
]
. Interestingly enough, even though a positive operator, the strategy employed for analyzing this bilinear maximal operator was still inspired by the wave-packet analysis performed on the bilinear Hilbert transform, though the presence of the maximal truncation makes the orthogonality argument significantly more involved.
33
33

33

In this context it is worth recalling the discussion in Section
1.2.2
that reveals the key rôle played by the bilinear maximal operator in bounding the low oscillatory component
T_{0}
of our bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator
BC^{a}
.

The non-zero-curvature case
:
\gamma(t)=\sum_{j=2}^{d}a_{j}t^{j}
, with
d>1
.

This problem can be motivated from three different directions: a) from the ergodic theory side in connection with the
L^{p}
-norm convergence of non-conventional bilinear averages of the type
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{1}(T^{n})f_{2}(T^{n^{2}})

for
T
an invertible measure-preserving transformation of a finite measure space (see
e.g.

[
39
]
,
[
51
]
); b)
from the number theory side via the so-called Ergodic Roth type theorem(s) (see
e.g.

[
8
]
,
[
9
]
,
[
30
]
,
[
58
]
); and c) from the harmonic analysis side as a natural curved analogue of the standard bilinear Hilbert transform considered above.

Within this latter realm, a first result was obtained in
[
67
]
, in which Li proved that
B_{\Gamma}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
continuously for the case
\Gamma(t)=(t,t^{d})
,
2\leq d\in\mathbb{N}
. The proof of this relies on the concept of
\sigma
-uniformity introduced in
[
21
]
and inspired by Gowers’s work in
[
41
]
and only partially reflects the generic strategy described earlier here at point
(II)
, lacking the natural scale-type decay in (
9
).

The general curved case – this time faithfully reflecting
(II)
by also including (
9
) – was completed with different methods by the third author here in
[
73
]
,
[
75
]
. Indeed, the main combined result states that
B_{\Gamma}
satisfies
L^{p}\times L^{q}\to L^{r}
bounds for
1/p+1/q=1/r
with
1<p<\infty
,
1<q\leq\infty
and
1\leq r<\infty
for any
\Gamma=(t,\gamma(t))
with
\gamma\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{F}
. Here
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{F}
is a suitable class of curves that includes in particular any Laurent polynomial with no linear term, and within this class the above boundedness range is sharp up to the end-points. The proof of this result encompasses Gabor frame analysis, quadratic phase discretization, orthogonality/
TT^{*}
methods and shifted square function arguments and serves as a precursor for the methodology developed in
[
71
]
.

Further work regarding the maximal curved analogue was performed in
[
69
]
and
[
40
]
.

Observation 1.7
.

One can create a parallelism between the topic treated in the previous section in the form
given by (
45
) and the current topic by considering the study of the more general expression

(48)

B_{\gamma}(f,g)(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t)g(x+\gamma(x,t))\frac{dt}{t},

where here
\gamma
has the same properties as the one stated in Observation
1.6
. When
\gamma
obeys suitable nondegeneracy and non-zero curvature conditions, one can provide
L^{p}
bounds for (
48
) via the methodology developed in
[
71
]
; this is also the subject of upcoming work by the third author.
34
34

34

This includes in particular the “curved” polynomial case
\gamma(x,t)=\sum_{j=2}^{d}a_{j}(x)t^{j}
with
\{a_{j}\}_{j}
measurable and
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
d\geq 2
.
If one considers the analogue of the polynomial Carleson question, that is, the case when
\gamma(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}(x)t^{j}
with
\{a_{j}\}_{j}
measurable functions and
d\in\mathbb{N}
, then this remains a difficult open question that in particular includes the problem of providing uniform bounds for the classical bilinear Hilbert transform.

1.3.3.
The Hilbert transform along curves

Mirroring the formulation of the previous questions as displayed in Observations
1.6
and
1.7
, the loose generic problem addressing the current topic may be stated as follows:

The Hilbert Transform along variable curves.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and assume that
\Gamma:=(t,-\gamma(x,y,t))
is a planar parametrized curve with
\gamma
a suitable real function that is piecewise smooth in
t
and measurable in
x,y
. Investigate the boundedness properties of the Hilbert transform along the curve
\Gamma
, defined as

(49)

H_{\Gamma}(f)(x,y):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t,y+\gamma(x,y,t))\frac{dt}{t}.

The zero-curvature case
:
\gamma(x,y,t)=a(x,y)t
with
a:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
a suitable function.

We start by noticing that the answer to the simplest possible case
a(x,y)=a
is essentially equivalent to the celebrated result of Riesz,
[
89
]
, on the
L^{p}
-boundedness for
1<p<\infty
of the classical Hilbert transform, whose history goes back at least to the study of the (conjugate) harmonic functions in the
19^{\textsuperscript{th}}
century.

Moving forward, the study of (
49
) for more general functions
a
is intimately connected with the problem of differentiability along vector fields, which revolves around one of the deep open problems in harmonic analysis:

Conjecture (Zygmund):

Let
\gamma(x,y,t)=a(x,y)t
with
a:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
a Lipschitz vector field. Then, taking
\epsilon_{0}
small enough depending on
\|a\|_{Lip}
and defining the maximal analogue of (
49
) as

(50)

M_{\Gamma}f(x,y)=M_{a,\epsilon_{0}}f(x,y):=\sup_{0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{-\epsilon}^{\epsilon}|f(x-t,y+a(x,y)t)|dt,

we have that
M_{\Gamma}
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
1<p<\infty
.

The singular integral analogue is precisely the key motivation for the generic question mentioned at the beginning of this section:

Conjecture (Stein):

With
\gamma
,
u
and
\epsilon_{0}
as before, define the Hilbert transform along
\Gamma
as

(51)

H_{\Gamma}f(x,y)=H_{a,\epsilon_{0}}f(x,y):=\int_{-\epsilon_{0}}^{\epsilon_{0}}f(x-t,y+a(x,y)t)\frac{dt}{t}.

Then
H_{\Gamma}
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
1<p<\infty
.

It is worth mentioning that the Lipschitz condition imposed on the vector field
a
is necessary (see e.g.
[
62
]
). This is a consequence of the existence of Besicovitch-Kakeya sets in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which prevents any
L^{p}
bound on either
M_{\Gamma}
or
H_{\Gamma}
even if
a
is Hölder continuous of class
C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
\alpha<1
.

The first major contribution was due to Bourgain in
[
11
]
, where he proved
L^{2}
-bounds for
M_{\Gamma}
under the assumption that
a
is analytic. The analogous result for
H_{\Gamma}
was proved by Stein and Street in
[
97
]
. In between, some particular cases of vector fields
a
were treated in
[
15
]
and
[
22
]
.

The next deep insight into Stein’s question was made by Lacey and Li in
[
61
]
,
[
62
]
based on the key observation that
H_{\Gamma}
enjoys a modulation invariance symmetry and hence, as already referred to several times by now, time-frequency techniques in the spirit of Carleson’s theorem form the natural framework for its study. The result in
[
62
]
was of conditional nature: if a suitable Kakeya-type maximal operator is assumed to be
L^{2}
-bounded, then, under the supplementary assumption that
a
is of class
C^{1+\epsilon}
, one has that
H_{\Gamma}
is
L^{2}
-bounded. This constitutes the last advancement recorded within the realm of genuine two variable dependence of
a
.

In the past decade, the effort has focused on understanding some model problems related to (
51
), all revolving around a one-variable type behavior of
a
. In such a situation, in contrast with the previous Lipschitz requirement, the mere measurability of
a
is enough for proving non-trivial bounds. Indeed, Bateman – in the single annulus case,
[
1
]
, and then Bateman and Thiele – for the general, non-localized frequency case,
[
2
]
, proved that
H_{\Gamma}
is
L^{p}
bounded for
p>\frac{3}{2}
. A few years later, in
[
46
]
,
[
47
]
, the same boundedness range was established by Guo for the situation when
a
is constant along suitable families of Lipschitz curves.

The non-zero curvature case
: a.e.
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
the function
\gamma(x,y,t)
“does not resemble” a line near
t=0
and
\pm\infty
.

This direction of research originates in the work of Jones
[
53
]
, Fabes
[
32
]
, and Fabes and Rivière
[
33
]
, on constant-coefficient parabolic differential operators. As a consequence of these, one obtains the
L^{2}
-boundedness of
H_{\Gamma}
for
\gamma(x,y,t)=t^{\alpha},\alpha\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1\}
. From this point on the study of (
51
) in the curved case has been adopted as a theme of independent interest within harmonic analysis area. The first such results were focused on the situation
\gamma(x,y,t)=\gamma(t)
and included works such as
[
98
]
,
[
84
]
and
[
85
]
.

The next step was the study of (
51
) for smooth dependence of
\gamma
on
x,y
. Thus in
[
16
]
and
[
5
]
the authors prove the expected
L^{p}
range for the situation
\gamma(x,y,t)=P(x)\tilde{\gamma}(t),
where
P
is a polynomial and
\tilde{\gamma}
is smooth and obeys some suitable non-vanishing curvature condition. In a different direction, this time analyzing the behavior of
H_{\Gamma}
under the assumption that
\gamma(x,y,t)
obeys
(x,y,t)
-smoothness and non-zero curvature in
t
hypotheses, we have: in the nilpotent setting the work in
[
18
]
, and in the context of singular Radon transforms (and their maximal analogues) i) along differentiable submanifolds the work in
[
22
]
, or ii) along variable curves in a diffeomorphism invariant setting, the work in
[
92
]
.

Taking one more step, one considers now the situation
\gamma(x,y,t)=a(x,y)t^{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1\}
and minimal smoothness in
(x,y)
. In this setting, it was shown in
[
79
]
that with the mere assumption that
a
is measurable one can prove
L^{p}
boundedness of
M_{\Gamma}
only for
p>2
. In
[
48
]
, requiring the minimal Lipschitz smoothness in
(x,y)
, the authors proved the full
L^{p}
range for
M_{\Gamma}
as well as the single annulus
L^{p}
-estimate for the singular counterpart
H_{\Gamma}
. Based on the latter and on a further square function estimate, the full treatment of
H_{\Gamma}
was provided in
[
26
]
.

Finally, focusing on the situation
\gamma(x,y,t)=\gamma(x,t)
assuming only measurability in
x
, the first such study was performed in
[
48
]
for
\gamma(x,t)=a(x)t^{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1\}
and resulted in the proof of the
L^{p}
boundedness of both
H_{\Gamma}
and
M_{\Gamma}
within the full expected range. Some related results appeared more recently – see for example
[
66
]
and
[
50
]
. The most recent result,
[
71
]
, establishes via the LGC-methodology the full
L^{p}
range for both
H_{\Gamma}
and
M_{\Gamma}
for a general class of curves
\gamma(x,t)
that includes in particular the case
\gamma(x,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{j}(x)t^{\alpha_{j}}
with
\{a_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}
arbitrary real measurable functions,
\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}\subset(0,\infty)\setminus\{1\}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
.

1.3.4.
Maximally modulated singular Radon transforms

This theme may be seen as a partial symbiosis between two of the topics discussed earlier: the polynomial Carleson operator (Section
1.3.1
) and the Hilbert transform along curves (Section
1.3.3
). The generic formulation of the problem may be phrased as follows:

Maximally modulated singular Radon transforms.

Fix
d,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\vec{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
\vec{\beta}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
. Then, given
(t,-P(t))\in\mathcal{F}_{m}[\vec{\beta}]
, we define the
\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]
–Radon transform along
P
as

(52)

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]}^{P}(f)(x,y):=\sup_{\Gamma=(\cdot,-\gamma(\cdot))\in\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t,y+P(t))e^{i\gamma(t)}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|.

Study the
L^{p}
boundedness properties of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]}^{P}
.

As hinted at in the beginning, this subject has its roots in the work of Stein and his collaborators on the Hilbert transform along curves and on the polynomial Carleson operator. Given the synthesizing character of this subject and consequently its more complex nature, far fewer things are known about it. Indeed, very little is understood about the behavior of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{F}_{d}[\vec{\alpha}]}^{P}
in either zero curvature or, easier, non-zero curvature cases.

The first explicit reference to (
52
) appears in the work of Pierce and Yung,
[
88
]
, which addresses a higher dimensional version given by

(53)

\mathcal{Q}(f)(x,y):=\sup_{Q\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{d}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(x-t,y-|t|^{2})e^{iQ(t)}K(t)dt\Big|,

where here
d,n\geq 2
,
K
is a standard (translation invariant) Calderón-Zygmund kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{d}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a suitable class of real polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of degree at most
d
that omits linear terms and certain types of quadratic terms. The specific restrictions imposed on (
53
) are fundamental for the success of the approach in
[
88
]
: 1) the requirement
n\geq 2
makes the expression in (
53
) less singular since the point singularities
\{0,\infty\}
of the kernel
K
are now embedded in a larger ambient space,
35
35

35

Loosely speaking, this creates more room for the cancellation offered by the phase and thus the “bad set” where
T
is “large” has a smaller relative size.
and, 2) the restriction over the class
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{d}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is essentially equivalent to imposing that
\mathcal{Q}
has no (generalized) modulation invariance symmetries, thereby placing this analysis in a non-zero curvature context.

These aspects allow the authors in
[
88
]
to adapt and refine – via involved technicalities – the methods employed by Stein and Wainger for the treatment of the curved polynomial Carleson operator,
[
99
]
, and prove the fully expected
L^{p}
boundedness range for
\mathcal{Q}
.

In a related direction, this time focusing on a zero curvature situation but assuming a higher degree of smoothness on the multiplier side, there is the work in
[
90
]
, providing
L^{2}
-weak bounds for a suitable anisotropic higher-dimensional version of the Carleson operator.

Returning now to our original problem in (
52
), very little is known. The only result specifically addressing (
52
) that we are aware of regards the following model operator considered in
[
49
]
:

(54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}[m,d](f)(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t,y-t^{m})e^{iat^{d}}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|

\displaystyle\approx\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t,y-t^{m})e^{ia(x,y)t^{d}}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|,

Here one works under the assumptions that the linearizing function
a(x,y)
depends only on one variable and that, essentially,
36
36

36

Up to, possibly, “subtracting” a Carleson type operator from the low oscillatory component.

m\not=d\geq 2
, thus discarding from the discussion the challenging resonant cases.
37
37

37

For the latter, see the discussion of the open problem proposed at the end of this section.
This work reveals some of the difficulties that one encounters even when attempting to treat just the model case of single-variable dependence of
a
in a resonant context. Such an example is provided by taking in (
52
) the case
P(t)=t^{2}
and assuming that after the linearization of the supremum the phase of the complex exponential has the form
a_{1}(x)t+a_{2}(x)t^{2}
. It is now immediate to see that this example covers the behavior of the Quadratic Carleson operator
C_{2}
treated in
[
72
]
.

Given the very limited understanding of the main topic discussed in this section, we extend the coverage of our open questions beyond subjects directly connected to the behavior of the bilinear Hilbert-Carleson operator
38
38

38

For this latter subject, see Section
1.4
.
and end the current section by formulating the following:

Open problem.
Investigate the
L^{p}
boundedness of the operator
\mathcal{R}[m,d]
defined by (
54
) in the following specific situations (in increasing order of difficulty):

•

m=2
,
d=3
: this corresponds to the non-zero curvature situation, and thus relies on the fact that
\mathcal{R}[2,3]
has no modulation invariance symmetry.

•

m=2
,
d=2
: in this situation we have a linear resonance in the second variable, and thus we are in the hybrid zero/non-zero curvature case with
\mathcal{R}[2,2]
obeying the modulation invariance relation

(55)

\mathcal{R}[2,2](f)=\mathcal{R}[2,2](M_{1,c}^{2}f)\quad\quad\forall\>c\in\mathbb{R},

where here
M_{1,c}^{j}f(x_{1},x_{2}):=e^{icx_{j}}f(x_{1},x_{2})
with
j\in\{1,2\}
. As a model case one may want first to investigate the (much) simpler situation of
a(x,y)=a(x)
measurable.

•

m=2
,
d=1
: this represents a zero curvature situation within the context represented by (
52
) with
\mathcal{R}[2,1]
obeying the modulation invariance relations

(56)

\mathcal{R}[2,1](f)=\mathcal{R}[2,1](M_{1,c}^{1}f)\quad\quad\forall\>c\in\mathbb{R},

and

(57)

\mathcal{R}[2,1](f)=\mathcal{R}[2,1](M_{2,b}^{1}M_{1,b}^{2}f)\quad\quad\forall\>b,c\in\mathbb{R}.

Again, as a toy model, one may want to consider first the situation
a(x,y)
measurable depending on only one variable.

1.3.5.
The triangular Hilbert transform

This last topic is of more recent extraction and can be viewed as a multivariable analogue of the subject treated in Section
1.3.2
. Mirroring the bilinear Hilbert transform setting, the generic formulation of the present problem may be shaped as follows:

The triangular Hilbert transform along curves.

Given
\Gamma:=(t,-\gamma(t))
a plane curve with
\gamma
a suitable (piecewise) smooth real function, study the boundedness properties of the triangular Hilbert transform along the curve
\Gamma
defined by

(58)

T_{\Gamma}(f,g)(x,y):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x-t,y)g(x,y+\gamma(t))\frac{dt}{t}.

The zero curvature case
:
\gamma(t)=at
with
