a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

This case of (
58
) was explicitly formulated in
[
57
]
and can be traced back to the study of the two dimensional bilinear Hilbert transform introduced in
[
25
]
. The motivation for considering the triangular Hilbert transform
T_{\Gamma}
with
\Gamma
a non-degenerate line is multifaceted: 1) its study explores the largely virgin territory of highly singular higher dimensional wave-packet analysis, thus revealing some subtle phenomena not present in the one-dimensional universe; 2) it is a highly singular object that covers both the behavior of the classical Carleson operator and that of the classical bilinear Hilbert transform (and its corresponding behavior with respect to uniform estimates); and 3) can be connected with relevant topics in ergodic theory such as the pointwise convergence of bilinear averages arising from
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
actions,
[
25
]
, and the problem of convergence for double ergodic averages with respect to two commuting transformations,
[
31
]
.

While the problem of providing
L^{p}
bounds for the triangular Hilbert transform remains wide open, the investigation of this subject has brought to light some new tools designed to better understand higher dimensional problems with (hidden) modulation invariant symmetries or presenting significant degeneracies. Among these, most notable is the surprising approach of
\textrm{Kova}\check{c}
,
[
56
]
, for establishing the boundedness of the so-called twisted paraproduct, which resembles energy methods in PDE and relies on subtle integration by parts and iterative applications of Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities. Also of relevance here is the work in
[
7
]
, a suitably degenerate situation appearing in the earlier study of the two-dimensional Hilbert transform
[
25
]
. The methodology developed in
[
56
]
remains at the core of the most recent advances in the study of the triangular Hilbert transform, with
[
57
]
discussing a particular model case and
[
31
]
investigating some interesting connections with ergodic theory.

The non-zero curvature case
:
\gamma(t)=t^{2}
.

The motivation for this problem – considered recently in
[
20
]
– is threefold: firstly, it serves as a toy model for the difficult zero curvature case represented by the triangular Hilbert transform discussed above; secondly, it encapsulates the behavior of the particular operators
C_{\mathcal{F}}
for
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{1}[2]
and
B_{\Gamma}
for
\Gamma=(t,-t^{2})
as in the general discussions of Sections
1.3.1
and
1.3.2
, respectively; and thirdly, it provides an application in the number theory realm (specifically to the study of patterns in the Euclidean plane) in the form of a quantitative nonlinear Roth-type theorem.

1.4.
Some related open questions

The present study on the boundedness properties of

(59)

BC^{a}(f,g)(x)=\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda t^{a}}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|

opens up an entire avenue of related open problems that are meant to advance our above described program. In what follows, we list them in increasing level of complexity, as reflected by the richness of their symmetry groups:

Study the boundedness properties of the following operators:

1)

BC^{a}
for
a=2
: quadratic resonance

This question requires key new ideas. Indeed, in addition to the properties displayed in
(P1)
-
(P3)
(for
T:=BC^{2}
) one has the
quadratic
modulation invariance

(60)

|BC^{2}(M_{2,c}f,M_{2,c}g)|=|BC^{2}(f,g)|\quad\quad\forall\>c\in\mathbb{R}\,.

As an immediate effect of this extra symmetry, the
m
-decaying estimate (
69
) in Section
2.1
no longer holds, and thus the strategy exposed in this paper for treating the oscillatory term must be fundamentally changed.
39
39

39

Indeed, if we assume by contradiction that the strategy in our present paper remains true for
a=2
, we see that by choosing the linearizing function
\lambda(x)=2^{2m+1}
for some fixed large
m\in\mathbb{N}
, relation (
69
) would imply in particular that
\big|\int_{|t|\gtrsim 1}f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i2^{2m+1}t^{2}}\frac{dt}{t}\big|\lesssim 2^{-m\delta}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}
for some absolute constant
\delta>0
and any
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. However, this latter inequality is trivially false, as can be seen by taking
f(x)=M_{2,-2^{m}}f_{1}(x)
and
g(x)=M_{2,-2^{m}}g_{1}(x)
with
f_{1},g_{1}
arbitrary functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

2)

BC^{a}
for
a=1
: The (linear resonant) bilinear Hilbert–Carleson operator

This situation is even more complex than the previous one due to the extra degree of freedom within the linear modulation symmetry group
40
40

40

Note the distinction between (
60
) and (
61
). Similarly, notice the extra degree of freedom in (
62
) as compared to
(P2)
.
:

•

the quadratic modulation symmetry

(61)

|BC^{1}(M_{2,c}f,M_{2,-c}g)|=|BC^{1}(f,g)|\quad\quad\forall\>c\in\mathbb{R},

•

the linear modulation symmetry

(62)

|BC^{1}(M_{b}f,M_{c}g)|=|BC^{1}(f,g)|\quad\quad\forall\>b,c\in\mathbb{R}.

As with the previous item, this problem requires key new ideas. In particular, it is worth noticing that
BC^{1}
encapsulates both the behavior of the bilinear Hilbert transform and that of the classical Carleson operator.

3)

BC_{d},d\geq 1
: The bilinear Hilbert – polynomial Carleson operator

Define the
bilinear Hilbert – polynomial Carleson operator
by

(63)

BC_{d}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{d}(\mathbb{R})}\Big|\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{iP(t)}\frac{dt}{t}\Big|,

where here
\mathcal{P}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
is the class of all real polynomials of degree
d\geq 1
.

As expected, because the operator
BC_{d}
has the richest group of symmetries, this is the most difficult problem considered here. In particular, we have

(64)

|BC_{d}(M_{j,b}f,M_{k,c}g)|=|BC_{d}(f,g)|\qquad\forall\>b,c\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\forall\>1\leq j,k\leq d.

The operator
BC_{d}
generalizes both the bilinear Hilbert transform and the polynomial Carleson operator.

1.5.
Structure of the paper

In what follows we briefly outline the organization of our paper:

•

In Section
2
we present an overview of most of the main ideas in our proof.

•

In Section
3
we perform some standard reductions: we make explicit the decomposition of
T=BC^{a}
into its low oscillatory component
T_{0}
and its high oscillatory component
T_{\text{osc}}
, provide the treatment of
T_{0}
and isolate the main part of each
T_{m}
within the decomposition
T_{\text{osc}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}T_{m}
.

•

Section
4
presents the first key ingredient: the discretization of the operator. Here we explain the concept of the two-resolution analysis by introducing 1) the high resolution model with its two variants: the discrete phase-linearized wave-packet model (Section
4.2
) and the continuous phase-linearized spatial model (Section
4.3
), and 2) the low resolution model (Section
4.5
).

•

Within the high resolution analysis, in Section
5
we provide the single scale decay estimate for each
41
41

41

Recall here that we expressed
T_{m}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}T_{m,k}
.

T_{m,k}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
fixed. This is the point where we make use in a key fashion of the phase curvature characterizing the modulated kernel of our operator. Our single scale estimate is achieved via the
TT^{*}
method and a discrete Van der Corput argument involving exponential sums – see Section
5.4.1
.

•

In Section
6
we refine the previously obtained single scale decay estimate by upgrading it to a decay estimate for the associated tri-linear form that achieves a desired time-frequency localization in all of the three input functions. This can be regarded as a single-tile estimate and prepares the ground for the multi-scale analysis performed in the next section.

•

In Section
7
, via suitable concepts of size and mass, we adapt the bilinear Hilbert transform methods to the context of our problem in order to obtain a global control over
T_{m}
and thus conclude our analysis of
T
. In addition, the helicoidal method will be used to obtain the full range of boundedness, including the case where
p
or
q
is infinite.

•

Finally, in Appendix A we discuss the lack of
m
-decaying absolute summability for our discrete (linearized) wave-packet model, while in Appendix B we provide the standard stationary phase analysis associated to the multiplier of our operator
T_{m}
.

2.
General overview of our proof: main ideas and picture from above

The novel hybrid character of our operator within the zero/non-zero curvature framework as revealed by the specific structure imposed by
(P1)
-
(P3)
in Section
1
will place us in some unprecedented situations whose treatment will require several new ideas that are discussed briefly below.

2.1.
Some heuristics

We gradually immerse into our story by noticing that an inspection of the key properties in
(P1)
-
(P3)
reveals the following:

•

The dilation commutation relation expressed by
(P1)
is a statement about the
multiscale
nature of our approach while the translation commutation in the same
(P1)
makes the point that our analysis places equal weight on any spatial location.

•

Next, the modulation invariance
(P2)
implies that our methodology must rely on
wave-packet decompositions
/
time-frequency analysis
originating in the celebrated work of Carleson (
[
17
]
) on the pointwise convergence of Fourier Series and further developed by the influential contributions of Fefferman (
[
37
]
) – in his reproof of Carleson’s Theorem – and of Lacey and Thiele (
[
63
]
,
[
64
]
) – in their study of the boundedness of the bilinear Hilbert transform.

•

Finally, the form of the integral kernel that includes a maximally modulated kernel in the presence of curvature requires an approach that is sensitive to the level set decomposition of the highly oscillatory phase of the multiplier – the central element used to extract the cancellation provided by the curvature. Moreover, given the structure of our operator
BC^{a}
and the discussion from Sections
1.2.1
and
1.2.2
, our approach needs to subsume the Stein-Wainger treatment (
[
99
]
) of the polynomial Carleson operator with no linear phase. On top of the above, the techniques involved for treating the curvature must be compatible with the wave-packet analysis employed in the second item above. All these aspects make the ‘‘LGC-methodology’’
42
42

42

For an overview of this methodology please see Section
4
.
developed in
[
71
]
an essential ingredient in our approach.

In order to detail the above description, we follow the (part II) strategy outlined in Section
1.2.1
and perform the linearization of the supremum in (
1
) followed by the decomposition of our operator
T
as

(65)

T=:T_{0}+T_{\text{osc}},

where (for
\chi(\cdot,\cdot)
a smooth function such that
\chi(\lambda,t)\neq 0
iff
|\lambda t^{a}|\lesssim 1
) we set

•

the low-oscillatory component
T_{0}
as

(66)

T_{0}(f,g)(x):=\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\chi(\lambda(x),t)\frac{dt}{t};

•

the high-oscillatory component
T_{\text{osc}}
as

(67)

T_{\text{osc}}(f,g)(x):=\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}(1-\chi(\lambda(x),t))\frac{dt}{t}.

The low-oscillatory component
T_{0}
behaves much like the maximally truncated bilinear Hilbert transform (see (
110
)), and hence, based on Lacey’s result
[
60
]
, is under control in the same range specified by Theorem
1.2
.

For the second term
T_{\text{osc}}
, immersing the philosophy from the third item at the beginning of this subsection into the scheme summarized by (
7
)-(
9
), we discretize the high-oscillatory component according to the level sets of the phase function as follows:

(68)

\displaystyle T_{\text{osc}}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle=:\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}T_{m}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\chi_{m}(\lambda(x),t)\frac{dt}{t},

where here
\chi_{m}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a smooth function such that
\chi_{m}(\lambda,t)\neq 0
iff
|\lambda t^{a}|\sim 2^{am}
.

Thus, our goal – formulated below as Theorem
3.1
– will be to show that each such piece
T_{m}
obeys an exponentially decaying bound of the form

(69)

\|T_{m}(f,g)\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim_{a,p,q}2^{-\delta am}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}},

where here
\delta=\delta(p,q)>0
and
p,q,r
lie within the range specified in the statement of our main result, Theorem
1.2
.

Returning now to the more philosophical tone of our discussion from the beginning of this section, we notice that the strategy set forth above is common
43
43

43

For a more detailed discussion on this, please see Section
1.3
.
to harmonic analysis problems involving a purely non-zero curvature, which canonically rely on two distinct treatments:

•

the
low oscillatory component
encapsulates the purely singular behavior of the kernel (in the absence of the oscillation) and is always controlled by a variant of a maximal/singular operator that is well understood;
44
44

44

This is the situation where the existence of a mean zero condition – as for our case
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{dt}{t}=0
– plays a determinative role.

•

the
high oscillatory component
is where the phase cancellation becomes relevant and is captured via the (non)stationary phase principle and orthogonality techniques relying on the
TT^{*}
method.

Consequently, in the non-zero curvature case, the treatments of the low and high oscillatory components are expected to be mutually exclusive, with the latter involving no genuine modulation invariance methods.

Going back to our specific problem, a thorough inspection of the elements displayed in (
67
)-(
69
)
reveal the key philosophical difficulty that we are about to face: on the one hand the exponential decay in
m
in (
69
) is a manifestation of the cancellation encoded in the non-zero curvature behavior of the Carleson-type operator
C^{a}
; on the other hand, the very symbol of the operator
T_{m}
– see (
70
) below – is translation invariant under the action
(\xi,\eta)\mapsto(\xi+N,\eta+N)
for arbitrary
N\in\mathbb{R}
.

This reveals a second novelty of this paper in accordance with the truly hybrid nature of our problem within the non-zero/zero paradigm: the approach of both the low and the high oscillatory components rely in a key fashion on modulation invariance and hence time-frequency analysis methods.

2.2.
Discretization strategy–preamble

With this context provided to our story, we resume our analysis of (
68
) by presenting an overview of the key steps in proving (
69
).

We start by decomposing each
T_{m}
as

\displaystyle T_{m}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle=:\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\rho_{a}(2^{-a(m-k)}\lambda(x))\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\rho(2^{-k}t)\frac{dt}{t},

where here we have conveniently decoupled the variables
\lambda
and
t
by writing
\chi_{m}(\lambda,t)=:\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\rho_{a}(2^{-a(m-k)}\lambda)\rho(2^{-k}t)
with
\rho,\rho_{a}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
suitable smooth functions supported away from the origin.

The first manifestation of the non-zero curvature of the phase
\lambda(x)t^{a}
– observe that for each
T_{m,k}
one has
|\lambda(x)t^{a}|\sim 2^{am}
and
|t|\sim 2^{k}
– is captured through a stationary phase analysis that facilitates the approximation

(70)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)\approx 2^{-\frac{am}{2}}\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))

\displaystyle\cdot\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\hat{f}(\xi)\hat{g}(\eta)e^{ic(\xi-\eta)^{\frac{a}{a-1}}\lambda(x)^{-\frac{1}{a-1}}}e^{ix(\xi+\eta)}\psi\big(\frac{\xi-\eta}{2^{am-k}}\big)d\xi\,d\eta,

where here
\psi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\textrm{supp}\,\psi\subset\{s:1/2<|s|<2\}
.

Analyzing (
70
) on the frequency side, we notice that the combined information for
f
and
g
is concentrated within the strip
45
45

45

For simplicity, we focus in what follows only on the case
\xi>\eta
.

\displaystyle 2^{am-k}\leq\xi-\eta\leq 2^{am-k+1}
. Guided by this geometry, we first split the strip into “big” squares of side-length
2^{am-k}
, obtaining a collection
\big\{\big[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)\big]\times\big[2^{am-k}(\ell-1),2^{am-k}\ell\big]\big\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}
as in Figure
2
.

\xi
\eta
[2^{am-k}(\ell-1),2^{am-k}\ell]
|\xi-\eta|\sim 2^{am-k}
[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)]

Figure 2.
Geometry of the frequency plane associated to
T_{m}
: Whitney “big” square decomposition with respect to the singularity
\xi=\eta
which may be seen as a
2^{am}
-dilation of the bilinear Hilbert transform’s frequency portrait. Here
k
stands for the scale of the spatial kernel variable
t
while
m
relates to the height of the oscillatory phase.

Once at this point we remark that Figure
2
captures the very essence of the hybrid character of our operator:

(i)

the dilation commutation and modulation invariance are encoded in the Littlewood-Paley strip decomposition of the frequency plane and the translation invariance of the family of big squares that contribute to the discretization of each of the strips, respectively;

(ii)

the nonzero curvature of the phase manifests (in a first instance) through the
2^{am}
thickening factor relative to the standard bilinear Hilbert transform’s frequency portrait.

Regarded from the angle provided by the above interpretation it becomes natural to split our analysis on two levels:

•

a
low resolution analysis
corresponding to (i) and thus addressing the properties
(P1)
and
(P2)
: the focus here is to exploit the almost orthogonality
among
(families of) big squares;

•

a
high resolution analysis
corresponding to (ii) and thus treating
(P3)
: this focuses on capturing the cancellation
within each
given big square.

In this way we introduce the third novelty of our paper: in treating our high-oscillatory component
T_{\text{osc}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}T_{m}
we design a “two-resolution analysis”.

2.3.
Two-resolution analysis: an introduction

In this section we very briefly present some key characteristics of the two-resolution analysis implemented in the present paper.

(I)

the high resolution, single-scale analysis
: as already alluded, this is the regime in which we exploit the non-zero curvature of the phase of the multiplier.

For this, we fix the scale
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the frequency parameter
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
and its dual space parameter
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and focus on the wave-packet interaction inside a fixed (given) big frequency square

(71)

\displaystyle\big[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)\big]\times\big[2^{am-k}(\ell-1),2^{am-k}\ell\big]\>.

Once at this point we employ the LGC-methodology from
[
71
]
as follows:

•

Stage one is represented by a discretization of the multiplier that achieves the
phase linearization
:

The effect of this process will be the division of every “big” square
\displaystyle\big[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)\big]\times\big[2^{am-k}(\ell-1),2^{am-k}\ell\big]
into
2^{am}
smaller squares, as illustrated in Figure
3
.
46
46

46

This amounts to partitioning every side into
2^{am\over 2}
intervals of equal length.

\begin{subarray}{c}\sim 2^{am/2}\\
\text{many}\\
\text{squares}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}\sim 2^{am/2}\\
\text{many squares}\end{subarray}

Figure 3.
The multiplier’s phase behavior is essentially linear within each small square obtained by splitting every “big” square into
2^{am}
identical pieces.

•

Stage two consists of an adapted
Gabor frame
discretization of the input functions.

•

Stage three employs almost orthogonality methods relying on the
TT^{*}
-method
and time-frequency
correlations
.

An additional, novel ingredient within this analysis is the simultaneous two types of models, that, though equivalent, organize differently the information of the small squares living within the given big square. For an overview of this approach please see Section
2.5
. The main conclusion of this analysis is provided in Proposition
5.1
, Section
5
.

(II)

A transitional step – acquiring compatibility between
(I)
and
(III)
: this step is required in order to harmonize the non-zero curvature methods from
(I)
with the wave-packet modulation invariant methods from
(III)
and at the heuristic level should be regarded as a decoupling of the action of the two components blended in
BC^{a}
, namely the bilinear Hilbert transform
B
and the Carleson-type operator
C^{a}
.

More concretely, at this step we refine the work performed in
(I)
by obtaining a time-frequency localized version of the single scale estimate in which both the spatial and the frequency localization of all the three inputs
f,g
and
h
as well as the information carried by the linearized function
\lambda
manifest. This refined analysis is required in order to properly implement the multi-scale methodology referred to in the next item. Our treatment of this second stage will involve the mass distribution of an weight encoding the behavior of
\lambda
(see (
81
)) as well as the size distribution of the functions
f
and
g
. At the end of this process we will have obtained a symmetric trilinear single scale estimate that properly quantifies the time-frequency information of each of the input functions as well as the mass carried by
\lambda
, and all these in a manner that is compatible with the size-energy approach for the bilinear Hilbert transform. The desired localized single-scale estimate is briefly outlined in Section
2.7
and proved in Section
6
– see Proposition
7.1
therein.

(III)

The low resolution, multi-scale analysis
: this is the regime that addresses the modulation-invariance symmetry
(P2)
.

In this situation we combine all the scales
k
together, allowing full liberty to
\ell,r
parameters in order to understand the time-frequency interactions among the big frequency squares

\left\{\displaystyle\big[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)\big]\times\big[2^{am-k}(\ell-1),2^{am-k}\ell\big]\right\}_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}

considered together with their dual space squares. A key insight here is the observation that the time-frequency representation of the entire collection of these squares corresponds to a suitable anisotropic dilation of the time-frequency portrait of the bilinear Hilbert transform. The above considerations trigger the appeal to the methods developed by Lacey and Thiele in
[
63
]
,
[
64
]
, and refined in the form offered by
[
82
]
, based on which – together with the localized version of the single-scale estimate discussed in
(II)
– we will be able to conclude that the dualized version of (
69
) holds. The procedure described here is previewed in Section
2.8
and covered in detail in Section
7
.

2.4.
Two-resolution analysis: time-frequency portraits

In this section we rephrase the above discussion in a geometric language.
47
47

47

Throughout the remainder of the present section the parameter
m\in\mathbb{N}
is fixed.
According to the two regimes – for expository reasons displayed here in reverse order – we will consider two types of families of (tri-)tiles, illustrated below in Figures
4
and
5
:

A)
The low resolution, multi-scale analysis
. In this regime, guided by the previously mentioned analogy with the bilinear Hilbert transform, we are invited to consider the family
{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}={{{\mathbb{P}}^{m}_{\textrm{BHT}}}}
of all tri-tiles
P=(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
obeying

(72)

P_{1}:=I_{P}\times\boldsymbol{\omega}_{P_{1}}=[r2^{k},(r+1)2^{k}]\times\big[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)\big],

(73)

P_{2}:=I_{P}\times\boldsymbol{\omega}_{P_{2}}=[r2^{k},(r+1)2^{k}]\times\big[2^{am-k}(\ell-1),2^{am-k}\ell\big],

and

(74)

P_{3}:=I_{P}\times\boldsymbol{\omega}_{P_{3}}=[r2^{k},(r+1)2^{k}]\times\big[2^{am-k}(2\ell),2^{am-k}(2\ell+1)\big],

with
k,r,\ell
varying in
\mathbb{Z}
. Each of the tiles above has area
2^{am}
as opposed to the area one canonically encountered in the time-frequency analysis literature. This is a direct manifestation of the presence of the maximal modulation, which via the curvature of the phase spreads out the localization of the wave-packet. With
P
as above, recalling (
70
) and defining the dual form
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k}(f,g,h):=\int T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)h(x)dx
(with the obvious analogue for
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m}
) one is able to decompose
48
48

48

For a precise definition please see (
84
), (
85
) and (
86
).

(75)

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k}=\sum_{P\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}\>.

As claimed earlier, it now becomes apparent that
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m}
has a similar (but dilated) time-frequency portrait to that of BHT, with the family of tri-tiles
{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
preserving the key rank-one property.
49
49

49

I.e.
, any tri-tile
P=(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
is completely determined by the spatial and frequency position of any one of
P_{1},P_{2}
or
P_{3}
.

B)
The high resolution, single-scale analysis.
In this setting, as already explained, we are dealing with a single-scale analysis that will take place within each (fixed) tri-tile
P\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
. Though involving only one scale, the time-frequency representation of
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}
comes with an increased level of complexity in part due to the fact that this representation

•

will have as an output the decomposition

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}(f,g,h)=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{am}{4}}\,|I_{P}|^{1\over 2}}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{S}(P)}\langle f,\phi_{s_{1}}\rangle\langle g,\phi_{s_{2}}\rangle\langle h,\underline{\phi}_{s_{3}}\rangle\,,

where here

,

are wave-packets adapted to

and
s_{2}
, respectively, while

stands for a suitable weighted wave-packet accounting for the joint information carried by the linearizing function

and the tile

.

)-(

2.5.
(I) The high resolution, single-scale analysis – taking advantage of the curvature

We design this approach in order to exploit the cancellation within each of the dilated “big square” (see Figure
2
). Thus, we fix in what follows the frequency square (
71
).

2.5.1.
The LGC-methodology: initiation

As briefly mentioned in Section
2.3
, the starting point for the high-resolution analysis is triggered by the methodology in
[
71
]
. Detailing the discussion in
(I)
we proceed as follows:

In the first stage, as a result of the multiplier’s phase linearization process we obtain the partitioning of the big square into
2^{am}
same-size squares – see Figure
3
.

The second stage consists from an adapted
Gabor frame
discretization of the input functions:

Let
f_{\ell+1}
be the Fourier projection of
f
onto the interval
\big[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)\big]
(and similarly
g_{\ell-1})
. Choose a suitably smooth compactly supported function
\psi_{1}
and define the
L^{2}
-normalized wave-packet

(77)

\varphi^{u,p}_{\frac{am}{2}-k,\ell}(\xi):=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{1}{2}(\frac{am}{2}-k)}}\psi_{1}\big(\frac{\xi}{2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}}-u-2^{{am\over 2}}\left(\ell-1\right)\big)e^{-i\frac{p\xi}{2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}}}.

Next, for
m,k,\ell
fixed, we expand
f_{\ell+1}
as

(78)

f_{\ell+1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\cdot 2^{am\over 2}<u\leq 3\cdot 2^{am\over 2}\\
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}\langle f,\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u,\bar{p}}\rangle\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u,\bar{p}},

with the obvious analogue for
g_{\ell-1}
given by
52
52

52

Observe that the shift between the frequency supports of
f_{\ell+1}
and
g_{\ell-1}
is encoded in the range of the frequency parameters
u,v
.

(79)

g_{\ell-1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<v\leq 2^{am\over 2}\\
\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}\langle g,\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{v,\bar{p}}\rangle\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{v,\bar{p}}.

With the above procedure completed,
T_{m,k}
becomes
53
53

53

For notational simplicity, throughout the paper, given
v
a real parameter and
A>0
we write
v\sim A
in order to designate that there exists an absolute constant
c>1
(allowed to change from line to line) such that
|v|\in[\frac{A}{c},Ac]
. If
v
is discrete (hence
v\in\mathbb{Z}
) the interpretation of the above notation is simply
|v|\in[\frac{A}{c},Ac]\cap\mathbb{N}
. Also we write
v\sim_{a}A
if the constant
c
above depends on
a
and it is in our intention to stress this dependence.

(80)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n,\bar{p}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{u,v\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\langle f,\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,\bar{p}-n}\rangle\langle g,\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,\bar{p}+n}\rangle\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,2\ell-1}^{2u,\bar{p}}(x)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))}{2^{am+2k\over 4}}w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)dx,

where

(81)

w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(\xi+2v)e^{in\xi}e^{ic_{a}2^{({am\over 2}-k)\frac{a}{a-1}}\xi^{\frac{a}{a-1}}\lambda(x)^{-{1\over{a-1}}}}\psi\Big(\frac{\xi}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\Big)d\xi

is regarded as a suitable weight whose behavior will play an important role in our subsequent analysis.

2.5.2.
The phase-linearized wave-packet model

The previous reasonings provide us naturally with a wave-packet discretized model of our operator. However, in order to further simplify it, we have to consider the
time-frequency correlations
arising among the parameters
u,v,n,\bar{p}
and
\lambda(x)
appearing in (
80
). A first expression of this phenomenon is revealed by the estimate
54
54

54

Here
\bar{c}_{a}\in\mathbb{R}
is a suitable constant depending only on
a
.

(82)

\big|\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)\big|\lesssim w_{k,n,v}(\lambda)(x):=\frac{\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))}{1+\Big|\bar{c}_{a}\frac{n^{a-1}\lambda(x)}{2^{\frac{a(am-2k)}{2}}}+2v\Big|^{2}},

which is the result of the non-stationary phase principle applied to (
81
). Thus, as a consequence of (
82
) we can reduce the range of
n
to
n\sim_{a}2^{am\over 2}
. Further on, applying another time-frequency correlation reasoning this time relying on almost orthogonality arguments, we deduce that the range of
\bar{p}
should also be restricted to intervals of length
\sim_{a}2^{am\over 2}
. Thus, replacing
\bar{p}
by the parameter
p_{r}
with

p_{r}=2^{am\over 2}(r-1)+p,\quad\text{with}\quad r\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad 2^{am\over 2}\leq p<2\cdot 2^{am\over 2},

and dualizing our expression in (
80
), we arrive at the final form of our wave-packet discretized model:

(83)

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m}(f,g,h):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k}(f,g,h),

where

(84)

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k}(f,g,h):=\sum_{r,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h),

and

(85)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n,p\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\sum_{u,v\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\frac{1}{2^{am+2k\over 4}}\langle f,\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n}\rangle\langle g,\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}\rangle

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,2\ell-1}^{2u,p_{r}}(x)h(x)\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)dx.

Equivalently, one can rewrite (
83
) (and hence (
85
)) in a tile format using the dictionary developed in Section
2.4
. Indeed, given a tri-tile
P=(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})=P(k,\ell,r)\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
verifying (
72
)-(
74
) we can now set

(86)

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}:=\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}\;.

Furthermore for every such tri-tile
P
there exists a unique
1
-to-
1
map between
\mathbb{S}(P)
and the collection of parameters
(u,v,n,p)
along which the sum in (
85
) is computed.
55
55

55

See (
174
) for a precise description of it.
Reparameterizing the expression in (
85
) according to this dictionary one recovers (
76
) with

(87)

\phi_{s_{1}}:=\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n},\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\>\phi_{s_{2}}:=\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}

and

(88)

\phi_{s_{3}}:=\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,2\ell-1}^{2u,p_{r}}\>\>\>\textrm{and}\>\>\>\underline{\phi}_{s_{3}}:=\phi_{s_{3}}\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda)w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)

thus obtaining the tile formulation of (
83
) in the form

(89)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m}(f,g,h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{P}(f,g,h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{am}{4}}\,|I_{P}|^{1\over 2}}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{S}(P)}\langle f,\phi_{s_{1}}\rangle\langle g,\phi_{s_{2}}\rangle\langle h,\underline{\phi}_{s_{3}}\rangle.

Once at this point we recall that our main objective is to prove (
69
). As a starting key step in achieving it one is required to obtain the single tile analogue of (
69
) hence an
m-
decaying control over
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}
.

The first natural attempt in fulfilling this task is to investigate the absolute summable behavior of (
76
) via a more refined exploitation of (
82
) which implies that for fixed
n\sim 2^{am\over 2}
and
x
so that
\lambda(x)\sim 2^{am-ak}
the main contribution comes from those
v\sim 2^{am\over 2}
satisfying

(90)

2v\approx\bar{c}_{a}\frac{n^{a-1}\lambda(x)}{2^{\frac{a(am-2k)}{2}}}\,.

In particular, (
90
) reduces the degrees of freedom of the family
\mathbb{S}(P)
by one. Using the latter, standard almost-orthogonality arguments imply the estimate

(91)

\displaystyle|\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}(f,g,h)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{S}(P)}|I_{s}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\langle f,\phi_{s_{1}}\rangle||\langle g,\phi_{s_{2}}\rangle||\langle h,{\underline{\phi}}_{s_{3}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}\|h\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}}.

However, as previously noted, our goal is to prove such an estimate with an additional
2^{-\delta am}
decay – without which the entire strategy outlined in Section
2.1
, in particular the statement in (
69
), fails. As it turns out, see Appendix
A
, one can in fact show that the second inequality in (
91
) is
sharp
.
56
56

56

If (
90
) were independent of or depend smoothly on the spatial variable
x
, one would have been able to obtain the desired
m
-decaying bound in (
91
).

This reveals that the decomposition in (
89
) does not represent an
m
-decaying absolute summable model
, but – as we will see – only an
m
-decaying conditional summable model
.
57
57

57

One can envision here a parallelism with the well known counterexample regarding the lack of absolute summability for
r<\frac{2}{3}
of the bilinear Hilbert transform model employed in
[
64
]
, though of course this latter phenomenon is significantly more subtle. In particular, not only that the BHT model lacks absolute summability, but moreover, as a consequence of a randomization process, it diverges in the
\ell^{2}
-sense (see
[
60
]
).

This brings us to another novelty of our paper – that of operating with and extracting cancellation from a discretization that is only
conditionally
well behaved. The latter requires the introduction of a second model operator that is regrouping the information carried by the first model above.

2.5.3.
The continuous phase-linearized spatial model

The lack of the
m
-decaying absolute summability of (
89
) reveals that we can not afford to ignore the signum of the Gabor/local Fourier coefficients associated to the input functions. As a consequence, we need to find a way to reshape the information in the above model such that the newly formed terms capture enough cancellation within themselves in order for the resulting model to become an
m
-decaying absolute summable model.
58
58

58

For a revelatory discussion on this issue the reader is invited to consult Section
4.3.1
.

The first step in accomplishing the above is to rewrite
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}=\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}
as follows
59
59

59

Notice that it is at this point in our argument where we break the symmetry between the inputs
(f,g)
and
h
, recasting the trilinear behavior of the form
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)
into an examination of the corresponding bilinear behavior of the operator
S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)
. Later however, see Section
2.7
below, we will need to make up for this asymmetry by a more involved analysis that will end up reinstating the role played by
h
.
:

(92)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,p\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\sum_{v\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)h(x)\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)dx,

with

(93)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\frac{1}{2^{am+2k\over 4}}\langle f_{\ell+1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n}\rangle\langle g_{\ell-1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}\rangle\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,2\ell-1}^{2u,p_{r}}(x).

The key insight now is provided by the fact that, at the heuristic level, one has for every
x\in I_{k}^{p_{r}}

(94)

\displaystyle S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle\approx\tilde{S}_{k}^{n,v}(f_{\ell+1},g_{\ell-1})(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{e^{2inv}}{2^{k}}\int_{I_{k}^{n}}f_{\ell+1}(x-t)g_{\ell-1}(x+t)e^{-i2^{{am\over 2}-k}2vt}\,dt,

where here we used that
I_{k}^{n}:=\Big[\frac{n}{2^{{am\over 2}-k}},\frac{n+1}{2^{{am\over 2}-k}}\Big]
and the convention made for the complex exponential in Footnote
4
.

As a result, combining (
93
) and (
94
), we obtain a new model (equivalent to (
89
)) that is based on the spatial discretization of the input functions in a shape that resembles more the original form of our operator
BC^{a}
:

(95)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k}(f,g,h)=\sum_{r,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n,p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{v\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{S}_{k}^{n,v}(f_{\ell+1},g_{\ell-1})(x)\,\mathbf{1}_{I_{k}^{p_{r}}}(x)\,\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))

\displaystyle\cdot w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)\,h(x)\,dx.

As it turns out, the above represents an
m
-decaying absolute summable model – an essential feature that will be exploited in the next section.

2.5.4.
The LGC-methodology: finalization

This component of our proof represents the last, key moment where we will still exploit the phase’s curvature: the actual extraction of the cancellation will be operated on the continuous phase-linearized spatial model (
95
) while once this is fulfilled it will be transferred to (a slight variant of) the phase-linearized wave-packet model (
89
) that will be needed when implementing the low-resolution multi-scale analysis – see Sections
2.6
and
2.8
.

Turning now our focus on (
95
), we remark that the presence of the weight function (
81
) in the expression of
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)
breaks the symmetry between the treatment of, on the one hand,
f,g
and, on the other hand,
h
. This explains why in this first stage of the single scale estimate the time-frequency analysis of
h
plays no significant role and also why we need to appeal to a transitional step – see Sections
2.6
and
2.7
– in which we refine and symmetrize the single scale estimate in order to prepare for the low-resolution multi-scale analysis from Section
2.8
.

Combining now (
92
)-(
94
), one deduces

|\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)|\lesssim\Lambda_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g)\|h\|_{L^{2}},

with

\displaystyle\Lambda_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g)

\displaystyle\qquad:=\Big(\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I_{k}^{p_{r}}}\Big(\sum_{n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\big|\sum_{v\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\tilde{S}_{k}^{n,v}(f_{\ell+1},g_{\ell-1})(x)w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)\big|\Big)^{2}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

being controlled by an expression that is essentially of the form

\Big(\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I_{k}^{p_{r}}}\Big(\sum_{n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\big|2^{-k}\int_{I_{k}^{n}}f_{\ell+1}(x-t)g_{\ell-1}(x+t)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x)t}dt\big|\Big)^{2}dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\>.

Obtaining an
m
-decaying upper bound for the above expression constitutes the most subtle argument in our paper: after a discussion on the size distribution of the input functions
f_{\ell+1}
and
g_{\ell-1}
, we invoke a double
TT^{*}
-argument that reduces our task to a problem involving both elements of analytic number theory – via Weyl sums/discrete Van der Corput, and elements of harmonic analysis via phase level set analysis. This approach discussed in Section
5
– see Proposition
5.3
therein –
will allow us to extract the cancellation offered by the phase curvature and achieve the
m
-exponential decay single scale estimate:

(96)

|\Lambda_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g)|\lesssim_{a}2^{-\delta_{0}am}2^{\frac{k}{2}}\|f_{\ell+1}\cdot\tilde{\chi}_{[r2^{k},(r+1)2^{k}]}\|_{L^{2}}\|g_{\ell-1}\cdot\tilde{\chi}_{[r2^{k},(r+1)2^{k}]}\|_{L^{2}}\>,

for some
\delta_{0}>0
.

2.6.
A symmetry conflict and the necessity of separating the behaviors of
B
and
C^{a}
within
BC^{a}

As already mentioned, a recurrent challenge throughout our proof is the “antagonistic behavior of the coexistent non-zero and zero curvature features of our operator”. While the design of a two-resolution approach provides us with a key insight in addressing this issue, it requires us to put together

•

the high-resolution, non-zero curvature methods induced by the Carleson-type component
C^{a}
, and

•

the low resolution, time-frequency methods induced by the bilinear Hilbert component
B
.

Thus, the initial conflict is now transferred into:

•

On the one hand, the structure of our operator
T_{m}
has a special type of asymmetry induced by the presence of the maximal modulation – inherited from
C^{a}
– that in particular gives rise to the linearized function
\lambda(x)
. This also explains the statement and the proof of the single-scale estimate (
96
) which in particular does not provide a well-localized time-frequency information on the dualizing function
h
.
60
60

60

Later on it will become transparent that this asymmetry may be explained by the fact that the desired
m
-decay in the single scale estimate can only be obtained by exploiting the behavior of
\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)
– see (
81
) and (
82
).

•

On the other hand, the proof of the boundedness of the bilinear Hilbert transform exploits – via its dualized form – a symmetric role of the time-frequency information carried by the input functions
f,g
and
h
.
61
61

61

This is transparent in the “size-energy” formulation stated below in Proposition
2.1
, Section
2.8
.

The appeasement of this conflict is obtained through a “half-way” compromise:

•

The first action, from the
T_{m}
side, is to substantially refine (
96
), in particular increasing its symmetry by also producing the time-frequency localization for
h\cdot w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)
.

•

The second action comes from the direction of the BHT analysis and relies on modifying/adapting the original concepts of size and energy to the presence of the weight
w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)
and, consequently, to the distinct roles played by
(f,g)
and
h
in our problem.

Now the first action is part of the transitional step to be discussed in the next section, while the second action is part of the multi-scale time-frequency approach that will be the subject of Section
2.8
.

We end this section by providing another angle on the above mentioned conflict that follows from inspecting the structure of
BC^{a}
: on the multiplier side – see
e.g.
(
70
) – there is a merging between the bilinear Hilbert transform behavior focusing on the Whitney decomposition relative to the
\xi-\eta=0
“singularity” and the Carleson-type behavior encapsulated in the expression
e^{ic(\xi-\eta)^{\frac{a}{a-1}}\lambda(x)^{-\frac{1}{a-1}}}
. These two operators end up on the multiplier side by acting on the same variable
\xi-\eta
. Equivalently, on the physical space side, there is an intertwinement between the structure of the input functions –
f(x-t)g(x+t)
– and that corresponding to the kernel
\frac{e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}}{t}
, respectively. This phenomenon makes more difficult the process of identifying a way to decouple/untwist the behaviors of
B
and
C^{a}
within
BC^{a}
, which plays a key role in our approach.
62
62

62

In contrast with this – see the discussion within Section
1.2.3
, II.4 – the decoupling process within the analysis of the Bi-Carleson operator (
33
) is easier since in this latter situation the BHT singularity and the Carleson-type singularity do not act on the same variable.

2.7.
(II) A transitional step: refinement of the single-scale estimate

The main task here is to obtain a trilinear dualized version of (
96
) with a sharp time-frequency localization of the input functions
f,g
and
h
that simultaneously quantifies the presence of the linearizing function
\lambda
and separates the information carried by the input functions
f,g
from the information carried by
h
and
w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)
.

The first key insight here is to notice that at a heuristic level one can pretend that

(97)

\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p^{\prime}}(f,g)(x):=S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p^{\prime}}(f,g)(x)e^{-i2\ell 2^{am\over 2}(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})}\>\>\textrm{is constant on}\>\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}\>,

where here we used the notation
\displaystyle\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}:=\Big[2^{k}(r-1)+\frac{p^{\prime}}{2^{am-k}},2^{k}(r-1)+\frac{p^{\prime}+1}{2^{am-k}}\Big]
.

As a consequence, for any positive
w_{k,n,v}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
, one may think that

(98)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p^{\prime}}(f_{\ell+1},

\displaystyle g_{\ell-1})(x)e^{i2\ell 2^{am\over 2}(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})}h(x)\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)dx

\displaystyle\approx 2^{-\frac{am-k}{2}}\big\langle h\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda)w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda),\check{\varphi}_{am-k,2\ell-1}^{0,2^{am}(r-1)+p^{\prime}}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p^{\prime}}(f_{\ell+1},g_{\ell-1})(x)w_{k,n,v}(x)dx}{\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}w_{k,n,v}(x)dx}.

After a Cauchy-Schwarz argument, one then concludes that

(99)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\big|\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{n,v\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\sum_{p^{\prime}\sim 2^{am}}\frac{\big|\langle h\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(\cdot))w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda),\check{\varphi}_{am-k,2\ell-1}^{0,2^{am}(r-1)+p^{\prime}}\rangle\big|}{\big(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}w_{k,n,v}(x)dx\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}\big|\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p^{\prime}}(f_{\ell+1},g_{\ell-1})(x)\big|^{2}w_{k,n,v}(x)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

More precisely, (
98
) and (
99
) have to be understood on the right hand side as a superposition of similar terms with fast decaying amplitudes – that will be made more rigorous in Section
6
.

As a quick remark, the liberty of choosing any positive
w_{k,n,v}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
in (
99
) will offer us great flexibility later when analyzing the behavior of each of the terms involved in decomposition (
100
) below.

Once at this point we will need to consider three key parameters
63
63

63

Recall relation (
82
).
:

•

the “mass” of the weight
w_{k,n,v}(\lambda)
, roughly defined as
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}w_{k,n,v}(\lambda)(x)dx
and encoding the behavior of the linearizing function
\lambda
;

•

the size distribution of the input function
f
; and

•

the size distribution of the input function
g
.

The behavior of these parameters will determine the partitioning of the set of indices

\mathcal{I}:=\Big\{(p^{\prime},n,v)|p^{\prime}\sim 2^{am},n,v\sim 2^{am\over 2}\Big\}

into three components

\mathcal{I}=\mathfrak{l}\cup\mathfrak{u}\cup\mathfrak{c}

with
\mathfrak{l}
the light mass set,
\mathfrak{u}
the heavy mass-uniform input distribution set and
\mathfrak{c}
the heavy mass-clustered input distribution set. This will reflect into the corresponding splitting

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}(f,g,h)

(100)

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{l}}(f,g,h)+\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{u}}(f,g,h)+\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{c}}(f,g,h)\>,

with each of the above forms analyzed accordingly:

•

the light mass component
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{l}}(f,g,h)
is composed of the terms that correspond to the indices where the associated mass of
w_{k,n,v}(\lambda)
is small;

•

the heavy mass-uniform input component
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{u}}(f,g,h)
addresses precisely the situation that captures the cancellation offered by the non-zero curvature and relies in a key fashion on the estimate (
96
); and

•

the heavy mass-clustered input
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{c}}(f,g,h)
captures the situation when the size of the input functions
f,g
is larger than the expected average value.

The conclusion of this analysis will be provided by Proposition
7.1
, Section
7
, which states that

(101)

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{l}}(f,g,h)\lesssim|I_{P}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}f(P_{1})g(P_{2})h_{\mathfrak{l}}(P_{3}),

(102)

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{u}}(f,g,h)\lesssim 2^{-\frac{\delta_{1}am}{2}}|I_{P}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}f(P_{1})g(P_{2})h_{\mathfrak{u}}(P_{3}),

for some
\delta_{1}>0
, and

(103)

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{\mathfrak{c}}(f,g,h)\lesssim|I_{P}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}f(P_{1})g(P_{2})h_{\mathfrak{c}}(P_{3}),

where here
f(P_{1})
and
g(P_{2})
represent local
\ell^{2}
-sums involving the Fourier coefficients of the corresponding function adapted to the family
\{s\}_{s\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{P})}
while
h_{*}(P_{3})
quantifies a suitable weighted version of similar local
\ell^{2}
-sums associated to
h
and restricted to
*\in\{\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{c}\}
and involving both the function
h
itself and the weight
w_{\cdot,\cdot,\cdot}(\lambda)
. The decay obtained in (
102
) is a direct manifestation of the non-zero curvature analysis performed at stage
(I)
, while for (
101
) and (
103
) the
m
-exponential decay is hidden in the structure of
h_{*}(P_{3})
.

This puts an end to the role played by the non-zero curvature in our problem and concurrently to the first part of our proof.

2.8.
(III) The low resolution, multi-scale analysis

In this final part of the proof’s exposition we address the underlying modulation invariance structure of our problem encoded in
(P2)
. As already explained in Section
2.3
, our plan is to appeal to the time-frequency techniques originating in the work of Lacey and Thiele as modeled by the following general abstract result
64
64

64

For the definitions of mass and energy (and other related concepts) please see Section
7
.
65
65

65

This general abstract result stated above was necessary when treating objects having a structure more complex than the standard BHT as revealed by the following examples: 1) the Bi-est operator regarded as BHT composed with another BHT, or, 2) the Bi-Carleson operator
Bi\text{-}C
already mentioned in Section
1.2.3
(see (
33
)) that can be seen as a composition between BHT and the Carleson operator.
:

Proposition 2.1

(Proposition 6.5 of
[
82
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}\subset{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
be a finite collection of tri-tiles and
(a_{P_{j}})_{P\in\mathbb{P}}
a sequence of complex numbers. Then for any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\in[0,1)
with
\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+\theta_{3}=1

\displaystyle\big|\sum_{P\in\mathbb{P}}\frac{1}{|I_{P}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}a_{P_{1}}a_{P_{2}}a_{P_{3}}\big|\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{3}\big(\mathrm{Size\,}^{\mathbb{P}}_{j}((a_{P_{j}})_{P\in\mathbb{P}})\big)^{\theta_{j}}\big(\mathrm{Energy\,}^{\mathbb{P}}_{j}((a_{P_{j}})_{P\in\mathbb{P}})\big)^{1-\theta_{j}}.

Defining now
\underline{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{m}(f,g,h):=\sum_{P\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}^{*}(f,g,h)
we insert
(
101
)-(
103
) into the above proposition – with the latter used for the most part as a black-box – where for
*\in\{\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{c}\}
and
P\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
we set
a_{P_{1}}:=f(P_{1})
,
a_{P_{2}}:=g(P_{2})
and
a_{P_{3}}:=h_{*}(P_{3})
.

In order to obtain though (
69
), we have to further address the symmetry conflict exposed in Section
2.6
and, in particular, the different scaling manifested in the “two-resolution analysis”: the above proposition involves the
2^{am}
-area tiles part of the definition of tri-tiles
P
while the structure of the coefficients
f(P_{1})
,
g(P_{2})
and
h_{*}(P_{3})
depends on the interaction among area one tiles part of the tri-tile family
\mathbb{S}(P)
.

In answering the above we will have to re-work the classical energy and size estimates and adapt them to our context. In particular, due to the high-resolution discretization – see Sections
2.4
and
2.7
– the sizes for
f
and
g
will be local
L^{2}
averages, with the associated energy still remaining an
L^{2}
-based quantity; for
h
, we will need to consider different sizes that also take into account the information carried by
w_{k,n,v}(\lambda)
, each developing from the light, uniform, or clustered components.

The key step for obtaining our desired
m
-decay is revealed in Proposition
7.2
of Section
7
whose proof goes through estimating size and energy quantities and can be summarized as follows: For any subcollection
\mathbb{P}\subset{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
we have

•

L^{2}
control over the size of the uniform component
\{h_{\mathfrak{u}}(P_{3})\}_{P\in\mathbb{P}}
;
i.e.
,

(104)

\mathrm{Size\,}^{\mathbb{P}}_{3}(\{h_{\mathfrak{u}}(P_{3})\}_{P\in\mathbb{P}})\lesssim\sup_{P\in\mathbb{P}}\frac{1}{|I_{P}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|h\cdot\tilde{\chi}_{I_{P}}\|_{L^{2}}.

The simple but relevant observation here is that due to the presence of
\lambda
, the orthogonality among the involved terms is a consequence of the disjointness of the scales.

•

m
-decay within the
L^{2}
control over the size of the light component
\{h_{\mathfrak{l}}(P_{3})\}_{P\in\mathbb{P}}
;
i.e.
,

(105)

\mathrm{Size\,}^{\mathbb{P}}_{3}(\{h_{\mathfrak{l}}(P_{3})\}_{P\in\mathbb{P}})\lesssim 2^{-\frac{\delta_{1}am}{4}}\sup_{P\in\mathbb{P}}\frac{1}{|I_{P}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|h\cdot\tilde{\chi}_{I_{P}}\|_{L^{2}}.

This is a direct consequence of the “lightness” of the weight
w_{\cdot,\cdot,\cdot}
.

•

m
-decay within the
L^{2}
control over the size of the clustered component
\{h_{\mathfrak{c}}(P_{3})\}_{P\in\mathbb{P}}
,
i.e.
for some
\mu>0

(106)

\mathrm{Size\,}^{\mathbb{P}}_{3}(\{h_{\mathfrak{c}}(P_{3})\}_{P\in\mathbb{P}})\lesssim 2^{-\frac{\mu am}{4}}\sup_{P\in\mathbb{P}}\frac{1}{|I_{P}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|h\cdot\tilde{\chi}_{I_{P}}\|_{L^{2}}.

Here one relies on the fact that there are at most
O(2^{(1-\mu)\frac{am}{2}})
values of
n
for which there exists
v\sim 2^{am\over 2}
such that
(p^{\prime},n,v)\in\mathfrak{c}
, where
\mu
is a numerical constant chosen small enough.

Putting (
101
)-(
106
) all together, we obtain – via the proposition above – the desired estimate (
69
) within the local-
L^{2}
range. A standard interpolation argument, having as end-points our earlier obtained local-
L^{2}
estimate and the result in
[
60
]
proves the result claimed by our Theorem
1.2
in the interior of the full range – see Section
7
. We point out that in order to get the estimates corresponding to one of the exponents
p
or
q
in Theorem
1.2
being
\infty
, we need to go through an extra interpolation step at the level of the spatially localized “sizes”, see Subsection
7.5.2
.

3.
Initial reductions

In this section we will operate via various decompositions several simplifications on our bilinear Hilbert-Carleson operator
T:=BC^{a}
. Indeed, departing from the strategy outlined in Section
2.1
– recall here the key relations (
65
)–(
68
) – we will
address the following items:

•

the rigorous decomposition of
T
into the sum of two components: the low oscillatory component
T_{0}
and the high oscillatory component
T_{\mathrm{osc}}
;

•

the complete treatment of the low oscillatory component
T_{0}
;

•

the rigorous decomposition of the high oscillatory component into a sum of operators according to the level sets of the phase of the multiplier thus clarifying the decomposition
T_{\mathrm{osc}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}T_{m}
evoked in (
68
);

•

the reduction to the local-
L^{2}
case for the boundedness range of
T_{m}
;

•

the scale decomposition
T_{m}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}T_{m,k}
and the identification of the main component of the multiplier associated with each
T_{m,k}
.

3.1.
The decomposition
T=T_{0}+T_{\mathrm{osc}}

By a standard argument, we start by constructing a smooth non-negative even bump function
\rho
supported in
[-2,-1/2]\cup[1/2,2]
such that for every
t\neq 0

(107)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\rho(2^{-k}t)=1.

Similarly, we can identify a smooth non-negative even bump function
\tilde{\rho}=\tilde{\rho}_{a}
supported in
[-2^{a},-2^{-a}]\cup[2^{-a},2^{a}]
such that for every
\lambda\neq 0

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-aj}\lambda)=1.

Next we set

\chi(\lambda,t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
j+k\leq 0\end{subarray}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-aj}\lambda)\rho(2^{-k}t)

and notice that this function has the following properties: 1)
\chi(\lambda,t)\lesssim 1
for all
\lambda,t\neq 0
, and 2) if
\chi(\lambda,t)\neq 0
then
|\lambda t^{a}|\lesssim 1
.

Selecting now in the rôle of
\lambda
the function
\lambda(x)
arising from the linearization of the supremum in the definition of
BC^{a}
, we have that

BC^{a}(f,g)(x)=:T(f,g)(x)=\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\frac{dt}{t}

which decomposes as

(108)

\displaystyle\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\chi(\lambda(x),t)\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle+\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}(1-\chi(\lambda(x),t))\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=:T_{0}(f,g)(x)+T_{\mathrm{osc}}(f,g)(x),

thus completing the decomposition of our operator
T
into a sum of low and high oscillatory components as stated in the introduction.

3.2.
The boundedness of
T_{0}

In this section we show that
T_{0}
is bounded in the same range as the one corresponding to the bilinear Hilbert transform. This will be achieved by showing that

(109)

|T_{0}(f,g)|\lesssim H_{\ast}(f,g)+M(f,g),

where here

•

H_{\ast}(f,g)
stands for the maximal (smooth) truncation of the bilinear Hilbert transform

(110)

H_{\ast}(f,g)(x):=\sup_{r>0}\Big|\int f(x-t)g(x+t)\theta(t/r)\frac{dt}{t}\Big|,

with
\theta
any smooth bump function supported near the origin, and

•

M(f,g)
stands for the bilinear maximal function

(111)

M(f,g)(x):=\sup_{r>0}\frac{1}{2r}\int_{-r}^{r}|f(x-t)||g(x+t)|dt.

Notice that, indeed, if (
109
) holds, then the claim about the boundedness range for
T_{0}
follows from Lacey’s work in
[
60
]
(see also
[
24
]
for further generalisations).

Turning now towards the proof of (
109
), we split
T_{0}
as

\displaystyle T_{0}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle=\int f(x-t)g(x+t)\chi(\lambda(x),t)\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int f(x-t)g(x+t)(e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}-1)\chi(\lambda(x),t)\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=:I+II.

We now see that the first term on the right-hand side is bounded by
H_{\ast}(f,g)(x)
:

\displaystyle|I|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int f(x-t)g(x+t)\chi(\lambda(x),t)\frac{dt}{t}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-aj}\lambda(x))\;\int f(x-t)g(x+t)\sum_{k\leq-j}\rho(2^{-k}t)\frac{dt}{t}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-aj}\lambda(x))\;\int f(x-t)g(x+t)\theta(2^{j}t)\frac{dt}{t}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-aj}\lambda(x))\Big)H_{\ast}(f,g)(x)=H_{\ast}(f,g)(x),

where here we have let
\theta(t):=\sum_{k\leq 0}\rho(2^{-k}t)
(a smooth bump function supported near the origin).

For the second term, we use the relations
|e^{is}-1|\leq|s|
and
\chi(\lambda,t)\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{[-C,C]}(\lambda t^{a})
in order to deduce that

\displaystyle|II|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{t:|\lambda(x)t^{a}|\lesssim 1\}}|f(x-t)||g(x+t)||\lambda(x)t^{a}|\frac{dt}{|t|}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-\ell}\int_{\{t:|\lambda(x)t^{a}|\sim 2^{-\ell}\}}|f(x-t)||g(x+t)|\frac{dt}{|t|}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-\ell}\frac{1}{2^{-\ell/a}|\lambda(x)|^{-1/a}}\int_{\{t:|t|\lesssim 2^{-\ell/a}|\lambda(x)|^{-1/a}\}}|f(x-t)||g(x+t)|dt

\displaystyle\lesssim M(f,g)(x).

This ends the proof of (
109
).

3.3.
Level set partition of the multiplier’s phase: the decomposition
T_{\mathrm{osc}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}T_{m}

We start by splitting the operator into components in which both the spatial scale and the parameter
66
66

66

Since any bound we will obtain below will be independent of the measurable function
\lambda(\cdot)
, from now on we consider it fixed once and for all and omit it when convenient from the notation.

\lambda
are localized. Then we will reassemble the pieces according to the magnitude of the oscillatory phase – it is in this latter parameter that we will obtain decay in our norm bounds, allowing us to sum all the contributions together.

Observe that by construction we have
67
67

67

Notice that
1-\chi(\lambda,t)
nicely factors into terms in which
\lambda
is localized and terms in which
t
is localized, this being the very reason for which we defined

the way we did, instead of something simpler such as

with

j+k>0\end{subarray}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-aj}\lambda)\rho(2^{-k}t),

and therefore we can decompose

accordingly as

T_{\mathrm{osc}}(f,g)(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
j+k>0\end{subarray}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-aj}\lambda(x))\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\rho(2^{-k}t)\frac{dt}{t}.

Letting

m

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{osc}}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-a(m-k)}\lambda(x))\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\rho(2^{-k}t)\frac{dt}{t}

(112)

\displaystyle=:\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)=:\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}T_{m}(f,g)(x).

Observe that in each of the integrals above for a given
m
we have
|\lambda(x)t^{a}|\sim 2^{am}
; in other words, we have localized the magnitude of the phase thus achieving the decomposition we desired.

3.4.
The boundedness range of
T_{m}
– reduction to the local-
L^{2}
case

As explained in the introduction, the rest of the paper will be devoted to the study of

T_{m}(f,g):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}T_{m,k}(f,g).

Given
m\in\mathbb{N}
, our final aim is to show that
T_{m}
obeys
L^{p}\times L^{q}\to L^{r}
bounds that decay exponentially in the parameter
m
.

More precisely, our main result will be the following:

Theorem 3.1
.

For any exponents
p,q,r
such that
1<p,q\leq\infty
,
2/3<r<\infty
and
1/p+1/q=1/r
there exists
\delta=\delta(p,q)>0
such that for all
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R})
and for all
m\in\mathbb{N}

(113)

\|T_{m}(f,g)\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim_{a,p,q}2^{-\delta am}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}.

Moreover, the implicit constant is independent of the fixed measurable function
\lambda
.

To begin with, we notice that each
T_{m}
is controlled pointwise by the bilinear maximal function
M
defined in (
111
):

(114)

\displaystyle|T_{m}(f,g)(x)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-a(m-k)}\lambda(x))\int f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\lambda(x)t^{a}}\rho(2^{-k}t)\frac{dt}{t}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-a(m-k)}\lambda(x))\int|f(x-t)||g(x+t)||\rho(2^{-k}t)|\frac{dt}{|t|}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\rho}(2^{-a(m-k)}\lambda(x))M(f,g)(x)\lesssim M(f,g)(x).

Hence the boundedness of
T_{m}
follows from that of

; in particular, from
[

]
,

is

,
2/3<r<\infty
with
1/p+1/q=1/r
. Therefore we also have uniformly in

\|T_{m}(f,g)\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim_{a,p,q}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}.

Because of this and by means of interpolation,

3.5.
The main component of
T_{m,k}
: stationary phase analysis.

In this section we will focus on the analysis of the multiplier symbol associated with
T_{m,k}
as represented by the formula

T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)=\tilde{\rho}\Big(\frac{\lambda(x)}{2^{a(m-k)}}\Big)\iint\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{g}(\eta)e^{i(\xi+\eta)x}\mathfrak{M}_{m,k}(\xi-\eta;\lambda(x))d\xi d\eta,

where

(116)

\mathfrak{M}_{m,k}(\zeta;\lambda):=\int e^{i\lambda t^{a}}e^{-i\zeta t}\frac{\rho(2^{-k}t)}{t}dt.

Since
\mathfrak{M}_{m,k}
is an oscillatory integral, we will make use of the stationary phase principle in order to decompose our operator
T_{m,k}
into a principal component that will become the main object of interest for the remainder of the paper and an error term that will be controlled by the bilinear maximal function defined in (
111
). In the present section we will only provide a heuristic argument for identifying the dominant term of
T_{m,k}
(respectively
\mathfrak{M}_{m,k}
), while the detailed but largely standard analysis involving more laborious computations and reductions can be find in Appendix
B
.

These being said, we turn out attention towards (
116
) and notice that the phase of
\mathfrak{M}_{m,k}
, that is
\mu(t):=\lambda t^{a}-\zeta t
, has – up to a sign – a single critical real point at
|t_{c}|:=\left|\zeta/a\lambda\right|^{1/(a-1)}
. A heuristic application of the stationary phase principle (see for example Ch. VIII of
[
95
]
) suggests that the main contribution to the oscillatory integral
\mathfrak{M}_{m,k}
is given, up to an absolute constant, by

e^{i\mu(t_{c})}\frac{\rho(2^{-k}t_{c})}{t_{c}(\mu^{\prime\prime}(t_{c}))^{1/2}},

provided that
\zeta
is such that
|t_{c}|\sim 2^{k}
. Expanding the definitions of
t_{c}
and
\mu
in the above formula and taking into account that
|t_{c}|\sim 2^{k},\lambda\sim 2^{a(m-k)}
and
|\zeta|\sim 2^{am-k}
, we necessarily have that the second factor above is of magnitude
\sim 2^{-am/2}
, and thus the main contribution to
\tilde{\rho}(\lambda 2^{-a(m-k)})\mathfrak{M}_{m,k}(\zeta;\lambda)
is morally of the form
70
70

70

Here one can compute exactly the value of
c_{a}
as given by the formula
c_{a}:=a^{-a/(a-1)}-a^{-1/(a-1)}
.

(117)

\mathfrak{m}_{m,k}(\zeta;\lambda):=\frac{1}{2^{am/2}}e^{ic_{a}\zeta^{a/(a-1)}\lambda^{-1/(a-1)}}\rho\Big(\frac{\lambda}{2^{a(m-k)}}\Big)\psi\Big(\frac{\zeta}{2^{am-k}}\Big)

for some bump functions
\rho(\lambda),\psi(\zeta)
supported in
|\lambda|\sim 1
,
|\zeta|\sim 1
respectively.
71
71

71

Notice that we are abusing notation once again: the latter
\rho
is not necessarily the same one as that introduced in (
107
).
For later convenience, we will denote from now on
\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda):=\rho(\lambda 2^{-a(m-k)})
.

Based on the above heuristic (once again made precise in Appendix
B
) we have that Theorem
3.2
will follow from the following:

Theorem 3.3
.

With
\mathfrak{m}_{m,k}
defined by (

) we let (with a further abuse of notation)

(118)

T_{m,k}(f,g)(x):=\iint\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{g}(\eta)e^{i(\xi+\eta)x}\mathfrak{m}_{m,k}(\xi-\eta;\lambda(x))d\xi d\eta

and

.

For any

such that

and

, there exists
\delta=\delta(p,q)>0
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
72
72

Here the implicit constant depends at most polynomially on the

appearing in (

).

(119)

\Big\|T_{m}(f,g)\Big\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim_{a,p,q}2^{-\delta am}\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}.

This result will be derived as a consequence of Proposition

4.
Two-resolution analysis: Discretization

4.1.
Preliminaries

In this section we will introduce the model operators that are critical for the study of
BC^{a}
. Generically speaking, the role of such a model is to express the original operator, say
T
, as a superposition of objects of lower complexity (thus easier to be analyzed) while properly capturing the key features of
T
(
e.g.
dilation, translation, modulation invariance with all these regarded as part of the larger interaction between the structure of the input functions and that of the kernel). Of course, the geometry of the time-frequency regions associated with these lower complexity objects plays the determinative role in the partition, organization of the terms and finally in regrouping the information carried by them in order to obtain the global control over
T
.

Loosely speaking, through our discretization procedure(s) we aim to handle the two competing features of
BC^{a}
: the non-zero curvature (phase) and the modulation invariance (input).

Indeed, the current section will be divided into two major components:

•

the high resolution modelling
: designed with the purpose of extracting the cancellation encoded in the curvature of the phase of our kernel/multiplier within every single scale, its analysis is further split into three stages:

–

construction of the
discrete phase-linearized wave-packet model
: this is performed in Section
4.2
and follows a methodology – detailed in Section
4.2.2
– that involves the phase linearization of the multiplier and the Gabor discretization of the input functions. While this model will be essential in the later stages of our proof when we need to perform a multiscale analysis of BHT-flavor it comes with a major constraint: it is
not
an
m
-decaying absolutely summable model thus making difficult the task of capturing the cancellation at the single scale level. This is the reason for which we need to appeal to a second, auxiliary model, that will be described immediately below.

–

construction of the
continuous phase-linearized spatial model
: discussed in Section
4.3
– see also the helpful heuristic in Section
4.3.3
– this relies on a linearization process that is applied directly on the kernel (spatial) side of our operator but
without
performing any Fourier analysis (wave-packet discretization) on the input functions. It is this coarser model that will be employed in Section
5
in order to obtain the desired single scale estimate with exponential decay in the parameter
m
.

–

transition between the two models above: this is explained in Section
4.3.2
and will become an important ingredient in Section
6
when refining the single scale estimate in order to capture the time-frequency localization properties of the dualizing function
h
and thus prepare the implementation of the low resolution multi-scale analysis.

Finally, a very concise antithetical view on these two high resolution models that summarizes the need for both of them is discussed in Section
4.4
.

•

the low resolution modelling
: presented in Section
4.5
, this model will be quintessential in approaching the zero-curvature feature of our operator
BC^{a}
. Its design relies on properly regrouping the information contained in the discrete phase-linearized wave-packet model in order to be able to extract the almost orthogonality across multiple scales thus involving the multi-scale nature of our problem. It is through this model that we will be able to exploit the bilinear Hilbert type features of our operator in order to propagate the exponential decay in
m
from the local level (single scale) to the global level (multi-scale model).

The success of our approach depends on the compatibility between the high and low resolution analysis as it is this joint contribution that will deliver the desired result.

4.2.
The high resolution model (I): a phase-linearized wave-packet discretization

In the present section we construct a first model operator relying on a wave-packet discretization of the input functions.

4.2.1.
Preparatives

Recalling the decompositions (
108
) and (
112
) and relation (
118
) in Section
3
, throughout this section we fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and focus our attention on the operator
T_{m,k}
given by

(120)

\displaystyle T_{m,k}(f,g)(x):=

\displaystyle\iint\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{g}(\eta)e^{i(\xi+\eta)x}\frac{\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))}{2^{am\over 2}}e^{ic_{a}(\xi-\eta)^{a^{\prime}}\lambda(x)^{-\frac{1}{a-1}}}\psi\Big(\frac{\xi-\eta}{2^{am-k}}\Big)d\xi d\eta,

where from now on, for notational simplicity, we set
a^{\prime}=a/(a-1)
.

Notice that in the formula above the frequency information is essentially supported in the strip
\{\xi-\eta\sim 2^{am-k}\}
. As a first step we partition this strip in square regions, thus decoupling the non-oscillatory information in
\xi
and
\eta
. For this, we consider a suitable partition of unity

1=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi\Big(\zeta-\ell\Big),

where
\phi
is a smooth function which is compactly supported say in
[\frac{1}{2},2]
. Inserting this into the formula of our operator, in the first instance, we get

\displaystyle T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{g}(\eta)\phi\Big({\xi\over 2^{am-k}}-\ell\Big)\phi\Big({\eta\over 2^{am-k}}-\ell^{\prime}\Big)

\displaystyle e^{i(\xi+\eta)x}\frac{\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))}{2^{am\over 2}}e^{ic_{a}(\xi-\eta)^{a^{\prime}}\lambda(x)^{-\frac{1}{a-1}}}\psi\Big(\frac{\xi-\eta}{2^{am-k}}\Big)d\xi d\eta.

Since the function
\psi
introduced in the stationary phase analysis of the multiplier in (
116
) is supported around
\zeta\sim 1
, simple considerations
74
74

74

By writing each of the functions
\psi
and
\phi
as a superposition of, say,
20
functions, we can further shrink the support of our functions and assume without loss of generality that
\psi
is supported in
[2-\frac{1}{10},2+\frac{1}{10}]
and
\phi
in
[\frac{3}{4},\frac{5}{4}]
.
imply that without loss of generality we may assume that

(121)

\displaystyle T_{m,k}(f,g)(x)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\iint\widehat{f}(\xi)\widehat{g}(\eta)\phi\Big({\xi\over 2^{am-k}}-(\ell+1)\Big)\phi\Big({\eta\over 2^{am-k}}-(\ell-1)\Big)

\displaystyle e^{i(\xi+\eta)x}\frac{\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))}{2^{am\over 2}}e^{ic_{a}(\xi-\eta)^{a^{\prime}}\lambda(x)^{-\frac{1}{a-1}}}\psi\Big(\frac{\xi-\eta}{2^{am-k}}\Big)d\xi d\eta.

Having reached this point, we turn our attention towards the crucial part of the multiplier – its oscillatory component – the one that will dictate the further discretization of our operator. Before entering into details, we make a short detour in order to explain the general methodology that we will follow.

4.2.2.
The LGC-method outlined

In this section we briefly discuss the key characteristics of the LGC-methodology employed in
[
71
]
; these are as follows: phase linearization, Gabor (local Fourier) analysis and almost orthogonality via time-frequency correlation
75
75

75

Throughout the paper the notion of time-frequency correlation designates the existence of a conditionality/relationship among the time and frequency parameters appearing in the Gabor discretizations of the input functions. It should be noted that in general the “correlation” aspect of the LGC method can also incorporate the standard frequency correlation of a given function relative to a Fourier mode; however, this latter notion of correlation is not used in the present paper.
and phase level set analysis. Before elaborating more on the above elements we emphasise that each of them has been commonly used in the math literature: for example phase linearization is extensively exploited in PDE, Gabor decompositions are standard tools in signal processing and more recently in time-frequency analysis while the study of the frequency correlations proves its utility in several areas with a special highlight on additive combinatorics. Thus, the novelty and power of the method
76
76

76

This was introduced in order to provide a unifying perspective on several themes belonging to the non-zero curvature realm. For more on this, the reader is invited to consult Section
1.3
in the Introduction.
does not rely on the nature of each of its constitutive elements but rather on their combined effect in analyzing problems that belong to the general class of singular and maximal integral operators.

With these being said, we now present a very concise overview of these key characteristics:

(L)

linearization
: this represents the design of the spatial/frequency discretization procedure through which the phase of the operator’s kernel (if regarded from the spatial side) or the phase of its associated multiplier (if regarded from the frequency side) is confined to oscillate at a linear level (when restricted to the regions arising from this discretization);

(G)

adapted Gabor frame analysis
: within each of the regions obtained at the first item, one performs an adapted Gabor frame decomposition of the functions on which the operator acts; this produces a new expression which now involves a sum of local Fourier (Gabor) coefficients multiplied by variable weights resulted from the kernel/multiplier discretization discussed at the previous step;

(C)

cancellation via
TT^{*}
-method and (time-frequency) correlation
: the resulting discrete operator is now analyzed at the purely
L^{2}
level via a
TT^{*}
argument. This analysis relies on the cancellation hidden in the oscillation of the discretized kernel/multiplier, and has as an output an expression involving
\ell^{2}
spatial/frequency coefficients associated to the input functions, multiplied by elementary expressions that depend on the time and frequency parameters involved in the discretization. Eventually, one exploits the created time-frequency correlations by employing various elements such as
77
77

77

This list is not complete, and depending on the nature of the problem, not all these elements need to appear in the proof.
the analysis of the size distribution of the (input dependent) coefficients, phase level set estimates, elements of combinatorics and number theory (see
e.g.
discrete Van der Corput or more general bounds for exponential sums) thus achieving the desired generic decay estimate shaped by (
9
).

With these explained, we are now ready to follow the above steps in order to achieve our first model operator.

4.2.3.
Phase linearization

In this section, for a fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
we are going to subdivide each of the frequency squares
[2^{am-k}(\ell+1),2^{am-k}(\ell+2)]\times[2^{am-k}(\ell-1),2^{am-k}\ell]
obtained in Section
4.2.1
into a collection of smaller dyadic squares of a prescribed length
2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}
, designed such that the phase of the multiplier oscillates at a linear level within each such smaller square.

In what follows we assume that
(x,\xi,\eta)
are such that
\mathfrak{m}_{m,k}(\xi-\eta;\lambda(x))\not=0
, see (
117
). Then, if we let
\mu(\xi,\eta)
stand for the phase of the multiplier
\mathfrak{m}_{m,k}(\xi-\eta;\lambda(x))
as given by (
117
), we notice that

\mu(\xi,\eta)=\mu(\xi-\eta)=c_{a}(\xi-\eta)^{a/(a-1)}\lambda(x)^{-1/(a-1)},

with
|\lambda(x)|\sim 2^{a(m-k)}
and
|\xi-\eta|\sim 2^{am-k}
.

From the above we deduce that

(122)

\displaystyle|\partial^{2}_{\xi}\mu(\xi,\eta)|=|\partial^{2}_{\eta}\mu(\xi,\eta)|=|\partial^{2}_{\xi\eta}\mu(\xi,\eta)|

\displaystyle=|c_{a}||\xi-\eta|^{\frac{2-a}{a-1}}|\lambda(x)|^{-\frac{1}{a-1}}

\displaystyle\sim 2^{2k-am}=(2^{\frac{am}{2}-k})^{-2}.

Consequently, if we want to impose that the second order term in the Taylor expansion of
\mu(\xi,\eta)
around a given point
(\xi_{0},\eta_{0})
is at most
O(1)
, we need to impose the condition
|\xi-\xi_{0}|,|\eta-\eta_{0}|\lesssim 2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}
. Now, within such a smaller square region we have that the phase can be decomposed as a linear component plus an – essentially non-oscillatory – nonlinear component.

In light of the above it becomes natural to subdivide the frequency support of
\mathfrak{m}_{m,k}(\xi-\eta;\lambda(x))
into (essentially) squares of size-length
2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}
. This process will be made precise in the next section.

4.2.4.
Gabor frame discretization

Following the above description we will perform a Gabor frame (windowed Fourier series) decomposition of the input functions that visualize the frequency universe in “unit” lengths of size
2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}
. Concretely, we first introduce the
L^{2}
-normalized Gabor frame (wave-packet)

(123)
