\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I^{p}}\bigg(\sum_{n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\Big|\int_{I^{n}}f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x)t}\,dt\Big|\bigg)^{2}\,dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a}(f,g)\lesssim_{a}2^{-\delta_{0}am}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

The proof of this statement will be split in two cases depending on the distribution of the spatial information carried by the functions
f
and
g
:

Fix
\mu\in(0,1)
a small parameter to be chosen later and define

•

the
f
-clustered
(heavy) component as

C_{\mu}(f):=\Big\{n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}:\int_{3I^{n}}|f|^{2}\geq 2^{(\mu-1)\frac{am}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big\}.

Notice here that

(193)

\#C_{\mu}(f)\lesssim 2^{(1-\mu)\frac{am}{2}}.

•

the
f
-uniform
(light) component as

(194)

U_{\mu}(f):=\Big\{n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}:\int_{3I^{n}}|f|^{2}<2^{(\mu-1)\frac{am}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big\}.

A similar decomposition applies to
g
.

In direct correspondence with the above we analyze the behavior of our form
\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a}
according to:

•

the
clustered
component defined as

(195)

\displaystyle\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a,C_{\mu}}(f,g):=

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I^{p}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}:\\
p-n\in C_{\mu}(f)\\
\text{ or }\\
p+n\in C_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\Big|\int_{I^{n}}f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x)t}\,dt\Big|\bigg)^{2}\,dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

•

the
uniform
component defined as

(196)

\displaystyle\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g):=

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I^{p}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}:\\
p-n\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
\text{ and }\\
p+n\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\Big|\int_{I^{n}}f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x)t}\,dt\Big|\bigg)^{2}\,dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and notice that

(197)

\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a}(f,g)\leq\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a,C_{\mu}}(f,g)\,+\,\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g).

With these done, we are now ready to perform our analysis.

Case 1:

The clustered component.

The clustered component has a straightforward treatment. Indeed, relying on the estimate (
193
) and applying (twice) a Cauchy-Schwarz argument we get

(198)

\displaystyle[\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a,C_{\mu}}(f,g)]^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(1-\mu)\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{p,n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I^{p}}\Big(\int_{I^{n}}|f(x-t)|^{2}\,dt\Big)\Big(\int_{I^{n}}|g(x+t)|^{2}\,dt\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-\mu\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{p,n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{3I^{p-n}}|f|^{2}\int_{3I^{p+n}}|g|^{2}\lesssim 2^{-\mu\frac{am}{2}}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Case 2:

The uniform component.

The treatment of the uniform component is the main step for our present single scale estimate and in particular for (
192
). It is within this chapter of our journey that the non-zero curvature plays a critical rôle. Because of its later relevance the proof of the key estimate for the uniform component will be delivered as part of an independent statement - see Proposition
5.5
below. In what follows we show that if the latter holds then relation (
192
) also holds.

For this, it is enough to notice that a simple Cauchy–Schwarz argument gives us

(199)

\displaystyle[\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g)]^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\\
p-n\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
p+n\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{I^{p}}\Big|\int_{I^{n}}f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x)t}\,dt\Big|^{2}\,dx.

However, from Proposition
5.5
, the right-hand side of the above inequality is bounded from above by
2^{-2\delta_{1}am}
for some absolute constant
\delta_{1}>0
and hence

(200)

[\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g)]^{2}\lesssim_{a}2^{-2\delta_{1}am}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Putting together (
197
), (
198
) and (
200
) we conclude that

(201)

|\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a}(f,g)|\lesssim_{a}2^{-\delta_{0}am}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}},

where here
108
108

108

In effect – see (
265
) and (
316
) – the proof of Proposition
5.5
will give us that, up to a polynomial term in
m
, (
200
) holds for
\delta_{1}=\frac{1}{68}\,(1-16\mu)
for
a\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1,2,3\}
and
\delta_{1}=\frac{1}{6}\,(\frac{1}{114}-\frac{4}{3}\mu)
for
a=3
. Thus, ignoring polynomial growth in
m
factors, (
201
) follows with
\delta_{0}=\frac{1}{1292}
(for
\mu=\frac{1}{323}
).

\delta_{0}:=\min\{\delta_{1},\frac{\mu}{4}\}
.
∎

We are now left with proving Proposition
5.5
.

Observation 5.4
.

As already mentioned in Section
5.1
, while Proposition
5.1
is to be regarded as providing the desired control over the single scale component of our operator, the key ingredient to be used later will be offered by the specialized version stated as Proposition
5.5
below (together with its consequence expressed as Corollary
5.6
).

5.4.
Cancellation via
TT^{*}
method, exponential sums and phase level set analysis

In this section we present the crux of our argument which relies on extracting the cancellation hidden in the non-zero curvature of the phase by applying a
TT^{*}
argument relying on elements of number theory (Weyl sums estimates). Within this argument a key rôle – see Section
5.4.2
– is played by the good control over the level sets of suitable exponential phases where these phases are treated as “fewnomials” having as coefficients expressions involving the linearizing frequency function
\lambda(\cdot)
.

Proposition 5.5
.

With the previous notations, let

(202)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g):=

\displaystyle\bigg(2^{\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\\
p-n\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
p+n\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{I^{p}}\Big|\int_{I^{n}}f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x)t}\,dt\Big|^{2}\,dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Then, for a small enough choice of
\mu>0
there exists a constant
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(\mu)>0
such that for any
a\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1,2\}
and any
f,g\in L^{2}
the following holds

(203)

\Lambda_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g)\lesssim_{a}2^{-\delta_{1}am}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}.

Pairing Proposition
5.5
with the reasoning involved in proving (
191
), which reduces the study of
\underline{\Lambda}_{m}^{a}
to that of
\bar{\Lambda}_{m}^{a}
, we deduce the following corollary:

Corollary 5.6
.

With the exponent
\delta_{1}
from Proposition
5.5
, we have that for all functions
f,g\in L^{2}
and all
m\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\bigg(2^{\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{v\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\\
p-n\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
p+n\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{I^{p}}\frac{\big|\int_{I^{n}}f(x-t)g(x+t)e^{-i2^{\frac{am}{2}}\,2vt}\,dt\big|^{2}}{\Big\langle\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a^{2}m}{2}}}\lambda(x)+2v\Big\rangle^{2}}\,dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0003pt\lesssim_{a}2^{-\delta_{1}am}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}.

In particular, recalling the definitions of
w_{k,n,v}(\lambda)
and
\tilde{S}_{k}^{n,v}
– see (
140
) and (
155
), respectively – we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big(2^{\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{v\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\\
p-n\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
p+n\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{I^{p}}|\tilde{S}_{0}^{n,v}(f,g)(x)|^{2}w_{0,n,v}(\lambda)(x)dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0003pt\lesssim_{a}2^{-\delta_{1}am}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}.

All the remaining part of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Without loss of generality, in what follows we assume
f,\,g\geq 0
and
\|f\|_{L^{2}}=\|g\|_{L^{2}}=1
.

5.4.1.
The
TT^{*}
argument and the reduction to an exponential sum estimate

This novel approach within the realm of our topic treats a problem originally formulated in a continuous setting via elements of analytic number theory.
109
109

109

This route was hinted at by the simpler discrete Van der Corput argument used as an alternative for analyzing the
L^{2}
bound of the bilinear Hilbert transform along curves - see Section 8.2. in the Appendix of
[
73
]
.

Returning now to (
202
), we square the expression therein and, applying a double change of variable, we obtain

(204)

\displaystyle[\Lambda_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g)]^{2}

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\\
p-n\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
p+n\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{I^{0}}\Big|\int_{I^{0}}f(x-t+(p-n)2^{-\frac{am}{2}})

\displaystyle\cdot g(x+t+(p+n)2^{-\frac{am}{2}})e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}n^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x+p\,2^{-\frac{am}{2}})t}\,dt\Big|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{am}{2}}\sum_{{u\in U_{\mu}(f)}\atop{v\in U_{\mu}(g)}}\int_{I^{0}}\Big|\int_{I^{0}}f(x-t+u2^{-\frac{am}{2}})g(x+t+v2^{-\frac{am}{2}})

\displaystyle\cdot e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(\frac{v-u}{2})^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\lambda(x+\frac{u+v}{2}2^{-\frac{am}{2}})t}\,dt\Big|^{2}\,dx.

Set now
f_{u}(t):=f(t+u2^{-\frac{am}{2}})
and

(205)

\underline{\lambda}_{x}(v):=\lambda(x+v\,2^{-\frac{am}{2}-1}),

and redenote for notational simplicity
\mathcal{I}:=[\Lambda_{m}^{a,U_{\mu}}(f,g)]^{2}
. Thus

(206)

\mathcal{I}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|I^{0}|}\,\int_{I^{0}}\Big|\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(x-t)g_{v}(x+t)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}_{x}(u+v)t}\,dt\Big|^{2}\,dx.

Rewrite the expression above as

(207)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|I^{0}|}\int_{I^{0}}\int\limits_{|s|\leq 2^{-\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(x-t)g_{v}(x+t)

\displaystyle\cdot f_{u}(x-(t-s))g_{v}(x+t-s)\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}_{x}(u+v)s}\,dt\,ds\,dx,

where
\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}
denotes the characteristic function of
I^{0}
.

Fix now
\nu\in(0,1)
a small parameter that will be chosen later. We next decompose

(208)

\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}^{<}+\mathcal{I}^{>},

where

(209)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{<}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|I^{0}|}\int_{I^{0}}\int\limits_{|s|\leq 2^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\nu)am}}\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(x-t)g_{v}(x+t)

\displaystyle\cdot f_{u}(x-(t-s))g_{v}(x+t-s)\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}_{x}(u+v)s}\,dt\,ds\,dx,

and

(210)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{>}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|I^{0}|}\int_{I^{0}}\int\limits_{2^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\nu)am}<|s|\leq 2^{-\frac{am}{2}}}\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(x-t)g_{v}(x+t)

\displaystyle\cdot f_{u}(x-(t-s))g_{v}(x+t-s)\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}_{x}(u+v)s}\,dt\,ds\,dx.

We start with term
\mathcal{I}^{<}
which is much easier to treat.

Setting
f_{u,x}(-t):=f_{u}(x-t)
and
g_{v,x}(t):=g_{v}(x+t)
we apply a Cauchy-Schwarz inequality under the integral signs in order to deduce that

(211)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{<}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-\nu am}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{I^{0}}\int_{I^{0}}f_{u,x}(-t)^{2}g_{v,x}(t)^{2}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-\nu am}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{3I^{0}}\int_{3I^{0}}f(u2^{-\frac{am}{2}}+y)^{2}g^{2}(v2^{-\frac{am}{2}}+z)\,dy\,dz

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-\nu am}.

We pass now to the treatment of
\mathcal{I}^{>}
. Define the expression

(212)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{2^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\nu)am}<|s|\leq 2^{-\frac{a}{2}m}}\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(x-t)g_{v}(x+t)

\displaystyle\cdot f_{u}(x-(t-s))g_{v}(x+t-s)\mathbf{1}_{I_{0}}(t-s)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}_{x}(u+v)s}\,dt\,ds

and deduce that

(213)

\mathcal{I}^{>}=\frac{1}{|I^{0}|}\,\int_{I^{0}}\mathcal{I}(x)\,dx\>.

We will show that there exists an absolute constant
\bar{\epsilon}>0
such that the following uniform pointwise estimate holds:

(214)

\mathcal{I}(x)\lesssim_{a}2^{-\bar{\epsilon}am},\qquad\forall x\in I^{0}.

For notational simplicity we will take
x=0
and redenote
f_{u}(-t)
by
f_{u}(t)
and
\underline{\lambda}_{0}(u+v)
by
\underline{\lambda}(u+v)
. Also set
J^{0}:=\{s:2^{-(\frac{1}{2}+\nu)am}<|s|\leq 2^{-\frac{am}{2}}\}
. Thus

(215)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(0)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{J^{0}}\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(t)g_{v}(t)f_{u}(t-s)g_{v}(t-s)\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v)s}\,dt\,ds.

Using now (
194
) and applying repeatedly Cauchy-Schwarz, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(0)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{u\in U_{\mu}(f)}\iint\limits_{J^{0}\times I^{0}}|f_{u}(t)f_{u}(t-s)|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)\,dt\,ds\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\cdot\bigg(\iint\limits_{J^{0}\times I^{0}}\sum_{u\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\Big|\sum_{v\in U_{\mu}(g)}g_{v}(t)g_{v}(t-s)e^{i\frac{\bar{c}_{a}(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v)s}\Big|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)\,dt\,ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(\mu-1)\frac{am}{4}}\bigg(\iint\limits_{J^{0}\times I^{0}}\sum_{v,v_{1}\in U_{\mu}(g)}g_{v}(t)g_{v}(t-s)g_{v_{1}}(t)g_{v_{1}}(t-s)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt\cdot\bigg(\sum_{u\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}e^{i\bar{c}_{a}\Big[\frac{(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v)-\frac{(v_{1}-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v_{1})\Big]s}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)\,dt\,ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(\mu-1)\frac{am}{4}}\bigg(\iint\limits_{J^{0}\times I^{0}}\Big(\sum_{v,v_{1}\in U_{\mu}(g)}|g_{v}(t)g_{v}(t-s)g_{v_{1}}(t)g_{v_{1}}(t-s)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v,v_{1}\\
\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\bigg|\sum_{u\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}e^{i\bar{c}_{a}\Big[\frac{(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v)-\frac{(v_{1}-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v_{1})\Big]s}\bigg|^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)\,dt\,ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Denote now by

(216)

\mathcal{J}(s):=\sum_{v,v_{1}\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\bigg|\sum_{u\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}e^{i\bar{c}_{a}\Big[\frac{(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v)-\frac{(v_{1}-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v_{1})\Big]s}\bigg|^{2}.

Then, based on the above reasonings, we deduce

(217)

\mathcal{I}(0)^{2}\leq 2^{(\mu-1)\frac{am}{2}}\int_{J^{0}\times I^{0}}\sum_{v\in U_{\mu}(g)}|g_{v}(t)g_{v}(t-s)|^{2}|\mathcal{J}(s)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)\,dt\,ds.

Our aim now is to show that we can do better than the trivial estimate for the term
\mathcal{J}(s)
, that is, to prove that there exists an absolute constant

\epsilon^{\prime}>0
such that

(218)

\mathcal{J}(s)\lesssim_{a}2^{(2-\epsilon^{\prime})am}.

We start by noticing that

(219)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(s)=

\displaystyle\sum_{u,v,u_{1},v_{1}\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\exp\Big(i\bar{c}_{a}\Big[\frac{(v-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v)-\frac{(v_{1}-u)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u+v_{1})

\displaystyle-\frac{(v-u_{1})^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u_{1}+v)+\frac{(v_{1}-u_{1})^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\underline{\lambda}(u_{1}+v_{1})\Big]s\Big).

Next, we are applying a change of variables as follows:
u+v=p
,
u+v_{1}=q
and
u_{1}+v=r
. Define now

(220)

\displaystyle P_{p,q,r}(v)

\displaystyle:=\frac{(2v-p)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\tilde{\lambda}(p)-\frac{(2v+q-2p)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\tilde{\lambda}(q)

\displaystyle-\frac{(2v-r)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\tilde{\lambda}(r)+\frac{(2v+q-p-r)^{a-1}}{2^{\frac{a(a-1)m}{2}}}\tilde{\lambda}(q+r-p),

where here we set
\tilde{\lambda}:=\bar{c}_{a}\,\underline{\lambda}\,2^{-\frac{am}{2}}
. Notice that in particular

(221)

\tilde{\lambda}(p)\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\qquad\text{ for any }p\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}.

Our approach will be conducted by regarding
P_{p,q,r}(v)
as a piecewise smooth function in
v
with coefficients depending on
p,q
and
r
.
110
110

110

Notice that if
a\in\mathbb{N}
then
P_{p,q,r}(v)
is a polynomial in
v
.

Once at this point we rewrite the expression in (
219
) as

(222)

\mathcal{J}(s)=\sum_{p,q,r\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\sum_{v\sim_{p,q,r}2^{\frac{am}{2}}}e^{i2^{\frac{am}{2}}sP_{p,q,r}(v)},

where in the above we used the notation
v\sim_{p,q,r}2^{\frac{am}{2}}
to state that simultaneously
v\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}
and
a_{1}(p,q,r)\leq v\leq a_{2}(p,q,r)
.
111
111

111

Here
a_{2}(p,q,r)
is of the form
\min\{p-2^{\frac{am}{2}},\,r-2^{\frac{am}{2}},\,2^{\frac{am}{2}+1}+p-q\}
while

a_{1}(p,q,r)
is of the form
\max\{p-2^{\frac{am}{2}+1},\,r-2^{\frac{am}{2}+1},\,2^{\frac{am}{2}}+p-q\}
. If the two relations in the definition of
v\sim_{p,q,r}2^{\frac{am}{2}}
cannot be satisfied simultaneously, then by convention the
v
summation in (
222
) is taken to be zero.

Fix now in what follows
p,q
and
r
. We rewrite (
220
) in the form

(223)

P_{p,q,r}(v)=F_{4}^{a-1}\Big(\frac{2v}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\Big),

where here we set the function

(224)

F_{4}^{a-1}:=x\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{4}a_{k}(x+\alpha_{k})^{a-1},

with

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{p}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}},\quad

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{q-2p}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}},\quad

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{r}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}},\quad

\displaystyle\alpha_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{q-p-r}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}},

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda}(p),

\displaystyle a_{2}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\lambda}(q),

\displaystyle a_{3}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\lambda}(r),

\displaystyle a_{4}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda}(q+r-p).

5.4.2.
Analysis of the phase level sets

Our aim in this section is to obtain good upper bounds on the size of the level sets of the phase
P_{p,q,r}(\cdot)
.
112
112

112

The level set analysis (this time applied to the phase of the multiplier) had a similar importance in the problem treated in
[
71
]
– see the crucial estimate on the number of heavy pairs provided by Proposition 49 via Lemma 51 therein.
The main result here is stated as Lemma
5.10
and is a consequence of the non-zero curvature of the original phase in (
1
) – hence the restriction of the parameter
a
to the non-resonant case.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
d\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
be fixed. Assume we are given
\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}\subset(\alpha,\infty)
pairwise distinct and
\{a_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Define the function
F_{n}^{d}:(-\alpha,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by

(225)

F_{n}^{d}(x):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}(x+\alpha_{k})^{d}.

Then either
F_{n}^{d}
is identically zero or it has at most
n
real distinct zeros.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we assume that

(226)

F_{n}^{d}\text{ is not identically }0\text{ and }d\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0,1\},

as otherwise there is nothing to prove.

We now perform an induction on
n
. Notice that the base case
n=1
is trivially true.
Assume now that the statement holds for
n-1
and assume by contradiction that there exists
F_{n}^{d}
as in (
225
) having (at least)
n+1
real distinct roots, say
\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n+1}
with
-\alpha<x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{n+1}<\infty
.

Deduce then that the function

(227)

G_{n}^{d}(x):=\frac{F_{n}^{d}(x)}{(x+\alpha_{1})^{d}}=a_{1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n}a_{k}\frac{(x+\alpha_{k})^{d}}{(x+\alpha_{1})^{d}},

has the same zeros as
F_{n}^{d}
within
(-\alpha,\infty)
.

Applying now Rolle’s theorem on each interval
(x_{j},x_{j+1})
we deduce the existence of
y_{j}\in(x_{j},x_{j+1})
such that

({G^{d}_{n}})^{\prime}(y_{j}):=\frac{d}{dx}G_{n}^{d}(y_{j})=0
for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. At this point it is worth noticing that
(G^{d}_{n})^{\prime}
can not be identically zero as otherwise this would contradict our initial assumption (
226
).

Next, we notice that

(G^{d}_{n})^{\prime}(x)=-\sum_{k=2}^{n}a_{k}d\cdot\frac{\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{1}}{(x+\alpha_{1})^{2}}\cdot\frac{(x+\alpha_{k})^{d-1}}{(x+\alpha_{1})^{d-1}},

hence we must have that the function

(228)

F_{n-1}^{d-1}(x):=\sum_{k=2}^{n}a_{k}d(\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{1})(x+\alpha_{k})^{d-1},

obeys: 1)
d-1\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, 2) it is not identically zero, and 3) has the above
\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
among its zeros. However, this contradicts the induction hypothesis at step
n-1
, thus concluding our proof.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
F_{n}^{d}
be a non-zero function whose form is prescribed by (
225
) with
n\geq 2
. With the notations from above, assume for simplicity that

(229)

\{a_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\text{ and }\{|\alpha_{k}|\}_{k=1}^{n}\subset\Big[0,\frac{1}{2}\Big]\text{ with }\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}\text{ pairwise distinct}.

Then, given a parameter
\eta>0
, we have

•

if
d\in\mathbb{N}
with
d<n-1
then

(230)

\big|\{x\in[1,2]:|F_{n}^{d}(x)|<\eta\}\big|\leq 100d^{2}\Big(\frac{\eta}{\|F_{n}^{d}\|_{L^{\infty}([1,2])}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{d}};

•

otherwise,

(231)

\big|\{x\in[1,2]:|F_{n}^{d}(x)|<\eta\}\big|\leq K(F_{n}^{d})\eta^{\frac{1}{n-1}},

with
K(F_{n}^{d}):=\frac{3^{2|d|+2n+10}}{\left(|a_{n}|\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}|d-j+1|\,|\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{j}|\right)\,\right)^{\frac{1}{n-1}}}
.

Proof.

First we notice that (
230
) is a simple application of Lagrange’s interpolation formula for polynomials (see e.g. Lemma B in the Appendix of
[
77
]
).

Turning our attention towards (
231
) we will prove this via induction. Set
\eta_{n}:=\eta
and define

(232)

J_{n}:=\{x\in[1,2]:|F_{n}^{d}(x)|<\eta_{n}\}.

Notice now that
J_{n}
is a subset of

(233)

\tilde{J}_{n}:=\{x\in[1,2]:|G_{n}^{d}(x)|<\tilde{\eta}_{n}\},

where
G_{n}^{d}(x)
is given by (
227
) and
\tilde{\eta}_{n}:=3^{|d|}\eta_{n}
.

From Lemma
5.7
we deduce that
\tilde{J}_{n}
can be written as a union of at most
2(n+1)
intervals and, moreover

(234)

\int_{\tilde{J}_{n}}|(G^{d}_{n})^{\prime}(x)|\,dx<4(n+1)\tilde{\eta}_{n}.

Let now
0<\tilde{\eta}_{n-1}
(to be properly chosen later) and write
J_{n}^{1}:=\{x\in\tilde{J}_{n}:|(G^{d}_{n})^{\prime}(x)|<\tilde{\eta}_{n-1}\}
and
J_{n}^{2}:=\tilde{J}_{n}\setminus J_{n}^{1}
.
From (
234
) we deduce that

(235)

|J_{n}^{2}|<4(n+1)\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{n}}{\tilde{\eta}_{n-1}},

while
J_{n}^{1}\subseteq J_{n-1}
where, recalling (
228
), we let

(236)

J_{n-1}:=\{x\in[1,2]:|F_{n-1}^{d-1}(x)|<\eta_{n-1}\},

with
\eta_{n-1}:=3^{|d|+2}\tilde{\eta}_{n-1}
.

Putting together (
232
)–(
236
) we get

(237)

|J_{n}|\leq 4(n+1)3^{2|d|+2}\frac{\eta_{n}}{\eta_{n-1}}+|J_{n-1}|.

Iterating (

(238)

\displaystyle|J_{n}|\leq 4(n+1)3^{2|d|+2}\frac{\eta_{n}}{\eta_{n-1}}

\displaystyle+4n3^{2|d-1|+2}\frac{\eta_{n-1}}{\eta_{n-2}}+\ldots

\displaystyle+4\cdot 3\cdot 3^{2|d-(n-2)|+2}\frac{\eta_{2}}{\eta_{1}}+|J_{1}|,

where

J_{1}:=\{x\in[1,2]:|F_{1}^{d-n+1}(x)|<\eta_{1}\},

with

Taking now
\eta_{1}:=|a_{n}|\big(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}|d-j+1||\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{j}|\big)(\frac{1}{2})^{|d-n|+1}
we see that

.

Choosing now

such that the terms in the right-hand side of (

∎

Observation 5.11
.

The form of our lemma above is suggestive for the restriction

in our main result – see Theorem
1.2
. The fact that for the resonant values

no decay is possible in (

BC^{a}
– see for this the discussion in Section

. In particular, the failure of (

).

Proof.

We first address the cases

. In this situation, with the notations from (

) and (

), we have

(247)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda}(p)-\tilde{\lambda}(q)-\tilde{\lambda}(r)+\tilde{\lambda}(q+r-p),

(248)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\big(-p\tilde{\lambda}(p)-(q-2p)\tilde{\lambda}(q)+r\tilde{\lambda}(r)+(q-p-r)\tilde{\lambda}(q+r-p)\big),

(249)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{am}}\big(p^{2}\tilde{\lambda}(p)-(q-2p)^{2}\tilde{\lambda}(q)-r^{2}\tilde{\lambda}(r)+(q-p-r)^{2}\tilde{\lambda}(q+r-p)\big).

From (

) we have

\Big|\frac{p+r-q}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}A\Big|+|B|\geq\frac{1}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}|(r-q)\tilde{\lambda}(p)+(p-r)\tilde{\lambda}(q)+(q-p)\tilde{\lambda}(r)|,

which immediately implies (

Next, from (

Finally, combining (

) and (

) we deduce that

(252)

\displaystyle\frac{|p|}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\Big(\Big|\frac{p+r-q}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}A\Big|+|B|\Big)+\Big|\frac{p+r-q}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}B\Big|+|C|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2^{am}}|p-r||q-p||\tilde{\lambda}(q)+\tilde{\lambda}(r)|,

from which we deduce

113

113

Notice that relation (

to the parameters

arising from the spatial localization of the input functions (after a double

argument).

(253)

|A|+|B|+|C|\gtrsim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}\frac{|p-r|}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\frac{|q-p|}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}.

Conclude now that (

5.4.3.
The Cases 2 and 3:

. A discrete Van der Corput argument

We are now ready to evaluate expression (

). Our analysis will depend on the amount of cancellation encoded by the rate of change of the phase associated with our exponential sum. Consequently, for a suitable small parameter
\bar{\eta}\in(0,1)
(to be chosen later) we will split our exponential sum into two components:

(254)

\mathcal{J}(s)=\mathcal{J}_{L}(s)+\mathcal{J}_{H}(s),

with

(255)

the
high oscillatory

We now analyze these two terms separately.

The low oscillatory component.

This is the easy situation in which we can simply ignore the cancellations coming from the exponential sum. Indeed, from (

and hence, applying
114

Here trivial considerations allow us to extend the applicability of our Lemma

.

5.10
with

Deduce

•

The high oscillatory component.

We start by recalling the following classical number theoretical result which may be regarded as particular case of the discrete Van der Corput lemma (see
[

):

Lemma 5.12
.

[
Discrete Van der Corput
] Let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Assume we are given a smooth function
F:\,J\,\mapsto\,\mathbb{R}
such that
F
is a continuously differentiable with
115
115

115

Here
\|x\|
stands for the distance from
x
to the nearest integer.

\|F^{\prime}(x)\|\geq\alpha
for all
x\in J
and
F^{\prime}
monotonic. Then
116
116

116

Recall that throughout the paper the display
e^{ix}
stands for
e^{2\pi ix}
.

(260)

\Big|\sum_{n\in J\cap\mathbb{Z}}e^{iF(n)}\Big|\lesssim\alpha^{-1}.

In treating our high oscillatory component
\mathcal{J}_{H}(s)
we are going to apply the above lemma to the function
F(x):=2^{\frac{am}{2}}sP_{p,q,r}(x)
, given that we are now working in the regime
\{v:|P^{\prime}_{p,q,r}(v)|\geq\bar{\eta}\}
.

Next, taking (
206
), we notice that by subdividing
117
117

117

Say for example in
2^{100(a^{2}+1)}
equidistant intervals.
the region of integration in
t
followed by a Cauchy-Schwarz argument, one may reduce the range of

. Since

\frac{1}{2}>\|F^{\prime}(v)\|=\|P^{\prime}_{p,q,r}(v)2^{\frac{am}{2}}s\|\gtrsim\bar{\eta}2^{\frac{am}{2}}|s|.

Finally, based on (

Thus, as desired, we are now in the setting prescribed by Lemma

) we deduce that

\mathcal{J}_{H}(s)\lesssim 2^{\frac{3am}{2}}(\bar{\eta}2^{\frac{am}{2}}|s|)^{-1}\lesssim 2^{\frac{3am}{2}}2^{\nu am}(\bar{\eta})^{-1}.

where in the last inequality we used the fact that

.

For Case 2:

), for

5.4.4.
The Case 1:

The analysis in this case turns out to be more delicate since the decay offered by (

) depends on the structure of the linearizing function
\tilde{\lambda}
. Indeed, a closer inspection of (

) reveals the key underlying philosophical difficulty: as
\tilde{\lambda}
gets “closer” to a straight line the strength of the decay weakens. Thus it becomes natural to perform a quantitative analysis of “the amount of linearity” encoded into the graph of
\tilde{\lambda}
.

We remark here that the decomposition in (

) is not unique and also that it is possible that one of the two sets therein is empty. With these done, we pass to the explicit, quantitative realization of (

):

Construction of
\mathcal{L}_{x}
and
\mathcal{N}_{x}

This construction is realized via the following greedy algorithm:

(1)

set
\mathcal{L}_{x},\,\mathcal{T}_{x}^{H}=\emptyset
;

(2)

set
\mathcal{N}_{x}=(I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathcal{L}_{x}
;

(3)

select
\bar{\tau}\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{x}^{H}
such that
\Delta_{x}^{\mathcal{N}_{x}}(\bar{\tau})\geq 2^{-2\epsilon m}
maximal with this property. If no such
\bar{\tau}
exists then Stop; otherwise move to the next item.

(4)

update
\mathcal{L}_{x}=\mathcal{L}_{x}^{old}\cup E_{x}^{\mathcal{N}_{x}}(\bar{\tau})
;

(5)

update
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{H}=\mathcal{T}_{x}^{H,old}\cup\{\bar{\tau}\}
;

(6)

return to item 2.

Notice that this algorithm must stop in at most
2^{2\epsilon m}
steps.

Properties of
\mathcal{L}_{x}
and
\mathcal{N}_{x}

There exists
N_{x}\in\mathbb{N}
with

(271)

N_{x}\leq 2^{2\epsilon m}\,,

such that
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{H}=\{\tau_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N_{x}}
where here the index
j
reflects the position in the selection of
\tau_{j}
in the above algorithm. For
j\in\{1,\ldots,\,N_{x}\}
, we set
121
121

121

Here, for a generic
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
, we denote with
E_{x}(\tau):=E_{x}^{I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z}}(\tau)
and by convention let
E_{x}(\tau_{0})=\emptyset
.

\bar{E}_{x}(\tau_{j}):=E_{x}(\tau_{j})\setminus\bigcup_{k=1}^{j-1}E_{x}(\tau_{k})\quad\text{ and }\quad\bar{\Delta}_{x}(\tau_{j}):=\frac{\#\bar{E}_{x}(\tau_{j})}{\#(I_{\tau_{j}}\cap\mathbb{Z})}.

Now from the algorithm above we deduce that the set
\mathcal{L}_{x}
can be covered disjointly by a small collection of “heavy” tubes,
i.e.
each such tube has a high density:

(272)

\mathcal{L}_{x}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{N_{x}}\bar{E}_{x}(\tau_{j})\quad\text{ with }\quad\bar{\Delta}_{x}(\tau_{j})\geq 2^{2\epsilon m}.

In contrast with the above, the complement set
\mathcal{N}_{x}
can not concentrate in a suitably small neighbourhood of any given tube
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
, that is

(273)

\forall\tau\in\mathcal{T},\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\#\{p\in\mathcal{N}_{x}:|\tilde{\lambda}_{x}(p)-\ell_{\tau}(p)|\leq 2^{\epsilon m-10}\}}{\#(I_{\tau}\cap\mathbb{Z})}\leq 2^{-\epsilon m}.

Indeed, if (
273
) would fail then via a pigeonhole principle argument we would be able to identify at least one
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
such that

(274)

\#E_{x}^{\mathcal{N}_{x}}(\tau)\geq 2^{-2\epsilon m+5}\,\#(I_{\tau}\cap\mathbb{Z}).

However (
274
) contradicts the construction algorithm of
\mathcal{L}_{x}
and
\mathcal{N}_{x}
.

Construction of a family of auxiliary linearizing functions

In this subsection, for a fixed
x\in I^{0}
, we introduce two families of linearizing functions:

(1)

The first one corresponds to the set of points
\mathcal{L}_{x}
and consists of functions
\{\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{L},j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{2\epsilon m}}
so that for each such
j
the graph of
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{L},j}
approximates the central line of
\tau_{j}
.

(2)

The second one consists of a single element,
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{N}}
, that incorporates the behavior of the initial linearizing function
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}
on the set
\mathcal{N}_{x}
thus isolating the “curved” (quadratic) behavior of
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}
.

We emphasize here that our later number theoretical reasonings depend in a key fashion on the fact that each of the above linearizing functions is extended (and hence well defined) on the full domain
I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z}
while preserving its key flat/nonflat features. This extension can be operated due to the positivity of the expression
\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda})
in (
277
) below which facilitates the fundamental relation (
278
) thus allowing an independent treatment of the linear and curved cases.

Concretely, with the previous notations, we define the following functions:

For
1\leq j\leq 2^{2\epsilon m}
we define
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{L},j}:\,I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z}\,\mapsto\,J_{Q}
by

(275)

\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{L},j}(p):=\begin{cases}\tilde{\lambda}_{x}(p)&\text{if }1\leq j\leq N_{x}\text{ and }p\in\bar{E}_{x}(\tau_{j}),\\
\ell_{\tau_{j}}(p)&\text{if }1\leq j\leq N_{x}\text{ and }p\in(I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z})\setminus\bar{E}_{x}(\tau_{j}),\\
p&\text{if }N_{x}<j\leq 2^{2\epsilon m}\text{ and }p\in(I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z}).\end{cases}

Also we define
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{N}}:\,I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z}\,\mapsto\,J_{Q}
by

(276)

2^{\frac{3m}{2}-1}+2^{-\frac{3m}{2}}(p-2^{\frac{3m}{2}})^{2}&\text{if }p\in(I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{x}.\end{cases}

\ell_{\tau_{j}}
introduced by the definition of
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{L},j}
. On the right, the graph of
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{N}}
, where this time the black points are the ones in
\mathcal{N}
, the shaded points are those in
\mathcal{L}
and the points with empty interior are the auxiliary points on the parabola introduced by the definition of
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

With this, returning to (
206
), we have that

(277)

\displaystyle(\underline{\Lambda}_{m}^{3,U_{\mu}}(f,g))^{2}=\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|I^{0}|}\int_{I^{0}}\Big|\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(x-t)g_{v}(x+t)e^{i\frac{(v-u)^{2}}{2^{3m}}\tilde{\lambda}_{x}(u+v)2^{\frac{3m}{2}}t}\,dt\Big|^{2}\,dx.

Based on (
275
)–(
277
) we immediately deduce that

(278)

\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda})\lesssim\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}})+\sum_{j=1}^{2^{2\epsilon m}}\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},j})=:\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}})+\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L}}).

Once at this point we follow the strategy described in Section
5.4.1
:

•

with the obvious correspondences we perform the analogue decomposition to (

), that is

(279)

\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*})=:\mathcal{I}^{<}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*})+\mathcal{I}^{>}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*}),

where here
\{*\}\in\{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{N}\}
. We emphasize here that we will use two distinct cut-off parameters
\nu_{\mathcal{L}},\,\nu_{\mathcal{N}}>0
corresponding to the parameter
\nu
considered in the decomposition (

)–(

).

•

since the argument in (

) is independent on the structure of the linearizing function
\tilde{\lambda}
we can maintain the same reasonings in order to deduce that

(280)

\mathcal{I}^{<}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L}})\lesssim 2^{2\epsilon m}2^{-3\nu_{\mathcal{L}}m}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{I}^{<}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}})\lesssim 2^{-3\nu_{\mathcal{N}}m}.

•

for the second component,
\mathcal{I}^{>}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*})
, we apply the analogue of (

), and, with the obvious correspondences we write

(281)

\mathcal{I}^{>}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*}):=\frac{1}{|I^{0}|}\int_{I^{0}}\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*})(x)\,dx,

with

(282)

\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L}})(x):=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{2\epsilon m}}\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},j})(x).

•

as in the case
a\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1,2,3\}
, one can prove a pointwise decay for
\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*})(\cdot)
(thus in effect estimating
\|\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{*})(x)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}(I^{0})}
) and hence without loss of generality we will set

.

•

taking for simplicity
j=1
,
\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{\mathcal{L},1}=\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{\mathcal{N}}=\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}
it thus remains to estimate the expressions

(283)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1})(0)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{J^{0}}\Big(\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(t)g_{v}(t)f_{u}(t-s)g_{v}(t-s)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)e^{i\frac{(v-u)^{2}}{2^{3m}}\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(u+v)2^{\frac{3m}{2}}s}\,dt\Big)\,ds,

and

(284)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}})(0)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in U_{\mu}(f)\\
v\in U_{\mu}(g)\end{subarray}}\int_{J^{0}}\Big(\int_{I^{0}}f_{u}(t)g_{v}(t)f_{u}(t-s)g_{v}(t-s)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathbf{1}_{I^{0}}(t-s)e^{i\frac{(v-u)^{2}}{2^{3m}}\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(u+v)2^{\frac{3m}{2}}s}\,dt\Big)\,ds.

•

Following similar reasonings to those in (

)–(

) and letting
\{*\}\in\{(\mathcal{L},1),\mathcal{N}\}
, we reduce our task to that of estimating the exponential sums

(285)

\mathcal{J}^{*}(s)=\sum_{p,q,r\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}\sum_{v\sim_{p,q,r}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}e^{i2^{\frac{3m}{2}}sP_{p,q,r}^{*}(v)},

with

(286)

\displaystyle P_{p,q,r}^{*}(v)

\displaystyle:=\frac{(2v-p)^{2}}{2^{3m}}\tilde{\lambda}^{*}(p)-\frac{(2v+q-2p)^{2}}{2^{3m}}\tilde{\lambda}^{*}(q)

\displaystyle-\frac{(2v-r)^{2}}{2^{3m}}\tilde{\lambda}^{*}(r)+\frac{(2v+q-p-r)^{2}}{2^{3m}}\tilde{\lambda}^{*}(q+r-p),

As before, regarding now

as a polynomial in

and writing

we have that

Treatment of the term
\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1})(0)

In this specific situation, relying on the fact that the graph of
\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}
is by construction “close” to a line, the key insight is to notice that, by rewriting
P_{p,q,r}^{\mathcal{L},0}(v)
as a function of
p
as opposed to

and hence regarding (

) as a discrete oscillatory sum in

, one can reduce the matters to the previous analysis.

More precisely, based on (
275
), we may assume without loss of generality that

(288)

\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(p):=\begin{cases}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}(p)&\text{if }p\in\bar{E}_{0}(\tau_{1}),\\
\ell_{\tau_{1}}(p)&\text{if }p\in(I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z})\setminus\bar{E}_{0}(\tau_{1}).\end{cases}

Moreover, since from the above definitions we know that

(289)

|\tilde{\lambda}_{0}(p)-\ell_{\tau_{1}}(p)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{ for all }p\in\bar{E}_{0}(\tau_{1}),

a standard Taylor argument in the same spirit as the one discussed in Section
5.2
allows us to reduce our discussion to the case in which

(290)

\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(p)=\ell_{\tau_{1}}(p)\quad\text{ for any }p\in(I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z}).

Now we notice that due to the linearity of
\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}
one has in (
287
) that

(291)

A=B=0,

while from (

) and (
291
) one has

(292)

C=Q_{q,r}(p):=\frac{1}{2^{3m}}(p-r)(q-p)(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(q)+\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(r))=:F_{3}^{2}\Big(\frac{p}{2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}\Big),

where, as before – recall (

), one has

(293)

F_{3}^{2}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}a_{k}(x+\alpha_{k})^{2}\>,

with

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{r}{2^{\frac{3m}{2}}},\quad

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=-(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(q)+\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(r)),

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{q}{2^{\frac{3m}{2}}},\quad

\displaystyle a_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(q)+\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(r)}{4},

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{q-2r}{2^{\frac{3m}{2}}},\quad

\displaystyle a_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(q)+\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(r)}{4}.

At this point we rewrite (

) as

(294)

\mathcal{J}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s)=\sum_{q,r,v\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}\sum_{p\sim_{q,r,v}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}e^{i2^{\frac{3m}{2}}sQ_{q,r}(p)},

and follow the analogue of (
254
), that is

(295)

|\mathcal{J}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s)|\leq\mathcal{J}_{L}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s)+\mathcal{J}_{H}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s),

where here, as expected

(296)

\mathcal{J}_{L}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s):=\sum_{q,r,v\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\sim_{q,r,v}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\\
|Q^{\prime}_{q,r}(p)|<\bar{\eta}\end{subarray}}e^{i2^{\frac{3m}{2}}sQ_{q,r}(p)}\bigg|,

and

(297)

\mathcal{J}_{H}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s):=\sum_{q,r,v\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\sim_{q,r,v}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\\
|Q^{\prime}_{q,r}(p)|\geq\bar{\eta}\end{subarray}}e^{i2^{\frac{3m}{2}}sQ_{q,r}(p)}\bigg|.

For the treatment of the low oscillatory component
\mathcal{J}_{L}^{\mathcal{L},1}
we are precisely in the setting of Lemma
5.9
, Case 1, with
\eta=2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\bar{\eta}
and
122
122

122

The parameters
A
and

are now adapted to the new context, that is, they are defined relative to
Q_{q,r}
.

|A|+|B|\sim|\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(q)+\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1}(r)|\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}
.

Thus, we deduce that

(298)

\mathcal{J}_{L}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s)\lesssim\sum_{q,r,v\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\min\{1,L_{\eta}^{2}(q,r)\}\lesssim 2^{6m}\bar{\eta}.

For the high oscillatory component

\mathcal{J}_{H}^{\mathcal{L},1}(s)\lesssim 2^{\frac{9m}{2}}(\bar{\eta}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}|s|)^{-1}\lesssim 2^{\frac{9m}{2}}2^{3\nu_{\mathcal{L}}m}(\bar{\eta})^{-1}.

Putting now together (

), (

) and (

Conclude now that

(301)

\mathcal{I}^{>}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L},1})\lesssim 2^{\frac{3m}{8}(4\mu+\nu_{\mathcal{L}}-\frac{1}{2})}.

Treatment of the term
\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}})(0)

We start by recalling (

) and notice that, due to (

) (for

\forall\tau\in\mathcal{T},\qquad\frac{\#\{p\in I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z}:|\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(p)-\ell_{\tau}(p)|\leq 2^{\epsilon m-10}\}}{\#(I_{Q}\cap\mathbb{Z})}\leq 2^{-\epsilon m}.

Now, with

and

\mathcal{J}_{H}^{\mathcal{N}}(s):=\sum_{p,q,r\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\sim_{p,q,r}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\\

For the low oscillatory component

we apply Lemma
5.10
, Case 1, for

.

Thus, using (

), we deduce that

(306)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{L}^{\mathcal{N}}(s)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{p,q,r\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\min\{1,L_{\eta}^{2}(p,q,r)\}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(6-2\epsilon)m}+2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q,r\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\\
|q-r|\gtrsim 2^{(\frac{3}{2}-2\epsilon)m}\end{subarray}}\sum_{p\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}}\min\bigg\{1,

\displaystyle\frac{2^{3m}\bar{\eta}}{|r-q|\Big|\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(p)-\Big(p\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(r)-\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(q)}{r-q}+\frac{r\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(q)-q\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}}(r)}{r-q}\Big)\Big|}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(6-\epsilon)m}+2^{3m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q,r\sim 2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\\
|q-r|\gtrsim 2^{(\frac{3}{2}-2\epsilon)m}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{3m}\bar{\eta}}{|r-q|2^{\epsilon m}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{(6-\epsilon)m}(1+m2^{\frac{3m}{2}}\bar{\eta}).

The final estimates

\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{L}})\lesssim 2^{2\epsilon m}2^{-\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{2}-4\mu)m}.

\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{N}})\lesssim m^{\frac{1}{8}}2^{-\frac{\epsilon-12\mu}{9}m}.

where in the last inequality we choose

.

Finally, deduce that, up to a polynomial term in

, (

) holds when

for

\delta_{1}=\frac{1}{6}(\frac{1}{114}-\frac{4}{3}\mu).

6.
The high resolution, single-scale analysis (II): Time-frequency localization via weight mass and input size distribution

As already noted before – recall the discussion in Section
5.1
with a special stress on the formulation (
184
) – Proposition
5.1
captures the cancellation hidden in the non-zero curvature of the
BC^{a}
kernel’s phase thus providing the single scale
m
-exponential decay of
\|\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}\|_{L^{2}\times L^{2}\times L^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}}
for every given
P=P(k,\ell,r)\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
. The difficulty now lies in putting the various scales together while preserving a similar type decay in
m
, thus aiming for a
global
estimate of the form

(317)

\|\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m}\|_{L^{p}\times L^{q}\times L^{r}\to{\mathbb{C}}}\lesssim 2^{-\delta(p,q,r)\,am}\,,

where, of course, this time
(p,q,r)
needs to obey the natural Hölder homogeneity condition and
\delta(p,q,r)>0
. As hinted by the modulation invariance property of
BC^{a}
in
(P2)
, it is natural to expect that time-frequency analysis methods inspired by the bilinear Hilbert transform approach in
[
64
]
would be required for achieving (
317
). These type of methods are designed for capturing the almost-orthogonality among suitable families of
modulated Calderón-Zygmund (sub)operators
125
125

125

Represented as
trees
in the time-frequency plane, these objects constitute the elementary building blocks within any modulation invariant analysis problem.
as opposed to the standard Calderón-Zygmund theory that exhibits a simpler, almost orthogonality relation among scales.

In the context given by our problem we need to combine the almost orthogonality methods employed in Section
5
and relying on a suitable bilinear
TT^{*}
argument involving exponential sums with the almost orthogonality methods developed for controlling tree-like structures that are part of the modulation invariance time-frequency analysis and that will be the focus of Section
7
. However, in order to be able to perform this latter analysis we will need a very precise (Heisenberg) time-frequency localization for all the three functions
f,g
and
h
that are involved within a generic trilinear form
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}(f,g,h)
,
P\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
. This latter fact will be required when quantifying the interactions among the tree structures associated with each of the input functions.

It is this last aspect that becomes the central theme of our present section: in what follows we will focus on the refinement of the single scale estimate proved in Section
5
thus addressing the item (a) – see also (II) – in Section
5.1
.

6.1.
Intuition – a heuristic

In this section we briefly review with few extra accents the outline provided in Section
2.7
. We first remind the concrete setting for our problem:

Fix
P=P(k,\ell,r)\in{{\mathbb{P}}_{\textrm{BHT}}}
; then, from (
179
) we have that

(318)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,P}(f,g,h)=\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,p\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\sum_{v\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)h(x)\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)dx,

where
S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}
is given by

(319)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\frac{1}{2^{am+2k\over 4}}\langle f_{\ell+1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n}\rangle\langle g_{\ell-1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}\rangle\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,2\ell-1}^{2u,p_{r}}(x).

The challenge is to find a consensus between the following two competing factors:

•

(i)
the extraction of accurate time-frequency information for the triple input function;

•

(ii)
the exploitation of the non-zero curvature in the oscillatory phase, now subsumed in the expression of
w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)
– see (
318
) above.

Each of the above items comes with its own set of difficulties:

•

for (i)
: while the time-frequency localization for
f
and
g
is readily available via the wave-packet coefficients resulted from their Gabor decomposition – see (
156
) and (
184
), no similar approach is available for
h

per se
due to the implicit presence of the stopping time function
\lambda(x)
via the “oscillatory weight”
w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)
;

•

for (ii)
: in order to preserve the decay provided by the single scale estimate in Proposition
5.1
the oscillatory weight
w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)
has to be paired with the output of the bilinear coefficient
\tilde{S}_{k}^{n,v}
as revealed by Corollary
5.6
.

The solution to
(i)
coincides with “the path of least resistance” following the most natural resolution: to simply switch the focus from the time-frequency localization of
h
to the time-frequency localization of the compound expression
h\,\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda)\,w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)
.

The solution to
(ii)
relies on the remark provided in (
97
) and also displayed for reader’s convenience below:

Claim:
[
informal
]
With the previous notations, set

(320)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle:=2^{-k}\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\langle f_{\ell+1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n}\rangle\langle g_{\ell-1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}\rangle\,e^{i(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})2u}.

Then, the expression
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)
is morally constant on any spatial interval of length
\lesssim 2^{-(am-k)}
.

The heuristic motivation of this is based on the following observation: inspecting (
320
) we notice that
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)
is a trigonometric polynomial that can be expressed as a linear combination of elements belonging to the family of complex exponentials
\{e^{i(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})2u}\}_{u\sim 2^{\frac{am}{2}}}
. Thus, the frequency support of such a trigonometric polynomial lies within an interval of the form
[c_{1}\,2^{am-k},\,c_{2}\,2^{am-k}]
for some suitable absolute constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)\approx

\displaystyle\sum_{n,v\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\,\sum_{p^{\prime}\sim 2^{am}}

\displaystyle 2^{-\frac{am-k}{2}}\langle h\,\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda)w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda),\check{\varphi}_{am-k,2\ell-1}^{0,2^{am}(r-1)+p^{\prime}}\rangle

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,\big\lfloor\frac{p^{\prime}}{2^{am\over 2}}\big\rfloor}(f_{\ell+1},g_{\ell-1})(x)w_{k,n,v}(x)dx}{\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}w_{k,n,v}(x)dx}

Assume for the sake of the argument in what follows that the masses of

For now, we may pretend that

6.2.
Sharp time-frequency localization for the input
h
: a rigorous approach to (

).

In this section we provide a detailed justification for the heuristic presented in the previous section.

We first notice based on (

) and (

) that

S_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)=\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)\,e^{i2^{\frac{am}{2}}(2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}x-p_{r})(2\ell-2)}\,\check{\varphi}(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})\>.

With this (
318
) becomes

(328)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)=\sum_{n,v,p\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\int\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)\,\check{\varphi}(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})

\displaystyle\cdot e^{i2^{am\over 2}(2\ell-2)(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})}\,\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))\,w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)h(x)\,dx.

Notice now that
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)
is a trigonometric polynomial of period
2^{k-{am\over 2}}
.

Defining

H_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(x):=\check{\varphi}(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})\,e^{i2^{am\over 2}(2\ell-2)(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})}\,\rho_{am-ak}(\lambda(x))\,w_{k,n,v}^{e}(\lambda)(x)h(x)\,,

we use the same periodization argument as in Section
4.3
in order to deduce that

(329)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)\,H_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{I_{k}^{p_{r}}}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)\,\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(x+j2^{k-{am\over 2}})\Big)\,dx\,.

The main reason for the formulation of (
329
) is to make transparent and later separate the contribution of the dominant and error terms. Indeed, since
H_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}
is morally localized around
I_{k}^{p_{r}}
, we immediately see that

•

the main term corresponds to the
j=0
case;

•

the error term represents the sum over
|j|>0
and accounts for the tail behavior of
\check{\varphi}
in the expression that defines
H_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}
.

Take now a smooth, compactly supported partition of unity adapted to (
321
)–(
322
) such that

128

128

In this section we say that
p^{\prime}\sim_{\lfloor\rfloor_{p}}2^{am}
iff
p^{\prime}\sim 2^{am}
and
\lfloor p^{\prime}2^{-\frac{am}{2}}\rfloor=p
.

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{p^{\prime}\sim_{\lfloor\rfloor_{p}}2^{am}}\check{\psi}(2^{am-k}(x-\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}))\,a(x)\,dx=\int_{I_{k}^{p_{r}}}a(x)\,dx

Let

From (

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{m,k,\ell,r}(f,g,h)=\sum_{n,v,p\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\sum_{p^{\prime}\sim_{\lfloor\rfloor_{p}}2^{am}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\int\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle\cdot\check{\psi}(2^{am-k}(x-\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}))\,e^{i2^{\frac{am}{2}}(2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}x-p_{r})(2\ell-2)}\,\underline{H}_{k,r}^{n,v,p}\Big(x+\frac{j}{2^{{am\over 2}-k}}\Big)\,dx.

Let now

adapted to scale

and so has no compact support in frequency, but is adapted to the frequency support of
\varphi_{am-k,2\ell-1}^{2^{am}(r-1)+p^{\prime}}
.

Based on the informal claim made in Section

we further claim that, essentially,

129

129

Strictly speaking, the left-hand side in (

the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\int\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)e^{i2^{\frac{am}{2}}(2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}x-p_{r})(2\ell-2)}

\displaystyle 2^{-\frac{am-k}{2}}\Big\langle\underline{H}_{k,r}^{n,v,p}\Big(\cdot+\frac{j}{2^{{am\over 2}-k}}\Big),\tilde{\varphi}_{am-k,2\ell-1}^{2^{am}(r-1)+p^{\prime}}\Big\rangle\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)w(x)\,dx}{\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}w(x)\,dx},

This claim is a consequence of the following lemma whose easy proof – that is omitted – relies on Taylor’s formula applied to the real analytic function
e^{iax}
,
a\in\mathbb{R}
restricted to a compact interval.

Lemma 6.1
.

Consider the interval
\displaystyle I=\Big[{s\over 2^{am-k}},{s+1\over 2^{am-k}}\Big]
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
t_{s}:={s\over 2^{am-k}}
be its left endpoint. Let
\check{\psi}
be a compactly supported bump function adapted to
[0,1]
. Then for any sequence of complex numbers
\{c_{u}\}_{u\sim 2^{am/2}}
and any locally integrable function
w_{1}
,
w_{2}
with
\int_{I}w_{2}(x)\,dx\not=0
we have

\displaystyle\int\Big(\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}c_{u}\check{\psi}(2^{am-k}(x-t_{s}))\,e^{i2^{{am\over 2}-k}2u(x-t_{s})}\Big)w_{1}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\geq 0}\frac{1}{\nu!}\Big(\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\Big({2u\over{2^{am\over 2}}}\Big)^{\nu}c_{u}\Big)\int(i2^{am-k}(x-t_{s}))^{\nu}\check{\psi}(2^{am-k}(x-t_{s}))w_{1}(x)dx,

and

\displaystyle\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}c_{u}=\sum_{\nu\geq 0}\frac{1}{\nu!}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{I}\Big(\sum\limits_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\Big({2u\over{2^{am\over 2}}}\Big)^{\nu}c_{u}e^{i2^{{am\over 2}-k}2u(x-t_{s})}\Big)(-i2^{am-k}(x-t_{s}))^{\nu}w_{2}(x)\,dx}{\int_{I}w_{2}(x)\,dx}.

In what follows, based on the above, we show how to make sense of (
333
).

Proof of (
333
).

To start with, we observe that
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)
is given by

2^{-k}\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}e^{i(2^{{am\over 2}-k}\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}-p_{r})2u}\langle f_{\ell+1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n}\rangle\langle g_{\ell-1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}\rangle\,e^{i2^{{am\over 2}-k}(x-\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}})2u},

so that we can apply the first part of Lemma
6.1
to the left-hand side of (
333
)
for
I=\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}
and
w_{1}(x):=e^{i2^{\frac{am}{2}}(2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}x-p_{r})(2\ell-2)}\underline{H}_{k,r}^{n,v,p}(x+\frac{j}{2^{{am\over 2}-k}})
in order to get

(334)

\displaystyle\int\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}(f,g)(x)e^{i2^{\frac{am}{2}}(2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}x-p_{r})(2\ell-2)}

\displaystyle\cdot\check{\psi}(2^{am-k}(x-\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}))\underline{H}_{k,r}^{n,v,p}\Big(x+\frac{j}{2^{{am\over 2}-k}}\Big)\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\geq 0}\frac{1}{\nu!}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu}(f,g)(\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}})\int\check{\psi}_{\nu}(2^{am-k}(x-\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle\cdot e^{i2^{\frac{am}{2}}(2^{\frac{am}{2}-k}x-p_{r})(2\ell-2)}\,\underline{H}_{k,r}^{n,v,p}\Big(x+\frac{j}{2^{{am\over 2}-k}}\Big)\,dx,

where
\psi_{\nu}
is a slight modification
130
130

130

Indeed it is simply
\check{\psi}_{\nu}(x)=(ix)^{\nu}\check{\psi}(x)
.
of
\psi
with the same support properties and
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu}
is the minor modification of
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p}
given by

\displaystyle\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu}(f,g)(x)

\displaystyle:=2^{-k}\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\Big(\frac{2u}{2^{\frac{am}{2}}}\Big)^{\nu}\langle f_{\ell+1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n}\rangle\langle g_{\ell-1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}\rangle\,e^{i(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})2u}.

Next, we use the second part of Lemma
6.1
for
\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}c_{u}=\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu}(f,g)(\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}})
,
I=\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}
and
w_{2}=w
in order to deduce

\displaystyle\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu}(f,g)(\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}})=\sum\limits_{\nu^{\prime}\geq 0}\frac{1}{\nu^{\prime}!}\;\frac{\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(f,g)(x)w(x)dx}{\int_{\underline{I}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}}w(x)dx},

where
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
is a further slight modification of
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu}
given by
131
131

131

Notice that
\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu,0}=\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu}
.

(335)

\displaystyle\underline{S}_{k,\ell,r}^{n,v,p,\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(f,g)(x):=\frac{1}{2^{k}}\sum_{u\sim 2^{am\over 2}}\Big(\frac{2u}{2^{am\over 2}}\Big)^{\nu+\nu^{\prime}}\langle f_{\ell+1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u-v,p_{r}-n}\rangle

\displaystyle\cdot\langle g_{\ell-1},\check{\varphi}_{{am\over 2}-k,\ell}^{u+v,p_{r}+n}\rangle(-i2^{am-k}(x-\underline{t}_{k,r}^{p^{\prime}}))^{\nu^{\prime}}e^{i(2^{{am\over 2}-k}x-p_{r})2u}\,.

Inserting the above in (

) we obtain a representation of the left-hand side of (

) as an

-summable superposition of terms having a similar shape as the right-hand side term in (

). Notice that if we only keep the main term in the above superposition – corresponding to
